On Spectral Inclusion Sets and Computing the Spectra and Pseudospectra of Bounded Linear Operators
Simon N. Chandler-Wilde, Ratchanikorn Chonchaiya, Marko Lindner

Abstract




Abstract.

In this paper we derive novel families of inclusion sets for the spectrum and pseudospectrum of large classes of bounded linear operators, and establish convergence of particular sequences of these inclusion sets to the spectrum or pseudospectrum, as appropriate. Our results apply, in particular, to bounded linear operators on a separable Hilbert space that, with respect to some orthonormal basis, have a representation as a bi-infinite matrix that is banded or band-dominated. More generally, our results apply in cases where the matrix entries themselves are bounded linear operators on some Banach space. In the scalar matrix entry case we show that our methods, given the input information we assume, lead to a sequence of approximations to the spectrum, each element of which can be computed in finitely many arithmetic operations, so that, with our assumed inputs, the problem of determining the spectrum of a band-dominated operator has solvability complexity index one, in the sense of Ben-Artzi et al. (
C. R. Acad. Sci. Paris, Ser. I

353
(2015), 931–936). As a concrete and substantial application, we apply our methods to the determination of the spectra of non-self-adjoint bi-infinite tridiagonal matrices that are pseudoergodic in the sense of Davies (
Commun. Math. Phys.

216
(2001) 687–704).

Mathematics subject classification (2010):
Primary 47A10; Secondary 47B36, 46E40, 47B80.

Keywords:
band matrix, band-dominated matrix, solvability complexity index, pseudoergodic

1
Introduction and overview

The determination of the spectra of bounded linear operators is a fundamental problem in functional analysis with applications across science and engineering (e.g.,
[
83
,
30
,
53
,
20
,
21
]
). In this paper
1
1

1

This paper, which has been a long time in preparation, is based in large part on Chapters 3 and 4 of the 2010 PhD thesis of the second author
[
18
]
, carried out under the supervision of the first and third authors. The title of earlier drafts of this paper was “Upper bounds on the spectra
and pseudospectra of Jacobi and related operators”. With this title this paper is referenced from other work, that derives in part from the results and ideas in this paper, by one or more of the three of us with collaborators Hagger, Seidel, and Schmidt
[
12
,
65
,
51
,
64
]
. An announcement of some of the results of this paper has appeared in the conference proceedings
[
13
]
.
we establish novel families of inclusion sets for the spectrum and pseudospectrum of large classes of bounded linear operators, and establish convergence of particular sequences of these inclusion sets to the spectrum or pseudospectrum, as appropriate. Our results apply, in particular, to bounded linear operators
A
on a separable Hilbert space
Y
that, with respect to some orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
for
Y
, have a matrix representation
[A_{ij}]_{i,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
, where
A_{ij}:=(Ae_{j},e_{i})
with
(\cdot,\cdot)
the inner product on
Y
, that is banded or band-dominated (as defined in §
1.3
below). More generally, our results apply in the case that
Y
is the Banach space
Y=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
, where
X
is a Banach space, and
A:Y\to Y
is a bounded linear operator that has the banded or band-dominated matrix representation
[A_{ij}]_{i,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
, where each
A_{ij}\in L(X)
, the space of bounded linear operators on
X
.

1.1
The main ideas, their provenance and significance

Let us sketch the key ideas of the paper, deferring a more detailed account of the main results to §
1.3
.
To explain these ideas, focussing first on the case that
A=[A_{ij}]_{i,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
is a bi-infinite tridiagonal matrix, let
A(i{:}j,k{:}\ell)
denote the
(j{+}1{-}i)\times(\ell{+}1{-}k)
submatrix (or finite section) of
A
consisting of the elements in rows
i
through
j
and columns
k
through
\ell
. Abbreviate the
n\times n
principal submatrix
A(k{+}1{:}k{+}n,k{+}1{:}k{+}n)
as
A_{n,k}
, and the
(n{+}2)\times n
submatrix
A(k{:}k{+}n{+1},k{+}1{:}k{+}n)
as
A^{+}_{n,k}
. Further, let
A_{n}:=A_{2n+1,-n-1}
and
A^{+}_{n}:=A^{+}_{2n+1,-n-1}
, so that
A^{+}_{n}
contains all the non-zero elements in columns
-n
through
n
. Similarly, for
x=(x_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
, let
x_{n,k}
denote the (column) vector of length
n
consisting of entries
k+1
through
k+n
of
x
.

Suppose that we wish to compute an approximation to
{\rm Spec}\,A
, the spectrum of a bi-infinite tridiagonal matrix
A

acting as a bounded linear operator on
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
. It is well known that if
\lambda\in{\rm Spec}\,A
then, for every
{\varepsilon}>0
, there exists
x\in\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
such that
x
is an
{\varepsilon}
-pseudoeigenvector of
A
for
\lambda
, meaning that
x\neq 0
and
\|(A-\lambda I)x\|\leq{\varepsilon}\|x\|
, or there exists an
{\varepsilon}
-pseudoeigenvector of
A^{*}
for
\lambda
, where
A^{*}
is the matrix that is the transpose of
A
. Our first key observation is the following:

If
x
is an
{\varepsilon}
-pseudoeigenvector of
A
for
\lambda
then, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there is a computable
{\varepsilon}_{n}>0
such that
x_{n,k}
is an
({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n})
-pseudoeigenvector of
A_{n,k}
for
\lambda
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

This observation implies that, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}A_{n,k}
, the (closed)
{\varepsilon}
-pseudospectrum of the matrix
A_{n,k}
(see §
1.3
for definitions). Indeed, this inclusion holds for all
{\varepsilon}>0
, which we will see implies that

{\rm Spec}\,A\ \subset\ \Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(A)\ :=\ \overline{\bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}A_{n,k}},

(1.1)

for some positive null sequence
({\varepsilon}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.
Optimising the above idea (see §
1.3.1
) leads, concretely, to the conclusion that (
1.1
) holds with
{\varepsilon}_{n}=r(A)\eta_{n}
, where

r(A)\ :=\ \sup_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}|A_{i,i+1}|+\sup_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}|A_{i,i-1}|\quad\mbox{and}\quad 2\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{4n+2}\right)\ \leq\ \eta_{n}\ \leq\ 2\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2n+4}\right).

(1.2)

In terms of provenance, the above observation and (
1.1
) are reminiscent of the classical Gershgorin theorem
[
42
,
84
]
that provides an enclosure for the eigenvalues of an arbitrary
n\times n
matrix. Indeed, in the simplest case
n=1
,
A_{n,k}=A_{k{+}1,k{+}1}
is the
(k{+}1)
th diagonal entry of the matrix
A
, the above inequality implies that
\eta_{n}=1
, and
{\rm Spec}\,_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}A_{n,k}
is just
A_{k{+}1,k{+}1}+r(A)\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
, the closed disc of radius
r(A)
centred on
A_{k{+}1,k{+}1}
. Thus (
1.1
) in the case
n=1
is simply

{\rm Spec}\,A\ \subset\ \overline{\bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(A_{kk}+r(A){\mathbb{D}})},

(1.3)

an enclosure of
{\rm Spec}\,A
by discs centred on the diagonal entries of
A
that is an immediate consequence of (an infinite matrix version) of the classical Gershgorin theorem
2
2

2

An infinite matrix version of the Gershgorin theorem that implies (
1.3
) is proved as
[
18
, Theorem 2.50]
. Alternatively, the inclusion (
1.3
) follows by applying (
2.3
) below, with
B
the diagonal of
A
, noting that
\|A-B\|\leq r(A)
and
{\rm Spec}_{r(A)}B={\rm Spec}\,B+r(A)\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
.
. So our enclosures (
1.1
) can be seen as an extension of Gershgorin’s theorem to create a whole family of inclusion sets for the spectra of bi-infinite tridiagonal matrices.

An attraction of a sequence
(\Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(A))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of inclusion sets is that one can think of taking the limit. We will see significant examples below, including well-studied examples of tridiagonal pseudoergodic matrices in the sense of Davies
[
28
]
, for which
\Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
(
is the Hausdorff convergence that we recall in §
1.3
). But, in general, the sequence
(\Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(A))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, based on computing spectral quantities of square matrix finite sections of
A
, suffers from
spectral pollution

[
31
]
: there are limit points of the sequence
(\Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(A))
that are not in
{\rm Spec}\,A
.

The second key idea, dating back to Davies & Plum
[
31
, pp. 423-434]
(building on Davies
[
26
]
) for the self-adjoint case, and Hansen
[
52
, Theorem 48]
(proved in
[
53
]
) and
[
53
]
for the general case (see also
[
58
,
2
,
22
]
), is to avoid spectral pollution by working with rectangular rather than square finite sections. In our context this means to replace the
n\times n
matrix
A_{n,k}
by the
(n+2)\times n
matrix
A_{n,k}^{+}
, containing all the non-zero entries of
A
in columns
k+1
to
k+n
. It is a standard characterisation of the pseudospectrum (see (
1.9
) and (
7.2
), or
[
83
, §I.2]
) that

{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}A_{n,k}\ =\ \left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\min\left(s_{\min}((A-\lambda I)_{n,k}),s_{\min}((A^{*}-\lambda I)_{n,k}\right)\leq{\varepsilon}_{n}\right\},

where
s_{\min}
denotes the smallest singular value. This implies (with a little work, see Proposition
3.1
) that

\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\ =\ \left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\min\left(s_{\min}((A-\lambda I)_{n,k}),s_{\min}((A^{*}-\lambda I)_{n,k}\right)\leq{\varepsilon}_{n}\right\}.

Replacing square with rectangular finite sections in the above expression gives an alternative sequence of approximations to
{\rm Spec}\,A
, viz.
(\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(A))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, where

\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)\ :=\ \left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\min\left(s_{\min}((A-\lambda I)^{+}_{n,k}),s_{\min}((A^{*}-\lambda I)^{+}_{n,k}\right)\leq{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}\right\}.

(1.4)

Again
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)
for each
n
(equation (
M

τ
1

)), provided
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
is large enough. (We show in §
5
that if
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}:=r(A)\eta^{\prime\prime}_{n}
then
\eta^{\prime\prime}_{n}=2\sin(\pi/(2n+2))
is the smallest choice of
\eta^{\prime\prime}_{n}
that maintains this inclusion for all tridiagonal
A
.) Crucially, and this is the benefit of replacing square by rectangular finite sections, also
\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
(Theorem
1.2
), for every tridiagonal
A
.

The third idea is to realise that the entries in the tridiagonal matrix can themselves be matrices (or indeed operators on some Banach space). This extends the above results to general banded matrices (see §
1.3
below). Further, by perturbation arguments, we can obtain inclusion sets, and a sequence of approximations converging to the spectrum, also for the band-dominated case (§
1.3.3
).

The final main idea of the paper is that, through a further perturbation argument (see §
1.3.4
), the union and infimum over all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, in (
1.1
) and (
1.4
), can be replaced by a union and infimum over
k\in K_{n}
, where
K_{n}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
is a finite,
A
- and
n
-dependent subset, so that a sequence of approximations to
{\rm Spec}\,A
is obtained that can be computed in a finite number of arithmetic operations. In the language of the solvability complexity index (SCI) of
[
1
,
2
,
21
,
22
,
23
]
our results (see §
1.3.5
and §
7
) lead to a proof that the computational problem of determining the spectrum of a band-dominated operator has SCI equal to one, if one chooses an evaluation set (in the sense of
[
1
,
2
,
21
,
22
]
) that provides access to appropriately chosen submatrices, i.e. to
A^{+}_{n,k}
and
(A^{*})^{+}_{n,k}
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
k\in K_{n}
. To showcase this result we provide a concrete realisation of the associated algorithm for the class of tridiagonal matrices that are pseudoergodic in the sense of Davies
[
28
]
(see §
8.4
), illustrating this algorithm by numerical results taken from
[
12
]
.

To clarify the significance of this combination of ideas, let us make comparison to the classical idea of approximating the spectrum or pseudospectrum of
A
by those of a single large finite section of
A
. To avoid spectral pollution we take a rectangular finite section: precisely (cf. (
1.4
)), consider the approximation

\mathcal{S}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)\ :=\ \left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\min\left(s_{\min}((A-\lambda I)^{+}_{n}),s_{\min}((A^{*}-\lambda I)^{+}_{n}\right)\leq{\varepsilon}\right\},

(1.5)

defined in terms of smallest singular values of
2n{+}3\times 2n{+}1
finite sections of
A
and
A^{*}
, which is a particular instance of the approximation sequence proposed by Hansen
[
52
]
. Then it follows from
[
52
, Theorem 48]
(the proof deferred to
[
53
]
) that, for every
{\varepsilon}>0
,

\mathcal{S}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
. Since also
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, we have that

{\rm Spec}\,A=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\lim_{n\to\infty}\,\mathcal{S}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A).

This result trivially implies that there exists a positive null sequence
(\epsilon_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that
\mathcal{S}^{n}_{\epsilon_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,

{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
. But, in contrast to the new approximation families that we introduce in this paper, this null sequence
(\epsilon_{n})
depends on
A
, in an ill-defined, and unquantifiable way. Indeed, the SCI result arguments of
[
1
,
2
]
, that we discuss in §
1.3.5
and §
1.5
, imply that there exists no sequence of universal algorithms, indexed by
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, operating on the set of all tridiagonal matrices, such that the
n
th algorithm takes just finitely many values of the matrix entries
A_{ij}
as input and gives
{\varepsilon}_{n}
as output, in such a way that
\mathcal{S}^{n}_{\epsilon_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
for all tridiagonal
A

[
23
]
. In the language of
[
1
,
2
]
, the SCI of computing the spectrum of a tridiagonal operator is two
[
23
]
if the evaluation set is restricted to the mappings
A\mapsto A_{ij}
, for
i,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

1.2
The structure of the paper

In the next subsection, §
1.3
, we give an overview of the main results of the paper. We state our results on inclusion sets for the spectrum and pseudospectrum in the case that the matrix representation of
A
is banded in §
1.3.1
, prove our results on convergence of our inclusion set sequences to the spectrum and pseudospectrum for the banded case in §
1.3.2
, detail the extension to the band-dominated case in §
1.3.3
, and summarise our results on computability and the solvability complexity index in §
1.3.4
and §
1.3.5
. In §
1.4
we give a first example (the shift operator) that serves to illustrate our inclusions (
M

τ

), (
M

π

) and (
M

τ
1

) and demonstrate their sharpness, and in §
1.5
we survey related work, expanding on the discussion above.

In §
2
we detail further properties of pseudospectra needed for our later arguments and introduce the so-called Globevnik property. Sections
3
–
5
prove the inclusions (
M

τ

), (
M

π

), and (
M

τ
1

), respectively. In §
6
we prove Theorem
1.9
that we state in §
1.3.4
, that justifies replacement of the infinite set of sub-matrices that are part of the definitions of our inclusion sets with a finite subset. This result is extended to the general band-dominated case in Theorem
6.1
. In §
7
we give details of implementation, leading to our solvability complexity index results. We discuss concrete applications of our general results in §
8
, notably to the case where
A
is tridiagonal and pseudoergodic in the sense of Davies
[
28
]
. In §
9
we indicate open problems and directions for further work.

1.3
Key notations and our main results

As usual, we write
{\mathbb{Z}},\,{\mathbb{R}},\,{\mathbb{C}}
for the integer, real, and complex numbers,
{\mathbb{N}}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
is the set of positive integers,

{\mathbb{D}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
the open unit disk,
{\mathbb{T}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
the unit circle, and, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\mathbb{T}}_{n}\subset{\mathbb{T}}
denotes the set of

n
th roots of unity,
{\mathbb{T}}_{n}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:z^{n}=1\}
.
\overline{S}
denotes the closure of a set
S\subset{\mathbb{C}}
and
\bar{z}
denotes the complex conjugate of
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Throughout,
X
will denote some Banach space: at particular points in the text we will make clear where we are specialising to the case that
X
is a Hilbert space or is finite-dimensional. We will use, throughout, the letter
E
as an abbreviation for the standard
X
-valued sequence space
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
, defined at (
1.12
) below. We will abbreviate
\ell^{2}(X,{\mathbb{C}})
as
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
.

The spectrum, pseudospectrum, and lower norm.
Let
Y
be a Banach space (throughout the paper our Banach and Hilbert spaces are assumed complex). Given
A\in L(Y)
, the space of bounded linear operators on
Y
, we denote the spectrum of
A
by
{\rm Spec}\,A
. With the convention that, for
B\in L(Y)
,
\|B^{-1}\|:=\infty
if
B
is not invertible, and that
1/\infty:=0
, and where
I
is the identity operator on
Y
, we define the
closed
{\varepsilon}
-pseudospectrum
,

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, by

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A:=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:1/\|(A-\lambda I)^{-1}\|\leq{\varepsilon}\},\quad{\varepsilon}>0.

(1.6)

Clearly
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\subset{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}A
, for
0<{\varepsilon}<{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
, and

{\rm Spec}\,A=\bigcap_{{\varepsilon}>0}{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A.

(1.7)

To write certain inclusion statements more compactly, we will define also
{\rm Spec}_{0}A:={\rm Spec}\,A
. For
{\varepsilon}>0
we define also the
open
{\varepsilon}
-pseudospectrum
3
3

3

We refer readers unfamiliar with the pseudospectrum, its properties and applications, to §
2
below, and to Böttcher & Lindner
[
7
]
, Davies
[
30
]
, Trefethen & Embree
[
83
]
, and Hagen, Roch, Silbermann
[
48
]
. We note that the
{\varepsilon}
-pseudospectrum, as defined in
[
83
,
30
]
and most recent literature, is our open
{\varepsilon}
-pseudospectrum, but in part of the literature, including
[
48
]
,
{\varepsilon}
-pseudospectrum means our closed
{\varepsilon}
-pseudospectrum. As we recall in §
2
, if the Banach space
X
has the so-called
Globevnik property
, which holds in particular if
X
is a Hilbert space or is finite-dimensional, these two pseudospectra are related simply by
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}(B)=\overline{{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}(B)}
, for
{\varepsilon}>0
and
B\in L(X)
.
,

{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, given by (
1.6
) with the
\leq
replaced by
<
; the above inclusions and (
1.7
) hold also with
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
replaced by
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
. As a consequence of standard perturbation arguments (e.g.,
[
30
, Theorem 1.2.9]
),
{\rm Spec}\,_{\varepsilon}A
is closed, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
, and
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
open, for
{\varepsilon}>0
.

Given Banach spaces
X
and
Y
and
B\in L(X,Y)
, the space of bounded linear operators from
X
to
Y
, one can study, as a counterpart to the operator norm
\|B\|:=\sup_{\|x\|=1}\|Bx\|
, the quantity

\nu(B)\ :=\ \inf_{\|x\|=1}\|Bx\|

that is sometimes (by abuse of notation) called the
lower norm
of
B
. In the case that
Y
is a Hilbert space and
A\in L(Y)
,
\|A\|
is the largest singular value
of
A
and
\nu(A)
is the smallest
4
4

4

Recall that the singular values of
A\in L(Y)
, defined whenever
Y
is a Hilbert space, are the points in the spectrum of
(A^{\prime}A)^{1/2}
, where
A^{\prime}
is the Hilbert space adjoint.
. For every Banach space
Y
and
A\in L(Y)
the equality

1/\|A^{-1}\|\ =\min(\nu(A),\nu(A^{*}))=:\mu(A)

(1.8)

holds, where
A^{*}
is the Banach space adjoint (see below). In particular,
A
is invertible if and only if
\nu(A)
and
\nu(A^{*})
are both nonzero, i.e., if and only if
\mu(A)\neq 0
, in which case

\nu(A)=\nu(A^{*})=\mu(A)
. Further, if
A
is Fredholm of index zero, in particular if
Y
is finite-dimensional, then
\nu(A)=0
if and only if
\nu(A^{*})=0
, so that
\mu(A)=\nu(A)
.

From the definitions of
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
and
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
and (
1.8
) it follows that

{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ =\ \{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu(A-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}\},\;\;{\varepsilon}>0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ =\ \{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu(A-\lambda I)\leq{\varepsilon}\},\;\;{\varepsilon}\geq 0.

(1.9)

Simple but important properties of the lower norm are that, if
A,B\in L(X,Y)
and
Z
is a closed subspace of
X
, then

\nu(A|_{Z})\geq\nu(A)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad|\nu(A)-\nu(B)|\leq\|A-B\|;

(1.10)

see, e.g.,
[
62
, Lemma 2.38]
for the second of these properties. It is easy to see (since
\mu(A)=\nu(A)
if
\mu(A)\neq 0
) that also

|\mu(A)-\mu(B)|\leq\|A-B\|.

(1.11)

Hausdorff distance and notions of set convergence.

Let
{\mathbb{C}}^{B}
,
{\mathbb{C}}^{C}
denote, respectively, the sets of bounded and compact non-empty subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
. For
a\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
S\in{\mathbb{C}}^{B}
, let
{\rm dist}(a,S):=\inf_{b\in S}|a-b|
. For
S_{1},S_{2}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{B}
let

d_{H}(S_{1},S_{2})\ :=\ \max\left(\sup_{s_{1}\in S_{1}}{\rm dist}(s_{1},S_{2})\,,\,\sup_{s_{2}\in S_{2}}{\rm dist}(s_{2},S_{1})\right),

which is termed the
Hausdorff distance
between
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
. It is well known (e.g.,
[
57
,
48
]
) that
d_{H}(\cdot,\cdot)
is a metric on
{\mathbb{C}}^{C}
, the so-called
Hausdorff metric
. For
S_{1},S_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{B}
it is clear that
d_{H}(S_{1},S_{2})=d_{H}(\overline{S_{1}},\overline{S_{2}})
, so that
d_{H}(S_{1},S_{2})=0
if and only if
\overline{S_{1}}=\overline{S_{2}}
, and
d_{H}(\cdot,\cdot)
is a pseudometric on
{\mathbb{C}}^{B}
.
For a sequence
(S_{n})\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{B}
and
S\in{\mathbb{C}}^{B}
we write
S_{n}\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,S
if
d_{H}(S_{n},S)\to 0
. This limit is in general not unique: if
S_{n}\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,S
and
T\in{\mathbb{C}}^{B}
then
S_{n}\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,T
if and only if
\overline{S}=\overline{T}.

Simple results that we will use extensively are that if
(S_{n})\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{C}
and
S_{1}\supset S_{2}\supset\ldots
, in which case
S:=\cap_{n=1}^{\infty}S_{n}
is non-empty, then
S_{n}\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,S
, and that if
S\in{\mathbb{C}}^{B}
,
(S_{n}),(T_{n})\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{B}
,
S\subset T_{n}\subset S_{n}
and
S_{n}\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,S
, then
T_{n}\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,S
. (These results can be seen directly, or via the equivalence of Hausdorff convergence with other notions of set convergence, see
[
57
, p. 171]
or
[
48
, Proposition 3.6]
.)

Band and band-dominated operators.
Given a Banach space
X
let

E:=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)

(1.12)

denote the space of bi-infinite

X
-valued sequences
x=(x_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
that are
square-summable, a Banach space with the norm
\|\cdot\|
defined
by
\|x\|=(\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}|x_{j}|_{X}^{2})^{1/2}
. (We will use the notation (
1.12
), without further remark, throughout the rest of the paper, and will abbreviate
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
as
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
when
X={\mathbb{C}}
.) Let
A\in L(E)
, in which case (e.g.,
[
62
, §1.3.5]
)
A
has a matrix representation
[A]=[A_{ij}]
, that we will denote again by
A
, with each
A_{ij}\in L(X)
, such that, for every
x=(x_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\in E
with finitely many non-zero entries,
Ax=y=(y_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
, where

y_{i}=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}A_{ij}x_{j},\quad i\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

(1.13)

The main body of our results are for the case where
A\in BO(E)
, where
BO(E)
denotes the linear subspace of those
A\in L(E)
whose matrix representation is
banded
with some
band-width
w
, meaning that
A_{ij}=0
for
|i-j|>w
, in which case (
1.13
) holds (and is a finite sum) for all
x\in E
. By perturbation arguments we also extend our results to the case where
A\in BDO(E)
, where
BDO(E)
, the space of
band-dominated operators
, is the closure of
BO(E)
in
L(E)
with respect to the operator norm (see, e.g.,
[
62
, §1.3.6]
).

Adjoints.
In many of our arguments we require the (Banach space) adjoint
A^{*}
of
A
which we think of as an operator on
E^{*}:=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X^{*})
, where
X^{*}
is the dual space of
X
. This has the matrix representation
[A_{ji}^{*}]
, where
A_{ji}^{*}\in L(X^{*})
is the Banach space adjoint of
A_{ji}\in L(X)
. In the case that
X={\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, equipped with the
\ell^{p}
norm, with
1\leq p\leq\infty
, we will identify
X^{*}
with
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
equipped with the
\ell^{q}
norm, where
q
is the usual conjugate index (
1/p+1/q=1
), so that
A_{ji}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
is just an
n\times n
matrix with scalar entries, and
A_{ji}^{*}
is just
A_{ji}^{T}
, the transpose of the matrix
A_{ji}
.

Reduction to the tridiagonal case.
A simple but important observation is that, to construct inclusion sets for the spectrum and pseudospectrum of
A\in BO(E)
it is enough to consider the case when the matrix representation has band-width one, so that the matrix is tridiagonal
5
5

5

In the case
X={\mathbb{C}}
, in which case
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
is a Hilbert space, and if
A
is self-adjoint, reduction of computation of
{\rm Spec}\,A
(and the pseudospectra of
A
) to consideration of the tridiagonal case is discussed in another sense
in
[
52
, Corollary 26]
, viz. the sense of reduction to tridiagonal form by application of a sequence of Householder transformations (which preserve spectrum and pseudospectra).
. For suppose that
A
is banded with band-width
w\in{\mathbb{N}}
and, for
b\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
\mathcal{I}_{b}:E\to E_{b}:=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X^{b})
be the mapping
x=(x_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\mapsto\xi=(\xi_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
, with
\xi_{i}=(x_{bi+1},\ldots,x_{b(i+1)})\in X^{b}
. Then
\mathcal{I}_{b}
is an isomorphism, indeed an isometric isomorphism if we equip the product space
X^{b}
with the norm

\|(y_{1},\ldots,y_{b})\|_{X^{b}}:=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{b}\|y_{j}\|_{X}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

(1.14)

(which we assume hereafter). Thus
A\in L(E)
and
A_{b}\in L(E_{b})
, given by
A_{b}=\mathcal{I}_{b}A\mathcal{I}_{b}^{-1}
, share the same spectrum and pseudospectra and, provided
b\geq w
, the matrix representation of
A_{b}
is tridiagonal.

Thus in the statement of our main results we focus on the case where
A\in L(E)
has a matrix representation that is tridiagonal, so that, introducing the abbreviations
\alpha_{j}:=A_{j+1,j}
,
\beta_{j}:=A_{j,j}
,
\gamma_{j}:=A_{j-1,j}
,

A\ =\ \left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}\ddots&\ddots\\
\ddots&\beta_{-2}&\gamma_{-1}\\
&\alpha_{-2}&\beta_{-1}&\gamma_{0}\\
\cline{4-4}\cr&&\alpha_{-1}&\vrule\lx@intercol\hfil\beta_{0}\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&\gamma_{1}\\
\cline{4-4}\cr&&&\alpha_{0}&\beta_{1}&\gamma_{2}\\
&&&&\alpha_{1}&\beta_{2}&\ddots\\
&&&&&\ddots&\ddots\end{array}\right),

(1.15)

where the box encloses
\beta_{0}=A_{0,0}
. As a special case of (
1.13
),
y:=Ax\in E

has
i
th entry
y_{i}\in X
given by

y_{i}\ =\ \alpha_{i-1}x_{i-1}\,+\,\beta_{i}x_{i}\,+\,\gamma_{i+1}x_{i+1},\qquad i\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

It is easy to see that the mapping
A
given by this rule is bounded, i.e.
A\in L(E)
, if and only if
(\alpha_{i})
,

(\beta_{i})
, and
(\gamma_{i})
are bounded sequences in
L(X)
, i.e. are sequences in
\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}},L(X))
, a Banach space equipped with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
defined by
\|x\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\|x_{i}\|_{L(X)}
, in which case

\|A\|\leq r(A)+\|\beta\|_{\infty},\quad\mbox{ where }r(A):=\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty}.

(1.16)

Conversely,
noting that
\|A_{ij}\|_{L(X)}\leq\|A\|
, for every
i,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

r(A)=\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty}\ \leq\ 2\|A\|.

(1.17)

1.3.1
Our spectral inclusion sets for the tridiagonal case: the
\tau
,
\pi
, and
\tau_{1}
methods

Since to derive spectral inclusion sets for
A\in BO(E)
it is enough to study the tridiagonal case, we restrict to this case in this subsection (and in much of the rest of the paper); the action of
A
is that of multiplication by the tridiagonal matrix (
1.15
). In this paper we derive for this tridiagonal case three different families of
spectral inclusion sets that enclose the spectrum or
pseudospectra of the operator
A
. Each spectral inclusion set is defined as the union of the
pseudospectra (or related sets) of certain finite matrices. These
finite matrices are either principal submatrices of the infinite matrix (
1.15
) (we call the corresponding approximation method
the “
\tau
method”, where
\tau
is for
truncation
), or are circulant-type
modifications of these submatrices (the “
\pi
method”,
\pi
for
periodised truncation
), or they are connected to
infinite submatrices with finite row- or column number (this is our “
\tau_{1}
method”,
\tau_{1}
for
one-sided truncation
).

In each method, as discussed below (
1.22
), there is a “penalty term”,
{\varepsilon}_{n}
,
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
, and
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
, for the
\tau
,
\pi
, and
\tau_{1}
methods, respectively, which arises from replacing infinite matrices by finite matrices. As will be demonstrated by the simple example where
A
is the shift operator in §
1.4
, our values for
{\varepsilon}_{n}
,
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
, and
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
are optimal; there is at least one tridiagonal
A\in L(E)
such that, if we make any of these values smaller, the claimed inclusions fail. In this limited sense our inclusion sets are sharp.

To define these methods,
for
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
P_{n,k}:E\to E
be the projection operator given by

(P_{n,k}x)_{j}\ =\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}x_{j},&j\in\{k+1,k+2,\dots,k+n\},\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

(1.18)

and let
E_{n,k}:=P_{n,k}(E)
be the
range of
P_{n,k}
.
In the
\tau
and
\pi
methods, we construct inclusion sets for
the spectrum and pseudospectrum of
A
from those of the finite
section operators
A_{n,k}:=P_{n,k}AP_{n,k}|_{E_{n,k}}:E_{n,k}\to E_{n,k}
and their “periodised” versions
A^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k}:E_{n,k}\to E_{n,k}
, with
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, so that, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
A_{n,k}
acts by multiplication by the
n\times n
principal submatrix

A_{n,k}\ =\ \left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\beta_{k+1}&\gamma_{k+2}&&&\\
\alpha_{k+1}&\beta_{k+2}&\gamma_{k+3}&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&\alpha_{k+n-2}&\beta_{k+n-1}&\gamma_{k+n}\\
&&&\alpha_{k+n-1}&\beta_{k+n}\end{array}\right)

(1.19)

of
A
and
A^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k}
acts by multiplication by the matrix
A^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k}:=A_{n,k}+B_{n,k}^{t}
, where
B_{n,k}^{t}
is the
n\times n
matrix whose entry in row
i
, column
j
is
\delta_{i,1}\delta_{j,n}t\alpha_{k}+\delta_{i,n}\delta_{j,1}\bar{t}\gamma_{k+n+1}
, where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta, so that, for
n\geq 3
,

A^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\beta_{k+1}&\gamma_{k+2}&&&t\alpha_{k}\\
\alpha_{k+1}&\beta_{k+2}&\gamma_{k+3}&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&\alpha_{k+n-2}&\beta_{k+n-1}&\gamma_{k+n}\\
\bar{t}\gamma_{k+n+1}&&&\alpha_{k+n-1}&\beta_{k+n}\end{array}\right).

(1.20)

The
\tau
method.
Defining
6
6

6

In the following equation
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,k}
denotes the open
{\varepsilon}
-pseudospectrum of the operator
A_{n,k}:E_{n,k}\to E_{n,k}
, where
E_{n,k}\subset E
is equipped with the norm of
E
or, what is the same thing, the open
{\varepsilon}
-pseudospectrum of the matrix (
1.19
), where the matrix norm is that induced by the norm (
1.14
) on
X^{n}
. A similar comment holds for the other
{\varepsilon}
-pseudospectra in (
1.21
) and (
1.23
).
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ :=\ \bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,k},\quad{\varepsilon}>0,\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ :=\ \overline{\bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,k}},\quad{\varepsilon}\geq 0,

(1.21)

the
\tau
-method inclusions sets are the right hand sides of the following inclusions (recall that
{\rm Spec}_{0}A:={\rm Spec}\,A
so that taking
{\varepsilon}=0
in the first of these inclusions provides an inclusion for
{\rm Spec}\,A
):

\begin{array}[]{|p{2mm}cp{2mm}|}\hline\cr&&\\[-7.11317pt]
&{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A),\quad{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A),\quad{\varepsilon}>0.&\\[5.69054pt]
\hline\cr\end{array}

(M
\tau
)

These inclusions (proved as Theorem
3.6
) hold for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the second inclusion with no constraint on the Banach space
X
, the first with the constraint that
X
satisfies Globevnik’s property (see §
2
), which holds in particular if
X
is finite-dimensional or a Hilbert space.
The term
{\varepsilon}_{n}
, defined in Theorem
3.6
(and see Corollary
3.7
for the special case that
A
is bidiagonal), enters (
M

τ

) as
a “truncation penalty” when
passing from the infinite matrix
A
to a finite
matrix of size
n\times n
. (Similar comments apply to the terms
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
and
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
in the
\pi
and
\tau_{1}
methods below.) Corollary
3.8
provides an upper bound for
{\varepsilon}_{n}
that implies that

0\ \leq\ {\varepsilon}_{n}\ \leq\ 2(\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})\,\sin\frac{\pi}{2(n+2)}\ \leq\ (\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})\,\frac{\pi}{n+2}.

(1.22)

The
\pi
method.
Similarly, defining, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
,

\pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ :=\ \bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k},\quad{\varepsilon}>0,\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ :=\ \overline{\bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k}},\quad{\varepsilon}\geq 0,

(1.23)

we prove in Corollary
4.5
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, the
\pi
-method inclusions

\begin{array}[]{|p{2mm}cp{2mm}|}\hline\cr&&\\[-7.11317pt]
&{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A),\quad{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A),\quad{\varepsilon}>0,&\\[5.69054pt]
\hline\cr\end{array}

(M
\pi
)

where

{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}\ :=\ 2(\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})\,\sin\frac{\pi}{2n},

(1.24)

and the second inclusion in (
M

π

) holds whatever the Banach space
X
, while the first requires that
X
has the Globevnik property.

Our motivation for the
\pi
method and the definitions (
1.23
) come from consideration of the case when the diagonals of
A
are constant, i.e.,
A
is a so-called
block-Laurent
matrix. If
A
is tridiagonal and block-Laurent and
X
is a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, then
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A=\cup_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)
, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
, and
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A=\cup_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}\pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)
, for
{\varepsilon}>0
(see Theorem
8.1
).

The
\tau_{1}
method.
The
\tau_{1}
method modifies these constructions using ideas from

[
26
,
31
,
52
]
(see the discussion in §
1.1
). To see the similarities but distinction between the
\tau
and
\tau_{1}
methods, for
B\in L(E)
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
let

\mu^{\dagger}_{n}(B)\ :=\ \inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\mu(P_{n,k}B|_{E_{n,k}})=\inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\min\Big(\nu\left(P_{n,k}B|_{E_{n,k}}\right),\,\nu\left(P_{n,k}B^{*}|_{E_{n,k}}\right)\Big).

(1.25)

Then, see Proposition
3.1
,

\sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ =\ \{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu^{\dagger}_{n}(A-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}\}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ =\ \{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu^{\dagger}_{n}(A-\lambda I)\leq{\varepsilon}\},

(1.26)

for
{\varepsilon}>0
, the first of these identities holding for every Banach space
X
, the second if
X
has the Globevnik property.
The operators
P_{n,k}B|_{E_{n,k}}
are two-sided truncations of
B
, each corresponding to an
n\times n
matrix. By contrast, in the
\tau_{1}
method we make one-sided truncations, dropping the
P_{n,k}
’s in (
1.25
) and so replacing
\mu^{\dagger}_{n}(B)
by

\mu_{n}(B)\ :=\inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\min\Big(\nu\left(B|_{E_{n,k}}\right),\,\nu\left(B^{*}|_{E_{n,k}}\right)\Big).

Precisely, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
let

\gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}}(A):=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu_{n}(A-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}\right\},\;\;{\varepsilon}>0,\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}}(A):=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu_{n}(A-\lambda I)\leq{\varepsilon}\right\},\;\;{\varepsilon}\geq 0.

(1.27)

Then the
\tau_{1}
method inclusions are

\begin{array}[]{|p{2mm}cp{2mm}|}\hline\cr&&\\[-7.11317pt]
&\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ \subset\ {\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A),\;\;{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ \subset\ {\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A),\;\;{\varepsilon}>0,&\\[5.69054pt]
\hline\cr\end{array}

(M
\tau_{1}
)

which hold for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, where

{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}:=2(\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})\sin\frac{\pi}{2(n+1)}.

(1.28)

In contrast to (
M

τ

) and (
M

π

), (
M

τ
1

) provides two-sided inclusions, which is significant for the convergence of the
\tau_{1}
method inclusion sets in the limit
n\to\infty
(see Theorem
1.2
). We will establish the inclusions from the right in §
5
, but the inclusions from the left, i.e. that
\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\subset{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
, and
\gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\subset{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, for
{\varepsilon}>0
, are immediate consequences of the first inequality in (
1.10
), which gives that
\mu_{n}(B)\geq\mu(B)
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
B\in L(E)
, the definitions (
1.27
), and (
1.9
).

Like the
\tau
- and
\pi
-method inclusion sets (
1.21
) and (
1.23
), also the
\tau_{1}
-method sets (
1.27
) can be expressed in terms of finite submatrices. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
(A-\lambda I)|_{E_{n,k}}
corresponds to the
\infty\times n
matrix consisting of columns
k+1
to
k+n
of
A
. Let
A^{+}_{n,k}
denote the matrix consisting of the
n+2
rows of this
\infty\times n
matrix that are non-zero, and let
I^{+}_{n}
denote the corresponding submatrix of the identity operator, so that
A^{+}_{n,k}
and
I^{+}_{n}
are the
(n+2)\times n
matrices

A^{+}_{n,k}:=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{k+1}&0&\cdots&0&0\\
\hline\cr&&A_{n,k}\\
\hline\cr 0&0&\cdots&0&\alpha_{k+n}\end{pmatrix}\;\mbox{and}\;\,I^{+}_{n}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&\cdots&0\\
\hline\cr&I_{n}&\\
\hline\cr 0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix},\;\mbox{where}\;\,I_{n}:=\begin{pmatrix}I_{X}\\
&\ddots\\
&&I_{X}\end{pmatrix}

(1.29)

is an
n\times n
identity matrix, and
I_{X}
is the identity operator on
X
. Then, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
\nu((A-\lambda I)|_{E_{n},k})=\nu\left(A^{+}_{n,k}-\lambda I^{+}_{n}\right)
and
\nu((A-\lambda I)^{*}|_{E_{n},k})=\nu\left((A^{*})^{+}_{n,k}-\lambda I^{+}_{n}\right)
, so that

\mu_{n}(A-\lambda I)=\inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\min\Big(\nu\left(A^{+}_{n,k}-\lambda I^{+}_{n}\right),\,\nu\left((A^{*})^{+}_{n,k}-\lambda I^{+}_{n}\right)\Big).

(1.30)

1.3.2
Our convergence results for the banded case

The inclusions (
M

τ

), (
M

π

), (
M

τ
1

), for the
\tau
,
\pi
, and
\tau_{1}
methods, are main results of the paper. A further key result, a corollary of the two-sided inclusion (
M

τ
1

), is the convergence result, stated as Theorem
1.2
below, that the
\tau_{1}
-method inclusion sets,
\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)
and
\gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)
, converge to the spectral sets that they include as
n\to\infty
.

The one-sided inclusions for the
\tau
and
\pi
methods, (
M

τ

) and (
M

π

), do not imply convergence of the corresponding inclusion sets to the spectral sets that they include; indeed we will exhibit examples where this convergence is absent (see Example
1.4
, §
8.1
, and §
8.3
). But these methods are convergent if they do not suffer from spectral pollution for the particular
A
in the sense of the following definition
7
7

7

The standard notion of spectral pollution, e.g.
[
31
]
, is that a sequence of linear operators
(A_{n})
approximating
A

suffers from spectral pollution
if there exists a sequence
z_{n}\in{\rm Spec}\,A_{n}
such that
z_{n}\to z
and
z\not\in{\rm Spec}\,A
. Spectral pollution is said to be absent if this does not hold. One might, alternatively, require absence of spectral pollution to mean that also every subsequence of
(A_{n})
does not suffer from spectral pollution, equivalently that
\max_{z\in{\rm Spec}\,A_{n}}{\rm dist}(z,{\rm Spec}\,A)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Absence of spectral pollution in this stronger sense is equivalent to a requirement that, for some positive null sequence
(\eta_{n})
,
{\rm Spec}\,A_{n}\subset{\rm spec}_{\eta_{n}}A
for each
n
. Our notion of absence of spectral pollution is a version of this requirement that is concerned with pseudospectra not just spectra, and is uniform with respect to the parameter
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
(cf.
[
31
]
). This is a strong assumption; indeed, to say that the
\tau
method does not suffer from spectral pollution is equivalent to saying that, for some positive null sequence
\eta_{n}
,
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)\subset{\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\eta_{n}}A
, for
{\varepsilon}>0
, and similarly for the
\pi
method, which inclusion takes the place, for the
\tau/\pi
methods, of the left hand inclusions in (
M

τ
1

), for the
\tau_{1}
method. Nevertheless, we will see below, in §
8.2
and §
8.4
, important applications where this absence-of-spectral-pollution assumption is satisfied.

Definition 1.1
(Absence of spectral pollution)

We say that the
\tau
method (the
\pi
method for a particular
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
) does not suffer from spectral pollution for a particular tridiagonal
A\in L(E)
if there exists a positive null sequence
(\eta_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that, for every
{\varepsilon}>0
,
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,k}\subset{\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\eta_{n}}A
(
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k}\subset{\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\eta_{n}}A
) for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

The following three theorems are our main convergence results for the
\tau_{1}
,
\tau
, and
\pi
methods. These are theorems for the case that
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal, but recall from §
1.3
that every
A\in BO(E)
can be written in this tridiagonal form. The proofs of these results, in which
denotes the standard Hausdorff convergence of sets introduced in §
1.3
, are so short that we include them here.

Theorem 1.2
(Convergence of the
\tau_{1}
method)

Suppose that the matrix representation of
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal. Then, as
n\to\infty
,

\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\;\;\mbox{for }{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\quad\mbox{in particular }\quad\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A,

(1.31)

If
X
has Globevnik’s property (see §
2
) then also

\gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\;\;\mbox{for }{\varepsilon}>0.

(1.32)

Proof.
It follows from (
M

τ
1

) that, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
,

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)\ \subset\ {\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}A.

(1.33)

Since
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, (
1.31
) follows from (
2.8
) and the observation at the end of the Hausdorff convergence discussion in §
1.3
. By (

τ
1

), for
{\varepsilon}>0
the inclusions (

) hold also with
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}
replaced by
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}
and
\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)

\gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)
. Further, if
X
has Globevnik’s property then
{\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}A\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
(see the comment below (
2.8
)), and (
1.32
) follows.

Theorem 1.3
(Convergence of the
\tau

Suppose that the matrix representation of

is tridiagonal, the
\tau

A
, and
X
has Globevnik’s property. Then, as
n\to\infty
,

\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\;\;\mbox{for }{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\quad\mbox{in particular }\quad\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A,

(1.34)

and

\sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\;\;\mbox{for }{\varepsilon}>0.

(1.35)

Proof.
It follows from (
M

τ

), and that the
\tau
method does not suffer from spectral pollution for
A
, that, for some positive null sequence
(\eta_{n})
,

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\ \subset\ \sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+1/n+{\varepsilon}_{n}}A\subset{\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+1/n+{\varepsilon}_{n}+\eta_{n}}A\subset{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+1/n+{\varepsilon}_{n}+\eta_{n}}A,

(1.36)

for

, so that (

) follows from (

). Similarly, for
{\varepsilon}>0
,

{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\ \subset\ {\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}+\eta_{n}}A.

so (

) follows from (

).

Arguing similarly, but using (

π

) instead of (

), we have an analogous result for the
\pi

Theorem 1.4
(Convergence of the
\pi

Suppose that the matrix representation of

is tridiagonal, that the

, and that
X

,

\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\;\;\mbox{for }{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\quad\mbox{in particular }\quad\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A,

(1.37)

and

\pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\;\;\mbox{for }{\varepsilon}>0.

(1.38)

Remark 1.5 (Estimation of
{\varepsilon}_{n}
,
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
, and
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
)
It is clear, inspecting the proofs, that Theorems
1.2
-
1.4
remain valid if
{\varepsilon}_{n}
,
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
, and
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
are replaced in the statements of the theorems by upper bounds
\eta_{n}
,
\eta^{\prime}_{n}
, and
\eta^{\prime\prime}_{n}
, as long as these upper bounds tend to zero as
n\to\infty
. In particular, Theorem
1.3
remains true with
{\varepsilon}_{n}
, as defined in Theorem
3.6
, replaced by the explicit upper bounds for
{\varepsilon}_{n}
obtained in Corollaries
3.8
and
3.9
.

1.3.3
The band-dominated case

The above results for banded operators can be extended to the band-dominated case by perturbation arguments, precisely by using (
2.4
) below, a simple perturbation inclusion for pseudospectra. Perhaps unexpectedly, this leads not just to inclusion sets but also to convergent sequences of approximations, even for the spectrum.

To see how this works, suppose that
A\in BDO(E)
, and let us focus for brevity on the
\tau_{1}
method and the case that
X
is finite-dimensional or a Hilbert space (we prove a closely related results for the general Banach space case in §
6
as Theorem
6.1
). Then, by definition, there exists a sequence
(A^{(n)})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset BO(E)
such that
\delta_{n}:=\|A-A^{(n)}\|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Let
w_{n}
be the bandwidth of
A^{(n)}
and let
A_{n}:=\mathcal{I}_{w_{n}}A^{(n)}\mathcal{I}^{-1}_{w_{n}}
, where
\mathcal{I}_{b}
is defined, for
b\in{\mathbb{N}}
, as below (
1.14
). Then, as discussed in §
1.3
, the matrix representation of
A_{n}
is tridiagonal. We will apply the inclusion (
M

τ
1

) to
A_{n}
. Let us write
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
in that inclusion as
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A)
to indicate explicitly its dependence on
A
, so that

{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A)=2r(A)\sin\frac{\pi}{2(n+1)},

(1.39)

where
r(A)
is defined by (
1.2
).
Then, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, using (
M

τ
1

) and (
2.5
), we see that

\displaystyle{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}}A^{(n)}={\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}}A_{n}

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A_{n})\subset{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A^{(n)})\subset{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+2\delta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A).

Now
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, since, by (
1.17
),
r(A_{n})\leq 2\|A_{n}\|=2\|A^{(n)}\|\leq 2(\|A\|+\delta_{n})
. Thus, by (
2.8
),
{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+2\delta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
, which gives immediately the following result.

Theorem 1.6

Suppose that
X
is finite-dimensional or a Hilbert space and
A\in BDO(E)
, let
(A^{(n)})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset BO(E)
be such that
\delta_{n}:=\|A-A^{(n)}\|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, and let
A_{n}:=\mathcal{I}_{w_{n}}A^{(n)}\mathcal{I}^{-1}_{w_{n}}
, where
w_{n}
is the band-width of
A^{(n)}
, so that
A_{n}
is
A^{(n)}
written in tridiagonal form. Then, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\subset\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A_{n})\subset{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+2\delta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A),

(1.40)

so that
\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A_{n})\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
, in particular
\Gamma^{n}_{\delta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A_{n})\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
.

Remark 1.7 (Construction of banded approximations and estimation of
\delta_{n}
)
It is easy to see that the statements in the above theorem remain true (cf. Remark
1.3.2
) if
\delta_{n}
is replaced throughout by any upper bound
\eta_{n}\geq\delta_{n}:=\|A-A^{(n)}\|
, as long as
\eta_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
; in particular,
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\subset\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+\eta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A_{n})
and
\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+\eta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A_{n})\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
. But to compute the inclusion set
\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+\eta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A_{n})
one needs both a concrete banded approximation
A^{(n)}
to
A
and the upper bound
\eta_{n}\geq\delta_{n}
. Concretely, one can take, for some
\mathfrak{p}\geq 0
,
A^{(n)}\in BO(E)
to be the matrix with band-width
n
and entries
[A^{(n)}_{ij}]
given by

A^{(n)}_{ij}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(1-\frac{|i-j|}{n+1}\right)^{\mathfrak{p}}A_{ij},&|i-j|\leq n,\\
0,&|i-j|>n,\end{array}\right.

(1.41)

for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Provided
\mathfrak{p}>0
, it holds that
\delta_{n}=\|A-A^{(n)}\|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
(see the proof that (e)
\Rightarrow
(a) in
[
72
, Theorem 2.1.6]
).
For
\mathfrak{p}=0
,
A^{(n)}
is the obvious approximation to
A
with band-width
n
obtained by simply discarding the matrix entries
A_{ij}
with
|i-j|>n
. In many cases this approximation is adequate, but it does not hold in this case that
\|A-A^{(n)}\|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
for all
A\in BDO(E)
(see the example in
[
62
, Remark 1.40]
).

One case where the simple choice
\mathfrak{p}=0
is effective is where
A\in\mathcal{W}(E)
, the so-called
Wiener algebra
(e.g.,
[
62
, Definition 1.43]
). In terms of the matrix representation
[A_{ij}]
,
A\in\mathcal{W}(A)
precisely when there exists
\kappa=(\kappa_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\in\ell^{1}({\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{R}})
such that
\|A_{ij}\|_{L(X)}\leq\kappa_{i-j}
, for
i,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. In that case it is clear that (see
[
62
, Equation (1.27)]
), defining
A^{(n)}
by (
1.41
) with
\mathfrak{p}=0
,

\|A-A^{(n)}\|\leq\eta_{n}:=\sum_{|k|>n}\kappa_{k},\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}},

with
\eta_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Remark 1.8

(Proving invertibility of operators)
Let us flag one significant application of Theorem
1.6
and our other convergence theorems, Theorems
1.2
-
1.4
, when coupled with our inclusion results, (
M

τ

), (
M

π

), (
M

τ
1

), and (
1.40
). Suppose that
A\in BDO(E)
and that
(U_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathbb{C}}
is a sequence of compact sets with the properties that: (a)
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset U_{n}
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
; (b)
U_{n}\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
. Then it is easy to see that the following claim holds for any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
:

\lambda I-A\mbox{ is invertible }\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\lambda\not\in U_{n},\mbox{ for some }n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(1.42)

In particular,
A
is invertible if and only if
0\not\in U_{n}
, for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Of course, by Theorem
1.6
, if
X
is finite-dimensional or a Hilbert space, a sequence
(U_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
with these properties, for any
A\in BDO(E)
, is the sequence
U_{n}:=\Gamma^{n}_{\delta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A_{n})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, where
A_{n}
is as in Theorem
1.6
. We will see below in Theorem
7.1
that, in the case that
X={\mathbb{C}}
, another sequence with properties (a) and (b) is the sequence
U_{n}=\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The attraction of this alternative choice for
(U_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is that one can determine whether
\lambda\in U_{n}
in only finitely many arithmetic operations (see Theorem
7.1
). Thus, if
\lambda I-A
is invertible, this can be proved in finite time, by checking whether
\lambda\in U_{n}
, successively for
n=1,2,\ldots
, stopping when a
U_{n}
is found such that
\lambda\not\in U_{n}
. (The existence of such a
U_{n}
is guaranteed by (
1.42
).)

At the end of §
8.4.1
we use (
1.42
), with
U_{n}=\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
, for an operator
A
, the so-called Feinberg-Zee operator, for which this choice of
U_{n}
satisfies (a) and (b), to show that a particular
\lambda\not\in{\rm Spec}\,A
, proving that
{\rm Spec}\,A
is a strict subset of the closure of its numerical range.

1.3.4
Computability of our inclusion sets: reduction to finitely many matrices

A criticism of the inclusion sets that we have introduced in §
1.3.1
from the perspective of practical computation is that, to determine whether or not a particular
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
is in one of these inclusion sets, one has to make a computation for infinitely many finite matrices, indexed by
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

In certain cases this infinite collection of finite matrices is in fact a finite collection. For the inclusion sets with parameter
n\geq 2
, this is the case precisely when the tridiagonal matrix
A
has only finitely many distinct entries. (If
n=1
it is enough, for the
\tau
method, if the set
\{\beta_{k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
is finite.)

When
A
has infinitely many distinct entries, provided the set of matrix entries is a relatively compact subset of
L(X)
, which is the case, in particular, if
X
is finite-dimensional, we can approximate the infinite collection arbitrarily closely by a finite subset. We spell out the details in the following theorem for the
\tau_{1}
method under the assumption that
X
is finite-dimensional or a Hilbert space; similar results hold for the
\tau
and
\pi
methods. The point of this theorem is that, for every
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
, whether or not
\lambda
is in
\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)
can be established by determining whether or not the lower norms of finitely many finite matrices are
\leq{\varepsilon}
. Note that the definition of
\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)
in (
1.45
) is identical to the definition (
1.27
) of
\Gamma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
, except that
\mu^{n}
, defined by taking an infimum over
{\mathbb{Z}}
, is replaced by
\mu_{n}^{\mathrm{fin}}
, defined by taking a minimum over the finite set
K_{n}
. Thus, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
,

\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\subset\Gamma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A),\quad\mbox{indeed also}\quad\Gamma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)\subset\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}+1/n}(A),

(1.43)

as a consequence of (
1.10
), if (
1.44
) holds.
This result, indeed an extended version of this result that applies in the general band-dominated case and for every Banach space
X
, is proved in §
6
as Theorem
6.1
. A version of this theorem for the
\tau
and
\pi
methods, under the assumption of absence of spectral pollution (Definition
1.1
), is proved as Theorem
6.3
.

Theorem 1.9

Suppose that
X
is finite-dimensional or a Hilbert space, that
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal, and that
\{A_{ij}:i,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}\subset L(X)
is relatively compact. Then, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists a finite set
K_{n}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
such that:

\forall k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\;\;\exists j\in K_{n}\mbox{ such that }\|A^{+}_{n,k}-A^{+}_{n,j}\|\leq 1/n\mbox{ and }\|(A^{*})^{+}_{n,k}-(A^{*})^{+}_{n,j}\|\leq 1/n.

(1.44)

Further, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ \subset\ {\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\subset\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}+1/n}(A),

where

\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A):=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu_{n}^{\mathrm{fin}}(A-\lambda I)\leq{\varepsilon}\right\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mu_{n}^{\mathrm{fin}}(B):=\min_{k\in K_{n}}\min\Big(\nu\left(B|_{E_{n,k}}\right),\,\nu\left(B^{*}|_{E_{n,k}}\right)\Big),

(1.45)

for
B\in L(E)
, so that

\mu_{n}^{\mathrm{fin}}(A-\lambda I)=\min_{k\in K_{n}}\min\Big(\nu\left(A^{+}_{n,k}-\lambda I^{+}_{n}\right),\,\nu\left((A^{*})^{+}_{n,k}-\lambda I^{+}_{n}\right)\Big),\quad\mbox{for }\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}.

(1.46)

Further, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
,
\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}+1/n}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
, in particular
\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}+1/n}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
.

Of course a practical issue for computation is how to determine the finite set
K_{n}
. As a concrete, nontrivial example, we demonstrate how to reduce from an infinite set to a finite set for the class of tridiagonal operators that are pseudoergodic in the sense of Davies
[
28
]
in §
8.4.2
.

1.3.5
An algorithm for computing the spectrum in the case
X={\mathbb{C}}^{p}
and its solvability complexity index

In the finite-dimensional case that
X={\mathbb{C}}^{p}
, for some
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
, equipped with the usual Euclidean norm, a sequence of approximations converging to
{\rm Spec}\,A
, in the case when
A
is band-dominated, can be realised with each member of the sequence computed in finitely many operations, provided we have available as inputs to the computation sufficient information about
A
.
We will demonstrate this for the general band-dominated case in §
7
. For the tridiagonal case, with
A
given by (
1.15
) and
X={\mathbb{C}}^{p}
(recall that we noted in §
1.3
that every
A\in BO(E)
with
X={\mathbb{C}}
can be written in this form), the
\tau_{1}
version of the algorithm proceeds as follows. We use in this definition the notation (cf. (
1.56
))

\mathrm{Grid}(n,r):=\frac{1}{n}({\mathbb{Z}}+{\rm i}{\mathbb{Z}})\cap r{\mathbb{D}},\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}},\;\;r>0.

(1.47)

Let
\Omega^{p}_{T}
denote the set of all tridiagonal matrices (
1.15
), with
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
and
X={\mathbb{C}}^{p}
, for some
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Where, as usual,
P(S)
denotes the power set of a set
S
, the inputs we need are:

1.

A mapping
\mathcal{A}_{p}:\Omega^{p}_{T}\times{\mathbb{N}}\to P({\mathbb{Z}})
,
(A,n)\mapsto K_{n}
, where
K_{n}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
is a finite set such that (
1.44
) holds.

2.

A mapping
{\mathcal{B}}_{p}:\Omega^{p}_{T}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
,
A\mapsto(\alpha_{\max},\beta_{\max},\gamma_{\max})
, such that
\alpha_{\max}\geq\|\alpha\|_{\infty}
,
\beta_{\max}\geq\|\beta\|_{\infty}
,
\gamma_{\max}\geq\|\gamma\|_{\infty}
.

3.

A mapping
{\mathcal{C}}_{p}:\Omega^{p}_{T}\times{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{N}}\to(X^{(n+2)\times n})^{2}
,
(A,n,k)\mapsto(A^{+}_{n,k},(A^{*})^{+}_{n,k})
.

It is not clear to us how to construct a map
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
such that
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}(A,n)
can be explicitly computed for general
A\in\Omega^{p}_{T}
. Indeed, establishing existence of a map
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}:\Omega^{p}_{T}\times{\mathbb{N}}\to P({\mathbb{Z}})
with the required properties may require an application of the axiom of choice. But, as a non-trivial example, we will construct in §
8.4.2
a version of the mapping
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
for the important subset
\Omega_{\Psi}\subset\Omega^{1}_{T}
of operators that are pseudoergodic in the sense of Davies
[
28
]
.

The sequence of approximations to
{\rm Spec}\,A
, each element of which can be computed in finitely many arithmetical operations, given finitely many evaluations of the above input maps, is
(\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, where (recalling definition (
1.45
))

\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A):=\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}+3/n}(A)\cap\mathrm{Grid}(n,R),\qquad n\in{\mathbb{N}},

(1.48)

with (cf. (
1.28
))

{\varepsilon}_{n}^{*}\ :=\ (\alpha_{\max}+\gamma_{\max})\,\frac{22}{7(n+1)}\ \geq\ 2(\alpha_{\max}+\gamma_{\max})\,\sin\frac{\pi}{2(n+1)}\geq{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n},

(1.49)

(\alpha_{\max},\beta_{\max},\gamma_{\max}):={\mathcal{B}}_{p}(A)
,
R:=\alpha_{\max}+\beta_{\max}+\gamma_{\max}
(so that
R
is an upper bound for
\|A\|
), and
K_{n}
, in the definition (
1.45
) and (
1.46
), given by
K_{n}:={\mathcal{A}}_{p}(A,n)
.

Proposition 1.10

For
A\in\Omega^{p}_{T}
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
can be computed in finitely many arithmetic operations, given finitely many evaluations of the functions
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{p}
, and
{\mathcal{C}}_{p}
, namely the evaluations:
K_{n}:={\mathcal{A}}_{p}(A,n)
;
(\alpha_{\max},\beta_{\max},\gamma_{\max}):={\mathcal{B}}_{p}(A)
, and
(A_{n,k}^{+},(A^{*})^{+}_{n,k}):={\mathcal{C}}_{p}(A,k,n)
, for
k\in{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
. Further,
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
, and also

\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\ :=\ \Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)+\frac{2}{n}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm Spec}\,A

as
n\to\infty
, with
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

We give the proof of the above result, and a version for
A\in BDO(E)
, in §
7
.

The above result can be interpreted as a result relating to the solvability complexity index (SCI) of
[
1
,
2
,
22
]
. Let us abbreviate
\Omega_{T}^{1}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
, and
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
as
\Omega_{T}
,
{\mathcal{A}}
,
{\mathcal{B}}
, and
{\mathcal{C}}
, respectively, so that
\Omega_{T}
is the set of tridiagonal matrices with complex number entries. With the
evaluation set
(in the sense of
[
1
,
2
,
22
]
)
8
8

8

Strictly, to fit with the definition in
[
22
, §2.1]
, each element of the evaluation set
\Lambda
should be a complex-valued function on
\Omega_{T}
. But it is easy to express our evaluation set in this form, expressing each of our functions in terms of a finite number of complex-valued functions. In particular, where
M:=|K_{n}|
, the set of integers
K_{n}=\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{M}\}
, with
k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots<k_{M}
, which is the output of the mapping
{\mathcal{A}}(\cdot,n)
applied to
A
, might be encoded as the complex number
x+{\rm i}y
, with
x:=k_{1}
and
y:=\Pi_{i=1}^{M}p_{i}^{k_{i}+1-k_{1}}
, where
p_{i}
is the
i
th prime number.

\Lambda:=\{{\mathcal{A}}(\cdot,n),{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{C}}(\cdot,k,n):k\in{\mathbb{Z}},\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\},

(1.50)

and where we equip
{\mathbb{C}}^{C}
, the set of compact subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
, with the Hausdorff metric (see §
1.3
),
the mappings

\Omega_{T}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{C},\qquad A\mapsto\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\quad\mbox{and}\quad A\mapsto\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A),

are
general algorithms
in the sense of
[
1
,
2
,
22
]
, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Further,
\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
can be computed in finitely many arithmetic operations and specified using finitely many complex numbers (the elements of
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
and the value of
n
). Thus, where
\Xi:\Omega_{T}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{C}
is the mapping given by
\Xi(A):={\rm Spec}\,A
, for
A\in\Omega_{T}
, the
computational problem

\{\Xi,\Omega_{T},{\mathbb{C}}^{C},\Lambda\}
has arithmetic SCI, in the sense of
[
1
,
2
,
22
]
, equal to one; more precisely, since also
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
A\in\Omega_{T}
, this computational problem is in the class
\Pi_{1}^{A}
, as defined in
[
2
,
22
]
.

The same observations on SCI classification hold true for the corresponding computational problem, with
\Omega_{T}
replaced by
\Omega:=BDO(E)
, that we consider in §
7
; again this has arithmetic SCI equal to one. In contrast, as we recall in §
1.5
, the computational problem of determining the spectrum of a band-dominated operator (indeed even the restricted problem of determining the spectrum of a tridiagonal matrix) has been shown in
[
23
]
, adapting the arguments of
[
1
,
2
]
, to have an SCI of two when the evaluation set, the set of allowed inputs to the computation, is restricted to the mappings
\Omega_{T}\to{\mathbb{C}}
,
A\mapsto A_{ij}
, for
i,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, providing evaluation of the matrix elements.

1.4
A first example demonstrating the sharpness of our inclusion sets

We will explore the utility and properties of these new families of inclusion sets in a range of examples in §
8
. But, to aid comprehension of the
\tau
,
\pi
, and
\tau_{1}
inclusion sets introduced in §
1.3.1
, and demonstrate their sharpness, let us pause to illustrate them as applied to one of the simplest scalar examples, an example with
X={\mathbb{C}}
so that the matrix entries are just complex numbers. We will return to this example in §
8.1
.

Example 1.11  –  The shift operator.

Let
V
denote the shift operator on
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
, taking
x=(x_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\in\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
to
y=(y_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\in\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})

with
x_{j}=y_{j+1}
for all
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Its matrix is of the form (
1.15
) with

\alpha_{j}=1
,
\beta_{j}=0
and
\gamma_{j}=0
for all
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For
A:=V
, the matrices (
1.19
) and (
1.20
) are given by

A_{n,k}=V_{n}\ :=\ \left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&\\
1&0&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&1&0\end{array}\right)_{n\times n}\ \textrm{and}\quad A^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k}=V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}\ :=\ \left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&&&t\\
1&0&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&1&0\end{array}\right)_{n\times n},

(1.51)

for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, independent of
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
The circulant matrix
V_{n}^{{\sf per},1}
is normal and has spectrum
{\rm spec}\,V^{{\sf per},1}_{n}={\mathbb{T}}_{n}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:z^{n}=1\}
(e.g.,
[
83
, Thm. 7.1]
). For
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, where
\alpha=t^{1/n}
denotes any one of the
n
th roots of
t
, and
D_{n}:={\rm diag}(\alpha,\ldots,\alpha^{n})
, it holds that
D_{n}^{-1}(V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}-\lambda I_{n})D_{n}=\alpha(V^{{\sf per},1}_{n}-\lambda\bar{\alpha}I_{n})
, so that
V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}
is also normal and

{\rm Spec}\,V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}=t^{1/n}{\rm Spec}\,V^{{\sf per},1}_{n}=t^{1/n}{\mathbb{T}}_{n}.

(1.52)

(Note that the set
t^{1/n}{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
is independent of which
n
th root of
t
we select.)
Thus, for each
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, the spectrum of
V
,
{\rm Spec}\,V={\mathbb{T}}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z|=1\}
, is well approximated as
n\to\infty
by

{\rm Spec}\,V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}
, but clearly not by
{\rm Spec}\,V_{n}=\{0\}
.

The
\tau
method and
\pi
method inclusion sets for
{\rm Spec}\,V={\rm Spec}_{0}V
, given by (
M

τ

) and
(
M

π

), respectively, reduce in this case to the sets

\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(V)={\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(V_{n})

and

\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(V)={\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}),\quad t\in{\mathbb{T}}.

Since
\|\alpha\|_{\infty}=1
and
\|\gamma\|_{\infty}=0
for this example, we have by Corollary
3.7
and (
1.24
) that

{\varepsilon}_{n}=2\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{4n+2}\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}=2\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2n}\right).

{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(V_{n})
and
{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(V^{{\sf per},t}_{n})
are neighbourhoods of
{\rm Spec}\,V_{n}=\{0\}
and
{\rm Spec}\,V^{{\sf per}_{n},t}=t^{1/n}{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
, respectively. By (
M

τ

) and
(
M

π

) these neighbourhoods must be large enough to cover

{\rm Spec}\,V={\mathbb{T}}
. As we will see in §
8.1
, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(V)
is precisely the closed unit disc, while (we recall this and other properties of pseudospectra below in §
2
), since
V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}
is normal,
{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(V^{{\sf per},t}_{n})=\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(V)
is the closed
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
neighbourhood of
t^{1/n}{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
. Further, as we will see in §
8.1
,
\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V)
, the
\tau_{1}
method inclusion set given by (
M

π

), is the closed annulus with outer radius 1, inner radius
1-({\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n})^{2}
, where

{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}=2\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2(n+1)}\right).

Of course any intersection of these inclusion sets is also an inclusion set. In particular, see §
8.1
,

\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(V)=\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}\left(t^{1/n}{\mathbb{T}}_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}\,\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\right)

is also a closed annulus, with outer radius 1, inner radius
1-({\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n})^{2}
.
The
\tau
,
\pi
, and
\tau_{1}
inclusion sets for
{\rm spec}\,V={\mathbb{T}}
are shown for a range of values of
n
, selecting
t=1
for the
\pi
method, in Figure
1.1
.

This simple example illustrates the sharpness of the inclusions (
M

τ

), (
M

π

), and (
M

τ
1

). As we will see in §
8.1
, the inclusion sets, which are the closed
disc
\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
for the
\tau
method, the union
t^{1/n}+{\varepsilon}_{n}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
of closed discs around shifted roots of unity for the
\pi
method,
and a closed annulus for the
\tau_{1}
method, do not cover
{\rm spec}\,V={\mathbb{T}}

if
{\varepsilon}_{n}
,
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
, and
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
are any smaller than the values given above.

This example also illustrates Theorem
1.2
on convergence of the
\tau_{1}
method, that
\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(V)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm spec}\,V
as
n\to\infty
. Since also, for each
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
,
\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(V)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm spec}\,V
as
n\to\infty
, this example also illustrates that the
\pi
method can be convergent. (Generalising this example, we will show in §
8.4
, by application of Theorem
1.4
, that the
\pi
method is convergent for all tridiagonal
A\in L(E)
with scalar entries that are pseudoergodic in the sense of Davies
[
28
]
, this class including all tridiagonal Laurent matrices, where the diagonals
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
are constant.) Of course, this is also an example illustrating that the
\tau
method need not be convergent, an example where the
\tau
method suffers from spectral pollution (see Definition
1.1
), so that the conditions of Theorem
1.3
are not satisfied.

n=8

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{$\tau$ method}}}{{\rule{0.0pt}{28.45274pt}}}

The
\tau

, and

, and

, respectively, for

.

1.5
Related work

Let us give more detail about previous work related to this paper, on inclusions sets and approximation algorithms for the spectrum and pseudospectrum of bounded linear operators. See also the recent, overlapping review in
[
22
]
.

Given a Banach space
Y
, and
A\in L(Y)
, a trivial but important inclusion set for
{\rm Spec}\,A
is the ball
\|A\|\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z|\leq\|A\|\}
. In the case that
Y
is a Hilbert space, a well-known, sharper inclusion set is
\overline{\mathrm{Num}\,A}
, the closure of
\mathrm{Num}\,A
, the numerical range of
A
. Important generalisations of these respective inclusion sets are the higher order hull and (in the case that
Y
is Hilbert space) the higher order numerical range, denoted
\mathrm{Hull}_{n}A
and
\mathrm{Num}_{n}A
, respectively, and defined by (e.g.,
[
30
, §9.4]
)

\mathrm{Hull}_{n}A:=\bigcap_{\mathrm{deg}(p)\leq n}\left\{z:|p(z)|\leq\|p(A)\|\right\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{Num}_{n}A:=\bigcap_{\mathrm{deg}(p)\leq n}\left\{z:p(z)\in\overline{\mathrm{Num}(p(A))}\right\},

for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Note that
\mathrm{Num}_{1}A=\overline{\mathrm{Num}\,A}
and, rather surprisingly (see
[
10
]
,
[
30
, Thm. 9.4.5]
),
\mathrm{Hull}_{n}A=\mathrm{Num}_{n}A
, for each
n
, in the case that
Y
is a Hilbert space. Clearly, the higher order hulls and numerical ranges form decreasing sequences, i.e.
\mathrm{Hull}_{n+1}A\subset\mathrm{Hull}_{n}A
and
\mathrm{Num}_{n+1}A\subset\mathrm{Num}_{n}A
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, so that

\mathrm{Hull}_{n}A\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\mathrm{Hull}_{\infty}A:=\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathrm{Hull}_{n}A\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{Num}_{n}A\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\mathrm{Num}_{\infty}A:=\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathrm{Num}_{n}A,

as
n\to\infty
. (Of course,
\mathrm{Num}_{\infty}A
is defined only in the Hilbert space case, in which case
\mathrm{Hull}_{\infty}A=\mathrm{Num}_{\infty}A
, since
\mathrm{Hull}_{n}A=\mathrm{Num}_{n}A
for each
n
.)

These inclusion sets may be sharp or asymptotically sharp as
n\to\infty
. In particular
[
30
, Lem. 9.4.4]
,
\mathrm{Hull}_{2}(A)=\mathrm{Num}_{2}(A)={\rm Spec}(A)
in the Hilbert space case if
A
is self-adjoint. Further
[
69
, Thm. 2.10.3]

\mathrm{Hull}_{\infty}A=\widehat{{\rm Spec}\,}A
, the complement of the unbounded component of
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\rm Spec}\,A
, so that
\mathrm{Hull}_{n}A\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
if and only if
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\rm Spec}\,A
has only one component. But, while these sequences of inclusion sets are of significant theoretical interest and converge in many cases to
{\rm Spec}\,A
, it is unclear, for general operators
A
and particularly for larger
n
, how to realise these sets computationally. (However, see the related work of Frommer et al.
[
40
]
on computation of inclusion sets for pseudospectra via approximate computation of numerical ranges of resolvents of the operator.)

For finite matrices, as we have noted already in §
1.1
, a standard inclusion set for the spectrum (the set of eigenvalues) is provided by Gershgorin’s theorem
[
42
,
84
]
. This result has been generalised, independently in Ostrowski
[
70
]
, Feingold & Varga
[
38
]
, and Fiedler & Pták
[
39
]
(and see
[
84
, Chapter 6]
), to cases where the entries of the
N\times N
matrix
A=[A_{ij}]
are themselves submatrices
9
9

9

These papers are arguably the earliest occurrence of pseudospectra in the literature (cf.
[
83
, §I.6]
