). The Gershgorin-type enclosure for the spectrum obtained in each of these papers (e.g.,
[
38
, Equation (3.1)]
), which is essentially the right-hand-side of (
1.53
), is thought of in
[
38
]
as the union of
N
so-called Gershgorin sets
G_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,N
. The
j
th set
G_{j}
(though this language is not used), is precisely a (closed) pseudospectrum of
A_{jj}
, the
j
th matrix on the diagonal.

.
A further extension to the case where each entry
A_{ij}
is an operator between Banach spaces has been made by Salas
[
74
]
. To make clear the connection with this paper, consider the case where each entry
A_{ij}\in L(X)
, for some Banach space
X
. The main result of
[
74
]
in that case, expressed in the language of pseudospectra, is that

{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}{\rm Spec}_{r_{i}}A_{ii},\qquad\mbox{where}\quad r_{i}:=\sum_{j=1,\,j\neq i}^{N}\|A_{ij}\|_{L(X)},

(1.53)

which is close to (
1.1
) in the case
n=1
, since
{\varepsilon}_{1}=r(A)
and
r(A)
is an upper bound for
\sup_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}r_{i}
. Of course, (
1.53
) is for finite rather than infinite matrices. Several authors have generalised the Gershgorin theorem to infinite matrices with scalar entries, but the focus has been on cases where the infinite matrix
A
is an unbounded operator with a discrete spectrum; see
[
81
,
34
,
34
]
and the references therein. A simple generalisation of the Gershgorin theorem to the case where
A
is a tridiagonal matrix of the form (
1.15
), with scalar entries, that is a bounded operator on
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
is proved as
[
18
, Theorem 2.50]
. This has (
1.1
) for
n=1
as an immediate corollary.

Probably the most natural idea to approximate
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, where
A\in BO(E)
or
BDO(E)
, with
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{N}})
or
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
, is to use the pseudospectra,
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n}
, of the finite sections of
A
, i.e.,
A_{n}:=[A_{ij}]_{i,j=1}^{n}
or
A_{n}:=[A_{ij}]_{i,j=-n}^{n}
, respectively.
In some cases, including Toeplitz operators
[
73
,
9
]
and semi-infinite random Jacobi operators

[
17
]
,
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n}\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
. (In some instances where the finite section method is not effective, the pseudospectra of periodised finite sections (cf. our
\pi
method) can converge to
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
; see
[
19
]
and Remark
8.4
.) But in general, as noted already in §
1.1
, the sequence
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n}
does not converge to
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
; its cluster points typically contain
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
but also contain points outside
{\rm Spec}\,A
(see, e.g.,
[
77
]
).

Even in the self-adjoint case one observes this effect of so-called spectral pollution; see, e.g., Davies & Plum
[
31
]
. As we noted in §
1.1
, Davies & Plum
[
31
]
, building on Davies
[
26
]
, proposed, as a method to locate eigenvalues of self-adjoint operators in spectral gaps while avoiding spectral pollution, to compute
\nu(P_{m}(A-\lambda I)|_{E_{n}})
, with
m>n
, as an approximation to
\nu(A-\lambda I)
(which coincides with
\mu(A-\lambda I)
, defined by (
1.8
), in this self-adjoint setting). Here
(P_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is any sequence of orthogonal projections onto finite-dimensional subspaces that is strongly convergent to the identity, and
E_{n}:=P_{n}(E)
is the range of
P_{n}
The significance of this proposal is that, in this self-adjoint setting: (a)
{\rm Spec}\,A=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}:\nu(A-\lambda I)=0\}
(see (
1.8
)); (b)
\nu(P_{m}(A-\lambda I)|_{E_{n}})\to\nu((A-\lambda I)|_{E_{n}})
as
m\to\infty
; (c)
\nu((A-\lambda I)|_{E_{n}})\to\nu(A-\lambda I)
as
n\to\infty
, uniformly for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
; indeed
[
31
, Lemma 5]
,

\nu(A-\lambda I)\leq\nu((A-\lambda I)|_{E_{n}})\leq\nu(A-\lambda I)+\eta_{n},

(1.54)

for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, where
\eta_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. To relate this to our results above, suppose that
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
and
P_{n}:=P_{2n+1,-n-1}
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, where
P_{n,k}
is defined by (
1.18
). Then, if
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal, and using the notations of §
1.1
,

\nu(P_{m}(A-\lambda I)|_{E_{n}})=\nu((A-\lambda I)|_{E_{n}})=\nu((A-\lambda I)^{+}_{n}),

for
m\geq n+1
, so that the second inequality of (
1.54
) bears a resemblance to Corollary
5.2
(except that, importantly, (
1.54
) can give no information on the dependence of
\eta_{n}
on
A
).

These ideas are moved to the non-self-adjoint case in Hansen
[
52
,
53
]
(see also
[
58
,
2
,
22
]
), where an approximation
{\mathcal{S}}^{n,m}_{\varepsilon}(A)
, for
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
m\geq n
, to
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
is proposed. This approximation, written in terms of lower norms using (
7.1
), to match the work of Davies & Plum
[
31
]
, is given by

{\mathcal{S}}_{\varepsilon}^{n,m}(A):=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\min\left(\nu\left(P_{m}(A-\lambda I)|_{E_{n}}\right),\nu\left(P_{m}(A-\lambda I)^{*}|_{E_{n}}\right)\right)\leq{\varepsilon}\right\},

(1.55)

where
E_{n}=P_{n}(E)
,
E
is some separable Hilbert space, and
(P_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is a sequence of projection operators converging strongly to the identity. Using (
7.1
), in the case we consider where
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
, and if we define
P_{n}:=P_{2n+1,-n-1}
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and if
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal, then, for
m\geq n+1
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{\varepsilon}^{n,m}(A)
is the specific approximation (
1.5
), which we have already contrasted with the new approximations in this paper in §
1.1
. Key features of the approximation (
1.55
) from the perspectives of the current work are: (i) that one can determine whether
\lambda\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\varepsilon}^{n,m}(A)
in finitely many arithmetic operations (via the characterisations (
7.3
) and (
7.4
) in terms of positive definiteness and singular values of rectangular matrices, see
[
53
]
); and that: (ii) if
A
band-dominated and
m=2n
, then
{\mathcal{S}}_{\varepsilon}^{n,m}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
(see
[
2
]
).

Hansen
[
52
,
53
]
extends (
1.55
) to provide, in the Hilbert space context, a sequence of approximations, based on computations with finite rectangular matrices, also to the so-called
(N,{\varepsilon})
-pseudospectra,
{\rm spec}_{N,{\varepsilon}}A
, defined in
[
53
, Definition 1.2]
. These generalise the pseudospectrum (note that
{\rm spec}_{0,{\varepsilon}}A={\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
), and are another sequence of inclusions sets: one has that
{\rm Spec}\,A+{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{D}}\subset{\rm spec}_{N,{\varepsilon}}A
, for
{\varepsilon}>0
and
N\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}:={\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
; further, for every
\delta>{\varepsilon}
,
{\rm spec}_{N,{\varepsilon}}A\subset{\rm Spec}\,A+\delta{\mathbb{D}}
, for all sufficiently large
N
. These approximation ideas are extended to the general Banach space case in Seidel
[
76
]
.

The Solvability Complexity Index (SCI), that we discuss in §
1.3.5
, §
7
, and §
8.4.2
, is introduced in a specific context in Hansen
[
53
]
, and in the general form that we use it in this paper in
[
1
,
2
]
. Building on the work in
[
53
]
,
Ben-Artzi el al.
[
1
,
2
]
consider the computation of
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, and hence, via (
1.7
),
{\rm Spec}\,A
, in the case when
A\in L(E)
and
E
is a separable Hilbert space. Via an orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}
for
E
, we may identify
E
with
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{N}})
and
A
with its matrix representation
A=[A_{ij}]_{i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, where
A_{ij}:=(Ae_{j},e_{i})
.
On the assumption that only the values
A_{ij}
are available as data, Ben-Artzi el al.
[
1
,
2
]
, building on
[
53
]
, discuss the existence or otherwise of algorithms for computing
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
. In particular, the authors construct in
[
2
]
, for each
{\varepsilon}>0
, the sequence of mappings
(\Gamma_{{\varepsilon},n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, from
L(E)
to
{\mathbb{C}}^{C}
, given by

\Gamma_{{\varepsilon},n}(A):={\mathcal{S}}_{\varepsilon}^{n,2n}(A)\cap\mathrm{Grid}(n),\quad\mbox{where}\quad\mathrm{Grid}(n):=\frac{1}{n}({\mathbb{Z}}+{\rm i}{\mathbb{Z}}).

(1.56)

It is clear, from the above discussion, that
\Gamma_{{\varepsilon},n}(A)
can be computed for each
n
from finitely many of the entries
A_{ij}
in finitely many operations, and it is not difficult, as a consequence of the properties of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\varepsilon}^{n,2n}(A)
noted above, to see (cf. Proposition
1.10
) that

\Gamma_{{\varepsilon},n}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
whenever the matrix
A
is band-dominated. Thus, where
(\epsilon_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is any positive null sequence,
{\rm Spec}\,A
can be computed as the iterated limit

{\rm Spec}\,A=\lim_{m\to\infty}{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}_{m}}A=\lim_{m\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}_{m},n}(A).

(1.57)

In the same paper
[
2
]
(and see
[
23
]
) they show moreover that this double limit is optimal; it cannot be replaced by a single limit. Precisely, they show that there exists no sequence of algorithms
(\gamma_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, from
L(E)
to
{\mathbb{C}}^{C}
, such that
\gamma_{n}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
for every band-dominated matrix
A
and
\gamma_{n}(A)
can be computed, for each
n
, with only finitely many of the matrix entries
A_{ij}
as input. In the language introduced in
[
1
,
2
]
, the SCI of computing the spectrum of a band-dominated operator from its matrix entries is two.

2
Pseudospectra and Globevnik’s property

We have introduced in §
1.3
the definitions of the open and closed
{\varepsilon}
-pseudospectra of
B\in L(Y)
, for a Banach space
Y
. A natural question is: what is the relationship between these two definitions? Clearly, for every
{\varepsilon}>0
,
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B\subset{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B
, so that also
\overline{{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B}\subset{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B
(since
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B
is closed). It is known
[
43
,
44
,
78
]
that equality holds, i.e.,
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B=\overline{{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B}
, if
Y
has
Globevnik’s property
, that is:
Y
is finite-dimensional or
Y
or its dual
Y^{*}
is a complex uniformly convex Banach space (see
[
78
, Definition 2.4 (ii)]
). Hilbert spaces have Globevnik’s property but also
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
has Globevnik’s property if
X
has it
[
33
]
. On the other hand, Banach spaces
Y
and operators
B\in L(Y)
are known
[
78
,
79
,
80
]
such that
\overline{{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B}\subsetneq{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B
.

Let us note a few further properties of pseudospectra that we will utilise. Given a Banach space
Y
,
B\in L(Y)
, and
{\varepsilon}>0
, let

{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}^{0}(B):=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mbox{ there exists }x\in Y\mbox{ with }\|(A-\lambda I)x\|<{\varepsilon}\|x\|\},

(2.1)

and let
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}^{0}B
denote the right hand side of the above equation with the
<
replaced by
\leq
.
Then (this is
[
83
, p.31]
plus (
1.8
)), we have the following useful characterisations of
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B
:

{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B\ =\ \bigcup_{\|T\|<{\varepsilon}}{\rm Spec}\,(B+T)={\rm Spec}\,B\cup{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}^{0}B={\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}^{0}(B)\cup{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}^{0}(B^{*}),\qquad{\varepsilon}>0.

(2.2)

For
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B
we have (see
[
79
]
and (
1.8
)) the weaker statement that

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B\ \supset\ \bigcup_{\|T\|\leq{\varepsilon}}{\rm Spec}\,(B+T)\supset{\rm Spec}\,B\cup{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}^{0}B={\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}^{0}(B)\cup{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}^{0}(B^{*}),\qquad{\varepsilon}>0.

(2.3)

It follows from (
2.2
) and (
2.3
) that, for
{\varepsilon}>0
,

{\rm Spec}\,B+{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{D}}\subset{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B
and
{\rm Spec}\,B+{\varepsilon}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\subset{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B
. We note that equality holds in these inclusions (see, e.g.,
[
30
, p. 247]
) if
Y
is a Hilbert space and
B
is normal. Further, it follows from the first equality in (
2.2
) that, for
B,T\in L(Y)
and
{\varepsilon},\delta>0
,

{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}(B+T)\subset{\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta}B\quad\mbox{if}\quad\|T\|\leq\delta.

(2.4)

If
Y
is finite dimensional or is a Hilbert space the first
\supset
in (
2.3
) can be replaced by
=
(see
[
48
, Theorem 3.27]
and
[
79
]
), so that we have also that

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}(B+T)\subset{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta}B\quad\mbox{if}\quad\|T\|\leq\delta.

(2.5)

But the first
\supset
in (
2.3
) cannot be replaced by
=
for every Banach space
Y
and every
B\in L(Y)
, even if
Y
has Globevnik’s property, as shown by examples of Shargorodsky
[
79
]
. The second
\supset
in (
2.3
) can be replaced by equality if
Y
is finite dimensional, but not, in general, otherwise. As an example relevant to this paper let
A\in L(E)
have the matrix representation (
1.15
) with
\alpha=\gamma=0
and
\beta_{k}=\tanh(k)
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
is a Hilbert space so that the first
\supset
in (
2.3
) is equality. However,
{\rm Spec}\,A=\overline{\{\beta_{k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}}
, so that
\pm 1\in{\rm Spec}\,A\subset[-1,1]
, which implies that
\lambda=1+{\varepsilon}\in{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\setminus{\rm Spec}\,A
. But this
\lambda\not\in{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}^{0}A
, for if
x\in E
with
x\neq 0
then

\|(A-\lambda I)x\|^{2}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(1+{\varepsilon}-\beta_{k})^{2}|x_{k}|^{2}>{\varepsilon}^{2}\|x\|,

(2.6)

since
\beta_{k}<1
for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

We have noted in §
1.3
the simple identity (
1.7
), and the same identity with
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}
replaced by
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}
. Similarly, immediately from the definitions, for every Banach space
Y
and
B\in L(Y)
, generalising (
1.7
), we have that

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B=\bigcap_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>{\varepsilon}}{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}B=\bigcap_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>{\varepsilon}}{\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}B,\qquad{\varepsilon}\geq 0.

(2.7)

Recalling the results on Hausdorff convergence of §
1.3
, this implies, for every positive null sequence
(\eta_{n})
and every
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
, that

{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\eta_{n}}B\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad{\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\eta_{n}}B\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B.

(2.8)

Recalling the discussion of Hausdorff convergence in §
1.3
, if
Y
has Globevnik’s property, so that
\overline{{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B}={\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B
, then we can, if we prefer, write (
2.8
) with
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B
replaced by
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B
.

3
The
\tau
method: principal submatrices

In this section we prove the inclusion (
M

τ

) and compute the optimal value of the
“penalty” term
{\varepsilon}_{n}
. The proof of
(
M

τ

) is so central to this paper that we want to sketch
its main idea, captured in Proposition
3.3
below, beforehand.

Recalling (
2.2
), suppose that
\lambda\in{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A\setminus{\rm Spec}\,A={\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}^{0}A
and
x=(x_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\in E
is a
corresponding pseudomode, i.e.

\|(A-\lambda I)x\|\ <\ {\varepsilon}\,\|x\|.

(3.1)

Consider all finite subvectors of
x
, of some fixed length
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, namely

x_{n,k}\ :=\ (x_{k+1},...,x_{k+n})\in X^{n},\qquad k\in{\mathbb{Z}},

(3.2)

and consider the corresponding principal
submatrices
A_{n,k}
of
A
introduced in (
1.19
). We claim
that, for at least one
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, it holds, for some
{\varepsilon}_{n}>0
to be specified, that

\|(A_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n})x_{n,k}\|\ <\ ({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n})\,\|x_{n,k}\|

(3.3)

and hence

\lambda\in{\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}A_{n,k}
.

One way to show this is to suppose that, conversely, for all

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the opposite of (
3.3
) holds, and then
to take all these (opposite) inequalities and add their squares up
over
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, which, after some computation, contradicts
(
3.1
). Although (
3.3
)
need not hold for every
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, it does hold “in the quadratic
mean” over all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and therefore for at least one
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

This computation reveals that (
3.3
) holds with
{\varepsilon}_{n}=O(n^{-1/2})
as
n\to\infty
; see (
3.19
). However, one can do better: instead of the
“sharp cutoff” (
3.2
), we can introduce weights

w_{1},...,w_{n}>0
and put

x_{n,k}\ :=\ (w_{1}x_{k+1},...,w_{n}x_{k+n})\in X^{n},\qquad k\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

With this modification (
3.3
) holds for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}

but now with
{\varepsilon}_{n}
dependent on the choice of
w_{1},...,w_{n}
. By
varying the weight vector
w=(w_{1},...,w_{n})
we can minimise

{\varepsilon}_{n}
; the minimal
{\varepsilon}_{n}=O(n^{-1})
as
n\to\infty
.

We structure these arguments as follows. In §
3.1
we prove, as Theorem
3.5
, a version of (
M

τ

) with a formula for
{\varepsilon}_{n}
dependent on the choice of
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
. The main idea of the proof, outlined above, is captured in
Proposition
3.3
. In §
3.2
we minimise
{\varepsilon}_{n}
over the choice of the weights
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
; the full proof of (
M

τ

), establishing the minimal formula for
{\varepsilon}_{n}
, is given as Theorem
3.6
. This formula for
{\varepsilon}_{n}
, while amenable to numerical computation, is complex, requiring minimisation of a function of one variable on a finite interval, this function defined implicitly through the solution of a nonlinear equation. Corollary
3.7
states an explicit formula for
{\varepsilon}_{n}
in the case that
A
is bidiagonal, and Corollaries
3.8
and
3.9
provide more explicit upper bounds for
{\varepsilon}_{n}
in the general tridiagonal case. These are of value as discussed in Remark
1.3.2
.

Before continuing with the above plan, let us note properties of the sets
\sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
and
\Sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
, defined by (
1.21
), that appear in (
M

τ

). Clearly, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
(and all tridiagonal
A\in L(E)
),
\sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
is open, for
{\varepsilon}>0
, and
\Sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
closed, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
. We have further that

\overline{\sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)}\subset\Sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A),\;\;{\varepsilon}>0.

(3.4)

The following proposition is a characterisation of
\sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
and
\Sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
that is key to the proof of (
M

τ

). This uses the notations

\widehat{\sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)\ :=\ \{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu_{n}^{\dagger}(A-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}\},\;\;{\varepsilon}>0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\widehat{\Sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)\ :=\ \{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu_{n}^{\dagger}(A-\lambda I)\leq{\varepsilon}\},\;\;{\varepsilon}\geq 0,

(3.5)

for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, where
\mu_{n}^{\dagger}
is defined by (
1.25
). Note that (
1.11
) and (
1.25
) imply that

|\mu_{n}^{\dagger}(A)-\mu_{n}^{\dagger}(B)|\leq\|A-B\|,

so that
\widehat{\sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
is open and
\widehat{\Sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
is closed. Note also that

\widehat{\Sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)\ =\ \bigcap_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>{\varepsilon}}\widehat{\sigma}^{n}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}(A),\qquad{\varepsilon}\geq 0.

(3.6)

Proposition 3.1

For every tridiagonal
A\in L(E)
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, it holds that

\sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)=\widehat{\sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
, for
{\varepsilon}>0
. If
X
has the Globevnik property, then also
\overline{\sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)}=\Sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)=\widehat{\Sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
, for
{\varepsilon}>0
.

Proof.
That
\sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)\ =\ \widehat{\sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
, for
{\varepsilon}>0
, follows easily from (
1.9
), recalling that, for every
{\varepsilon}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the infimum of a set of real numbers is
<{\varepsilon}
if and only if one of the numbers in the set is
<{\varepsilon}
.

Suppose that
\lambda\in\Sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
. Then there exists a sequence
(\lambda_{j})\subset{\mathbb{C}}
and a sequence
(k_{j})\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\lambda_{j}\to\lambda
and
\lambda_{j}\in{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}(A_{n,k_{j}})
, so that
\mu(A_{n,k_{j}}-\lambda_{j}I_{n})\leq{\varepsilon}
. By (
1.11
) it follows that

\mu^{\dagger}_{n}(A-\lambda I)\ =\ \inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\mu(A_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n})\ \leq\ \mu(A_{n,k_{j}}-\lambda I_{n})\leq{\varepsilon}+|\lambda-\lambda_{j}|,

for each
j
, so that
\mu^{\dagger}_{n}(A-\lambda I)\leq{\varepsilon}
and
\lambda\in\widehat{\Sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
. Thus
\Sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)\subset\widehat{\Sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
.

Define
D\in L(E)
by

D:={\rm Diag}\{A_{n,k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
, and note that, for all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
,

\mu(D-\lambda I)\ =\ \inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\mu(A_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n})\ =\ \mu^{\dagger}_{n}(A-\lambda I),

(3.7)

so that, by (
1.9
),
\widehat{\sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)={\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}D
and
\widehat{\Sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)={\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}D
. Noting also (
3.4
), we have shown that
\overline{{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}D}=\overline{\sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)}\subset\Sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)\subset\widehat{\Sigma}^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)={\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}D
.
But if
X
has the Globevnik property, then
\overline{{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}D}={\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}D
, completing the proof.

3.1
Proof of the inclusion (
M

τ

)

We start our proof of (
M

τ

) with a very simple lemma
that proves helpful throughout.

Lemma 3.2

For
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\theta>0
we have

(a+b)^{2}\ \leq\ a^{2}(1+\theta)\,+\,b^{2}(1+\theta^{-1})
,
with equality if and only if
a\theta=b
.

Proof.
Clearly,

(a+b)^{2}\,\leq\,(a+b)^{2}+(a\theta^{\frac{1}{2}}-b\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{2}\,=\,a^{2}(1+\theta)+b^{2}(1+\theta^{-1})

with equality if and only if
a\theta^{\frac{1}{2}}-b\theta^{-\frac{1}{2}}=0
, i.e.

a\theta=b
.

The following results lead to Theorem
3.5
that provides justification of (
M

τ

), initially with a formula for
{\varepsilon}_{n}
that depends on general weights
w_{1},...,w_{n}
.

Proposition 3.3

Let
{\varepsilon}>0
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, suppose that
w_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
j=1,...,n
, with
at least one
w_{j}
non-zero, and that
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal and
\|Ax\|\leq{\varepsilon}
, for some
x\in E
with
\|x\|=1
. Then, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
it holds that

\nu(A_{n,k})\leq{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n},

(3.8)

with

{\varepsilon}_{n}\ :=\ \left\|\alpha\right\|_{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{T_{n}^{-}}{S_{n}}}\ +\ \left\|\gamma\right\|_{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{T_{n}^{+}}{S_{n}}}\,,

(3.9)

where

S_{n}\ :=\ \sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{j}^{2},\qquad T_{n}^{-}\ :=\ \sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(w_{j-1}-w_{j}\right)^{2},\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad T_{n}^{+}\ :=\ \sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(w_{j+1}-w_{j}\right)^{2},

with
w_{0}:=0
and
w_{n+1}:=0
.

Proof.

Let
y=Ax
,
so
\left\|y\right\|\leq{\varepsilon}
. For
j=0,\ldots,n
, let

E_{j}:=|w_{j+1}-w_{j}|.

(3.10)

Note that

\sum_{j=1}^{n}E^{2}_{j}\ =\ T^{+}_{n}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\sum_{j=1}^{n}E_{j-1}^{2}\ =\ T^{-}_{n}.

(3.11)

For
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let

a_{k}:=\left\|A_{n,k}\widetilde{x}_{n,k}\right\|
,
where

\widetilde{x}_{n,k}=(w_{1}x_{k+1},w_{2}x_{k+2},\ldots,w_{n}x_{k+n})^{T}
,
and let

b_{k}:=\left\|\widetilde{x}_{n,k}\right\|
.
We will prove that
a_{k}\leq({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n})b_{k}
for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
b_{k}\neq 0
,
which will show that
\nu(A_{n,k})\leq{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}
.

Note first that, using the notation (
3.10
),

\displaystyle a_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\|w_{j-1}\alpha_{j+k-1}x_{j+k-1}\ +\ w_{j}\beta_{j+k}x_{j+k}\ +\ w_{j+1}\gamma_{j+k+1}x_{j+k+1}\right\|_{X}^{2}

(3.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\|(w_{j-1}-w_{j})\alpha_{j+k-1}x_{j+k-1}\ +\ w_{j}y_{j+k}\ +\ (w_{j+1}-w_{j})\gamma_{j+k+1}x_{j+k+1}\right\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(w_{j}\|y_{j+k}\|_{X}\ +\ E_{j-1}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\|x_{j+k-1}\|_{X}+E_{j}\|\gamma\|_{\infty}\|x_{j+k+1}\|_{X}\right)^{2}.

(3.13)

So, for all
\theta>0
and
\phi>0
, by Lemma
3.2
,

\displaystyle a_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left[(1+\theta)w_{j}^{2}\|y_{j+k}\|_{X}^{2}\ +\ (1+\theta^{-1})\left(E_{j-1}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\|x_{j+k-1}\|_{X}+E_{j}\|\gamma\|_{\infty}\|x_{j+k+1}\|_{X}\right)^{2}\right]

(3.14)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big[(1+\theta)w_{j}^{2}\|y_{j+k}\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle~+\ (1+\theta^{-1})\left((1+\phi)E^{2}_{j-1}\|\alpha\|^{2}_{\infty}\|x_{j+k-1}\|_{X}^{2}+(1+\phi^{-1})E^{2}_{j}\|\gamma\|^{2}_{\infty}\|x_{j+k+1}\|_{X}^{2}\right)\Big].

Thus, and recalling (
3.11
) and that
\|x\|=1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}a_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big[(1+\theta)\|y\|^{2}w_{j}^{2}+(1+\theta^{-1})\left((1+\phi)E^{2}_{j-1}\|\alpha\|^{2}_{\infty}+(1+\phi^{-1})E^{2}_{j}\|\gamma\|^{2}_{\infty}\right)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\theta)\left\|y\right\|^{2}S_{n}\ +\ (1+\theta^{-1})\left[(1+\phi)\left\|\alpha\right\|_{\infty}^{2}T_{n}^{-}+(1+\phi^{-1})\left\|\gamma\right\|_{\infty}^{2}T_{n}^{+}\right].

Similarly,

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{k}^{2}=S_{n}\left\|x\right\|^{2}=S_{n}
. Now, by
Lemma
3.2
and recalling (
3.9
),

\inf_{\phi>0}\left[(1+\phi)\left\|\alpha\right\|_{\infty}^{2}T_{n}^{-}+(1+\phi^{-1})\left\|\gamma\right\|_{\infty}^{2}T^{+}_{n}\right]\ \leq\varepsilon_{n}^{2}S_{n}.

Thus

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}a_{k}^{2}\ \leq\ \Big((1+\theta)\|y\|^{2}\ +\ (1+\theta^{-1}){\varepsilon}_{n}^{2}\Big)\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{k}^{2},

(3.15)

for all
\theta>0
. Applying Lemma
3.2

again, we see that

\inf_{\theta>0}\Big((1+\theta)\|y\|^{2}\ +\ (1+\theta^{-1}){\varepsilon}_{n}^{2}\Big)\ =\ (\|y\|\,+\,{\varepsilon}_{n})^{2}\leq({\varepsilon}\,+\,{\varepsilon}_{n})^{2},

(3.16)

so that

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}a_{k}^{2}\ \leq\ \left({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}\right)^{2}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{k}^{2}.

Thus, either
a_{k}>\left({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}\right)b_{k}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, in which case also
a_{k}<\left({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}\right)b_{k}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, which implies that
b_{k}>0
, or
a_{k}\leq\left({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}\right)b_{k}
, for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, so for some
k
with
b_{k}\neq 0
.

Corollary 3.4

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, suppose that
w_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
j=1,...,n
, with
at least one
w_{j}
non-zero, and that
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal. Then

\inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\nu(A_{n,k})\ \leq\ \nu(A)+{\varepsilon}_{n},

(3.17)

where
{\varepsilon}_{n}
is given by (
3.9
).

Proof.

Let
\eta>0
. By definition of
\nu(A)
there exists
x\in E
with
\|x\|=1
such that
\|Ax\|\leq\nu(A)+\eta
. By Proposition
3.3
,
\nu(A_{n,k})\leq\nu(A)+{\varepsilon}_{n}+\eta
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Since this holds for all
\eta>0
the result follows.

It is not true, for all tridiagonal
A\in L(E)
and
{\varepsilon}>0
, that
\nu(A)\leq{\varepsilon}
implies that
\nu(A_{n,k})\leq{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
(a strengthened version of (
3.17
)). For consider
A-\lambda I
, where
A
is as defined below (
2.4
), so that
X={\mathbb{C}}
, and choose
\lambda=1+{\varepsilon}
. For
x=(x_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\in E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
,
((A-\lambda I)x)_{k}=(\beta_{k}-1-{\varepsilon})x_{k}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
\beta_{k}=\tanh(k)
, so that
\nu(A)\leq 1+{\varepsilon}-\beta_{k}
, for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, i.e.,
\nu(A)\leq{\varepsilon}
. But (cf. (
2.6
))
\nu(A_{n,k})\geq 1+{\varepsilon}-\beta_{k+1}>{\varepsilon}
, for each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Theorem 3.5

Suppose that
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
w_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
j=1,...,n
, with
at least one
w_{j}
non-zero. Then

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \widehat{\Sigma}^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A),\quad{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A),\quad{\varepsilon}>0,

(3.18)

with
{\varepsilon}_{n}
given by (
3.9
), in particular the second inclusion of (
M

τ

) holds with
{\varepsilon}_{n}
given by (
3.9
). If
X
has Globevnik’s property, then also the first inclusion of (
M

τ

) holds with
{\varepsilon}_{n}
given by (
3.9
).

Proof.

To see that the second inclusion in (
3.18
)/(
M

τ

) holds, suppose that
\lambda\in{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
. Then, by (
1.9
) and the definition (
1.8
), either
\nu(A-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}
or
\nu(A^{*}-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}
. By Corollary
3.4
it follows that either
\nu(A_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n})<{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, or
\nu(A^{*}_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n})<{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Thus, recalling the notation (
1.8
),
\mu(A_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n})<{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, so that
\lambda\in{\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}A_{n,k}
by (
1.9
), so that
\lambda\in\sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
. The first inclusion in (
3.18
) follows from the second inclusion by taking intersections, noting (
2.7
), the first sentence of Proposition
3.1
, and (
3.6
). This implies, by the second sentence of Proposition
3.1
, that the first inclusion of (
M

τ

) holds if
X
has the Globevnik property.

If we evaluate (
3.9
) in the case when all weights

w_{1},...,w_{n}
are equal to
1
, we get that
S_{n}=n
and

T_{n}^{-}=T_{n}^{+}=1
, so that

{\varepsilon}_{n}\ =\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\ (\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\,+\,\|\gamma\|_{\infty}),

(3.19)

and hence
{\varepsilon}_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
In the next subsection we shall see that we can achieve much smaller values for
{\varepsilon}_{n}
, smaller by a factor
<2\pi/\sqrt{n}
for large
n
(Corollary
3.8
), by minimising
{\varepsilon}_{n}
as a function of the weight vector
w
.

3.2
Minimising
{\varepsilon}_{n}
via variation of the weight vector

We will now minimise the “penalty term”
{\varepsilon}_{n}
as a function,
(
3.9
), of the weight vector
w=(w_{1},...,w_{n})^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. That is, we will compute

\inf_{w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\,w\neq 0}{\varepsilon}_{n}=\inf_{w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\,\|w\|=1}{\varepsilon}_{n},

the equality of these two expressions following easily from (
3.9
). It follows from the compactness of the set
\{w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}:\,\|w\|=1\}
that this infimum is achieved by some weight vector
w=(w_{1},...,w_{n})^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, so that the infimum is a minimum. This is important because it implies that Theorem
3.5
applies with this minimal weight vector and
{\varepsilon}_{n}
taking its minimal value.

Let us abbreviate
\|\alpha\|_{\infty}=:r

and
\|\gamma\|_{\infty}=:s
, so that

{\varepsilon}_{n}^{2}\ =\ \left(r\sqrt{\frac{T_{n}^{-}}{S_{n}}}\ +\ s\sqrt{\frac{T_{n}^{+}}{S_{n}}}\right)^{2}\ =\ \inf_{\tau>0}\left(r^{2}(1+\tau)\frac{T_{n}^{-}}{S_{n}}+s^{2}(1+\tau^{-1})\frac{T_{n}^{+}}{S_{n}}\right),

(3.20)

by (
3.9
) and Lemma
3.2
. (This step (
3.20
) may appear to be introducing additional complexity, but we shall see that it helpfully reduces a large part of the minimisation to an eigenvalue computation.) Now put

B_{n}\ :=\ \left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}1&&&&\\
-1&1&&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&&\\
&&-1&1&\\
&&&-1&1\end{array}\right)\ \in\ {\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}.

Then

T_{n}^{-}\ =\ \|B_{n}w\|^{2}\ =\ (B_{n}w)^{T}(B_{n}w)\ =\ w^{T}B_{n}^{T}B_{n}w,

while

T_{n}^{+}\ =\ \|B_{n}^{T}w\|^{2}\ =\ (B_{n}^{T}w)^{T}(B_{n}^{T}w)\ =\ w^{T}B_{n}B_{n}^{T}w

and

S_{n}\ =\ \|w\|^{2}\ =\ w^{T}w
.
So

r^{2}(1+\tau)\frac{T_{n}^{-}}{S_{n}}+s^{2}(1+\tau^{-1})\frac{T_{n}^{+}}{S_{n}}\ =\ \frac{w^{T}D_{n}w}{w^{T}w}

(3.21)

with

D_{n}\ :=\ r^{2}(1+\tau)B_{n}^{T}B_{n}+s^{2}(1+\tau^{-1})B_{n}B_{n}^{T}.

It
is easy to see that
D_{n}
is symmetric, real and positive
definite. In fact,

w^{T}D_{n}w\ \geq\ \left[r^{2}(1+\tau)+s^{2}(1+\tau^{-1})\right]\lambda_{1},

where
\lambda_{1}>0
is the smallest eigenvalue of
B_{n}^{T}B_{n}

and
B_{n}B_{n}^{T}
. Our aim is to compute the sharpest lower bound on
(
3.21
), i.e. the smallest eigenvalue of
D_{n}
, since

\inf_{w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\,w\neq 0}{\varepsilon}^{2}_{n}=\inf_{\tau>0}\inf_{w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\,w\neq 0}\frac{w^{T}D_{n}w}{w^{T}w}.

(3.22)

To compute the infimum (
3.22
),
define
E_{n}(\phi)
, for
0\leq\phi\leq 1
, to be the real, symmetric, positive definite matrix

E_{n}(\phi)\ :=\ \phi B_{n}^{T}B_{n}+(1-\phi)B_{n}B_{n}^{T},

so that
E_{1}(\phi)=1
while, for
n=2,3,\dots
,

E_{n}(\phi)=\begin{pmatrix}1+\phi&-1&&&\\
-1&2&-1&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&-1&2&-1\\
&&&-1&2-\phi\end{pmatrix}\ \in\ {\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}.

Let
\varrho_{n}(\phi):=\lambda_{\sf min}\left(E_{n}(\phi)\right)>0
be
the smallest eigenvalue of
E_{n}(\phi)
.
Then

D_{n}\ =\ \left(r^{2}(1+\tau)+s^{2}(1+\tau^{-1})\right)E_{n}\left(\frac{r^{2}(1+\tau)}{r^{2}(1+\tau)+s^{2}(1+\tau^{-1})}\right),

so that from (
3.22
) it follows that

\inf_{w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\,w\neq 0}{\varepsilon}^{2}_{n}=\inf_{\tau>0}\left(\left(r^{2}(1+\tau)+s^{2}(1+\tau^{-1})\right)\varrho_{n}\left(\frac{r^{2}(1+\tau)}{r^{2}(1+\tau)+s^{2}(1+\tau^{-1})}\right)\right).

(3.23)

Further, if
\phi:=\frac{r^{2}\left(1+\tau\right)}{r^{2}\left(1+\tau\right)+s^{2}\left(1+\tau^{-1}\right)}
, it follows that

\tau=\frac{s^{2}\phi}{r^{2}(1-\phi)}
and that
\{\phi:\tau>0\}=(0,1)
. Thus (
3.23
) can be rewritten as

\inf_{w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\,w\neq 0}{\varepsilon}^{2}_{n}\ =\ \inf_{0<\phi<1}\left[\left(\frac{r^{2}}{\phi}+\frac{s^{2}}{1-\phi}\right)\varrho_{n}(\phi)\right].

(3.24)

The right hand side of equation (
3.24
) already solves our minimisation problem. But we calculate now a more explicit expression for
\varrho_{n}(\phi)
, which will make (
3.24
) more easily computable.

Clearly,
\varrho_{1}(\phi)=1
. If
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
E_{n}(\phi)
for
n\geq 2
then, since
E_{n}(\phi)
is positive definite, and applying the Gershgorin circle theorem, we see that
0<\lambda\leq 4
. It is rather straightforward to show that 4 is not an eigenvalue so that
0<\lambda<4
. But, in any case, for
n\geq 2
,
\lambda<4
is an eigenvalue of
E_{n}(\phi)
, with eigenvector
v=(v_{n},\ldots,v_{1})^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, if and only if

\lambda=2-2\cos\theta=4\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2},\quad\mbox{ for some }\theta\in(0,\pi),

(3.25)

and

\displaystyle\left[(1+\phi)-\lambda\right]v_{n}-v_{n-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(3.26)

\displaystyle-v_{j+1}+(2-\lambda)v_{j}-v_{j-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,\qquad\textrm{ for }j=2,\ldots,n-1,

(3.27)

\displaystyle-v_{2}+\left[(2-\phi)-\lambda\right]v_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(3.28)

Equation (
3.27
) holds if and only if
v_{j}
is a linear combination of
\cos((j-1)\theta)
and
\sin((j-1)\theta)
. To within multiplication by a constant, the linear combination that also satisfies (
3.28
) is

\displaystyle v_{j}=\sin\theta\cos\left[(j-1)\theta\right]+\left(\cos\theta-\phi\right)\sin\left[(j-1)\theta\right]=\sin(j\theta)-\phi\sin\left((j-1)\theta\right),\;j=1,\ldots,n.\;

(3.29)

Substituting in
(
3.26
), we see that
\lambda<4
is an eigenvalue of

E_{n}(\phi)
for
n\geq 2
if and only if (
3.25
) holds and
F(\theta)=0
, where

F(t)\ :=\ (2\cos t-1)\sin(nt)-\sin((n-1)t)\,+\,\phi(1-\phi)\sin((n-1)t),\qquad t\geq 0.

Now, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\displaystyle F(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sin(nt)+\sin\left(\left(n+1\right)t\right)+\phi(1-\phi)\sin\left(\left(n-1\right)t\right)

(3.30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle 2\sin\frac{t}{2}\,\cos\left(\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)t\right)+\phi(1-\phi)\sin\left(\left(n-1\right)t\right).

(3.31)

Thus, noting that
\phi(1-\phi)\leq 1/4
and setting
\sigma:=\pi/(n+2)\leq\pi/3
, so that
1/2\leq\cos\sigma<1
, we see from (
3.30
) that

\displaystyle\textstyle F(\sigma)\leq\textstyle-\sin 2\sigma+\sin\sigma+\frac{1}{4}\sin 3\sigma=\textstyle\frac{1}{4}\sin\sigma(3+4\cos^{2}\sigma-8\cos\sigma)\leq 0.

Moreover, from (
3.31
), while
F(0)=0
,

\textstyle F(t)>\phi(1-\phi)\sin((n-1)t)\ \geq\ 0,

for
0<t<\frac{\pi}{2n+1}
.
Let
\theta_{n}(\phi)
denote the smallest positive solution of the equation

F(t)=0
. Then we have shown that

\theta_{n}(\phi)\in[\frac{\pi}{2n+1},\frac{\pi}{n+2}]\subset(0,\pi)
, and hence that the smallest eigenvalue of
E_{n}(\phi)
is in
(0,4)
, precisely

\varrho_{n}(\phi)\ =\ \lambda_{\sf min}(E_{n}(\phi))\ =\ 4\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\theta_{n}(\phi)}{2}\right).

(3.32)

(We have shown this equation for
n\geq 2
, and it holds also for
n=1
since
\varrho_{1}(\phi)=1
and
\theta_{1}(\phi)=\pi/3
.)
Further,
\theta_{n}(\phi)
is the unique solution of
F(t)=0
in
[\frac{\pi}{2n+1},\frac{\pi}{n+2}]
. To see this in the case
n\geq 2
,
note that, defining
\eta:=\phi(1-\phi)\in[0,1/4]
, it follows from (
3.31
) that

\displaystyle\textstyle F^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle\cos\frac{t}{2}\cos\left(\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)t\right)-(2n+1)\sin\frac{t}{2}\sin\left(\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)t\right)+\eta(n-1)\cos((n-1)t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle\cos\left(\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)t\right){\mathcal{A}}_{n}(t)-\sin\left(\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)t\right){\mathcal{B}}_{n}(t),

where
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}(t):=\cos\frac{t}{2}+\eta(n-1)\cos\frac{3t}{2}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}(t):=(2n+1)\sin\frac{t}{2}-\eta(n-1)\sin\frac{3t}{2}
.
Now, for

n\geq 2
and
t\in(\frac{\pi}{2n+1},\frac{\pi}{n+2})
it holds that
\cos\left(\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)t\right)<0
,
\sin\left(\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)t\right)>0
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}(t)>0
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}(t)>0
(the last inequality holding since
\eta(n-1)\sin\frac{3t}{2}\leq 3\eta(n-1)\sin\frac{t}{2}\leq\frac{3}{4}(n-1)\sin\frac{t}{2}
). Thus
F^{\prime}(t)<0
for
t\in(\frac{\pi}{2n+1},\frac{\pi}{n+2})
, which implies that
F
has at most one zero in
[\frac{\pi}{2n+1},\frac{\pi}{n+2}]
.

So we have shown that
\theta_{n}(\phi)
is the unique solution of
F(t)=0
in
[\frac{\pi}{2n+1},\frac{\pi}{n+2}]
.
Combining this with
(
3.24
) and (
3.32
), we see that we have completed our
aim of minimising
{\varepsilon}^{2}_{n}
, proving the following
corollary of Theorem
3.5
.

Theorem 3.6

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
the second inclusion in (
M

τ

) holds with

{\varepsilon}_{n}\ :=\ 2\inf_{0<\phi<1}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\|\alpha\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\phi}+\frac{\|\gamma\|_{\infty}^{2}}{1-\phi}}\ \sin\frac{\theta_{n}(\phi)}{2}\right),

(3.33)

where
\theta_{n}(\phi)
is the unique solution in the range

\left[\frac{\pi}{2n+1}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{n+2}\right]

of the equation
F(t)=0
, i.e. of

2\sin\frac{t}{2}\cos\left(\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)t\right)\ +\ \phi(1-\phi)\sin\left(\left(n-1\right)t\right)\ =\ 0.

(3.34)

If
X
has the Globevnik property, then also the first inclusion in (
M

τ

) holds with
{\varepsilon}_{n}
given by (
3.33
).

The following properties of
\theta_{n}
in Theorem
3.6
are
worth noting:

\displaystyle\theta_{1}(\phi)=\frac{\pi}{3},

\displaystyle\quad 0\leq\phi\leq 1;

(3.35)

\displaystyle\theta_{n}(\phi)=\theta_{n}(1-\phi),

\displaystyle\quad 0\leq\phi\leq 1,\;n\in{\mathbb{N}};

(3.36)

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2n+1}=\theta_{n}(0)=\theta_{n}(1)\leq\theta_{n}(\phi)\leq\theta_{n}\left({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\quad 0\leq\phi\leq 1,\;n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(3.37)

These properties are evident from the definition of
\theta_{n}
, except for the bound
\theta_{n}(\phi)\leq\theta_{n}(\frac{1}{2})
. One way to see this bound is via
(
3.32
), as it follows from
E_{n}(\frac{1}{2})=\frac{1}{2}(E_{n}(\phi)+E_{n}(1-\phi))
that

\textstyle\varrho_{n}(\frac{1}{2})\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\min_{\|w\|=1}\left(\phi w^{T}E_{n}(\phi)w+w^{T}E_{n}(1-\phi)w\right)\,\geq\,\frac{1}{2}\left[\varrho_{n}(\phi)+\varrho_{n}((1-\phi))\right]\,=\,\varrho_{n}(\phi),

since
\varrho_{n}(\phi)=\varrho_{n}(1-\phi)
as a consequence of (
3.36
) and (
3.32
).

We remark also that
\theta_{n}(\frac{1}{2})
is the unique solution
of the equation

G(t):=\textstyle 2\cos\left(\left(\frac{n+1}{2}\right)t\right)-\cos\left(\left(\frac{n-1}{2}\right)t\right)\ =\ 0

(3.38)

in the interval
(\frac{\pi}{n+3},\frac{\pi}{n+2}]
. To see this
claim it is enough to check that (
3.38
) and (
3.34
)
have the same solutions in
(0,\frac{\pi}{n+2}]
when

\phi=\frac{1}{2}
, and to show that
\theta_{n}(\frac{1}{2})>\frac{\pi}{n+3}
. To see the first statement multiply both sides of
(
3.38
) by the positive term

2\sin\frac{(n+1)t}{2}-\sin\frac{(n-1)t}{2}
, giving

\textstyle 2\sin\left((n+1)t\right)-2\sin(nt)+\frac{1}{2}\sin\left((n-1)t\right)\ =\ 0.

This is equivalent to (
3.38
) with
\phi=\frac{1}{2}
, so that

\theta_{n}(\frac{1}{2})
is the unique solution of
G(t)=0
in

(0,\frac{\pi}{n+2}]
. To see the second statement, observe that

\textstyle G(\frac{\pi}{n+3})\,=\,2\sin\frac{\pi}{n+3}-\sin\frac{2\pi}{n+3}\ >\ 0,

whereas, where
\sigma=\frac{\pi}{n+2}
,

\textstyle G(\sigma)\,=\,2\sin\frac{\sigma}{2}-\sin\frac{3\sigma}{2}\,=\,\sin\frac{\sigma}{2}\left(4\sin^{2}\frac{\sigma}{2}-1\right)\leq\ 0,

since
0<\frac{\sigma}{2}\leq\frac{\pi}{6}
. Thus

\theta_{n}(\frac{1}{2})>\frac{\pi}{n+3}
.

In general, it appears not possible to explicitly compute the value
of
\phi
for which the bracket in (
3.33
) is minimised.
But it follows easily from the bound (
3.37
) that, in the case

r=\|\alpha\|_{\infty}=0
the infimum is attained by setting

\phi=0
, and this infimum is
{\varepsilon}_{n}\ =\ 2\|\gamma\|_{\infty}\sin\frac{\pi}{4n+2}
. Likewise, when
s=\|\gamma\|_{\infty}=0
we see that the infimum is attained by
setting
\phi=1
, and this infimum is
{\varepsilon}_{n}\ =\ 2\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\sin\frac{\pi}{4n+2}
. Thus we have computed the
mimimal value of
{\varepsilon}_{n}
completely explicitly in the case when
A

is bidiagonal, giving the following corollary.

Corollary 3.7

If
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\gamma=0
or
\alpha=0
, then
(
3.33
) reduces to

{\varepsilon}_{n}\ =\ 2\left(\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{4n+2}\right).

(3.39)

The example of the shift operator (see Example
1.4
and
§
8.1
below) shows that the value of
{\varepsilon}_{n}
in
Corollary
3.7
is the best possible, in
the sense that, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a bidiagonal
A\in L(E)
for
which (
M

τ

) is not true for any smaller value for

{\varepsilon}_{n}
.

In the case that
r=\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\neq 0
and
s=\|\gamma\|_{\infty}\neq 0
it is not clear what the infimum in
(
3.33
) is explicitly when
n\geq 2
. However, since

\theta_{n}(\phi)
depends continuously on
\phi
and is bounded as a
function of
\phi
on
(0,1)
, it is clear that the infimum is
attained at some
\phi\in(0,1)
. Further, as a consequence of
(
3.36
), it is easy to see that the infimum is attained
for some
\phi
in the range
(0,\frac{1}{2})
if
r<s
, for some

\phi
in the range
(\frac{1}{2},1)
if
r>s
. A simple choice of

\phi
which has these properties and which reduces to the optimal
values of
\phi
,
\phi=0
and
\phi=1
, respectively, when
r=0

and
s=0
, is

\phi=\frac{r}{r+s}=\frac{\|\alpha\|_{\infty}}{\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty}}
.
This choice is further motivated by the fact that this is the unique

\phi
that attains the infimum in (
3.33
) in the case

n=1
(which is an easy calculation in view of (
3.35
)).
If we evaluate the bracket in (
3.33
) for this choice
we obtain the following corollary which includes Corollary
3.7
as a special case.

Corollary 3.8

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\varepsilon}_{n}
given by (
3.33
) satisfies

{\varepsilon}_{n}\leq\eta_{n}:=2\left(\left\|\alpha\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\gamma\right\|_{\infty}\right)\sin\frac{\theta}{2},

where
\theta
is the unique solution in the range

\left[\frac{\pi}{2n+1}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{n+2}\right]

of the equation

2\sin\frac{t}{2}\cos\left(\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)t\right)\ +\ \frac{\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\|\gamma\|_{\infty}}{(\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})^{2}}\sin\left(\left(n-1\right)t\right)\ =\ 0.

In particular,
\eta_{1}={\varepsilon}_{1}=\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma_{\infty}\|_{\infty}
.

The following even more explicit result is obtained if we evaluate
the bracket in (
3.33
) for
\phi=\frac{1}{2}
, noting the
equivalent characterisation of
\theta_{n}(\frac{1}{2})
above, that
it is the unique solution in

\left(\frac{\pi}{n+3}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{n+2}\right]
of
(
3.38
).

Corollary 3.9

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\varepsilon}_{n}
given by (
3.33
) satisfies

{\varepsilon}_{n}\ \leq\ \eta^{\prime}_{n}\ :=\ 2\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\left\|\alpha\right\|^{2}_{\infty}+\left\|\gamma\right\|^{2}_{\infty}}\sin\frac{\theta}{2},

where
\theta
is the unique solution in the range

\left(\frac{\pi}{n+3}\,,\,\frac{\pi}{n+2}\right]
of (
3.38
).

Note that Corollaries
3.8
and
3.9
give the same upper bound for
{\varepsilon}_{n}
when
\|\alpha\|_{\infty}=\|\gamma\|_{\infty}
, but when

\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\neq\|\gamma\|_{\infty}
, as a consequence of
(
3.37
), Corollary
3.8
is sharper (
\eta_{n}<\eta^{\prime}_{n}
).

4
The
\pi
method: periodised principal submatrices

In this section we prove the inclusion (
M

π

) that is the
equivalent of (
M

τ

) but with periodised submatrices
(
1.20
) instead of (
1.19
).
We start with Proposition
4.1
, the equivalent of Proposition
3.3
for the
\pi
method. The matrix referenced in this proposition is a generalisation of the
\pi
-method matrix
A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}
of (
1.20
), defined, for any
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}},L(X))
, by

A_{n,k}^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}\ :=\ A_{n,k}+B_{n,k}^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}},\qquad k\in{\mathbb{Z}},\;\;n\in{\mathbb{N}},

where
B_{n,k}^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}
is the
n\times n
matrix whose entry in row
i
, column
j
is
\delta_{i,1}\delta_{j,n}\mathfrak{a}_{k}+\delta_{i,n}\delta_{j,1}\mathfrak{c}_{k}
. Thus, for
n\geq 3
,

A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k}
takes the form (
1.20
), but with the top right entry
t\alpha_{k}
replaced by
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
, and the bottom left entry
\bar{t}\gamma_{k+n+1}
replaced by
\mathfrak{c}_{k}
. Importantly,

A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}=A_{n,k}^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}\qquad\mbox{if}\qquad\mathfrak{a}_{k}:=t\alpha_{k}\mbox{ and }\mathfrak{c}_{k}:=\bar{t}\gamma_{k+n+1},\quad k\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

(4.1)

(Of course, also
A_{n,k}^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}=A_{n,k}
, if
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{c}=0
.)

Proposition 4.1

Let
{\varepsilon}>0
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, suppose that
w_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
j=1,...,n
, with
at least one
w_{j}
non-zero, that
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}},L(X))
, with
\|\mathfrak{a}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\alpha\|_{\infty}
and
\|\mathfrak{c}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\gamma\|_{\infty}
, and that
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal and
\|Ax\|\leq{\varepsilon}
, for some
x\in E
with
\|x\|=1
. Then, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
it holds that

\nu(A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k})\leq{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n},

(4.2)

with

{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}\ :=\ \left(\left\|\alpha\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\gamma\right\|_{\infty}\right)\sqrt{\frac{T_{n}}{S_{n}}},

(4.3)

where

S_{n}\ =\ \sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{j}^{2}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad T_{n}\ =\ (w_{1}+w_{n})^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(w_{j+1}-w_{j})^{2},

with
w_{0}:=0
and
w_{n+1}:=0
.

Proof.

Let
y=Ax
,
so
\left\|y\right\|\leq{\varepsilon}
. We use the notation
E_{j}
, for
j=0,\ldots,n
, defined in (
3.10
)
and put

a_{k}:=\|A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k}\tilde{x}_{n,k}\|
and
b_{k}:=\|\tilde{x}_{n,k}\|
, for

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, where

\tilde{x}_{n,k}:=(w_{1}x_{k+1},w_{2}x_{k+2},\ldots,w_{n}x_{k+n})^{T}
.
As in the proof of Proposition
3.3
, we have that
\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{k}^{2}=S_{n}
. Further, for
n\geq 2
and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, using (
3.10
) and the Kronecker delta notation,

\displaystyle a_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|w_{1}y_{k+1}+w_{n}\mathfrak{a}_{k}x_{k+n}-w_{1}\alpha_{k}x_{k}+(w_{2}-w_{1})\gamma_{k+2}x_{k+2}\right\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=2}^{n-1}\left\|w_{j}y_{k+j}+(w_{j-1}-w_{j})\alpha_{k+j-1}x_{k+j-1}+(w_{j+1}-w_{j})\gamma_{k+j+1}x_{k+j+1}\right\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|w_{n}y_{k+n}+(w_{n-1}-w_{n})\alpha_{k+n-1}x_{k+n-1}+w_{1}\mathfrak{c}_{k}x_{k+1}-w_{n}\gamma_{k+n+1}x_{k+n+1}\right\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(w_{j}\left\|y_{k+j}\right\|_{X}+\|\alpha\|_{\infty}C_{jk}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty}D_{jk}\right)^{2},

(cf. (
3.13
)) where, for
j=1,\ldots,n
and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

C_{jk}:=\delta_{j,1}w_{n}\|x_{k+n}\|_{X}+E_{j-1}\|x_{k+j-1}\|_{X}\quad\mbox{and}\quad D_{jk}:=\delta_{j,n}w_{1}\|x_{k+1}\|_{X}+E_{j}\|x_{k+j+1}\|_{X}.

(4.4)

After applying Lemma
3.2
twice, we get (cf. (
3.14
)) that,
for all
\theta>0
and
\phi>0
,

\displaystyle a_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left[(1+\theta)w^{2}_{j}\left\|y_{k+j}\right\|_{X}^{2}+(1+\theta^{-1})\left((1+\phi)\|\alpha\|^{2}_{\infty}C_{jk}^{2}+(1+\phi^{-1})\|\gamma\|^{2}_{\infty}D_{jk}^{2}\right)\right].

Now,
recalling that
\|x\|=1
,

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}C_{1,k}^{2}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\left(w_{n}\|x_{k+n}\|_{X}+w_{1}\|x_{k}\|_{X}\right)^{2}\leq w_{n}^{2}+2w_{1}w_{n}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\left\|x_{k+n}x_{k}\right\|_{X}+w_{1}^{2}\leq(w_{1}+w_{n})^{2},

so that

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}C_{jk}^{2}\leq(w_{1}+w_{n})^{2}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}E_{j-1}^{2}|x_{k+j-1}|^{2}=T_{n}.

Similarly,

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}D_{n,k}^{2}\leq(w_{1}+w_{n})^{2}\quad\mbox{so that}\quad\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}D_{jk}^{2}\leq T_{n}.

Thus, applying Lemma
3.2
(cf. (
3.15
) and (
3.16
)),

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}a_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\inf_{\theta,\phi>0}\left[(1+\theta)S_{n}\|y\|^{2}+(1+\theta^{-1})T_{n}\left[(1+\phi)\|\alpha\|_{\infty}^{2}+(1+\phi^{-1})\|\gamma\|_{\infty}^{2}\right]\right]

(4.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\theta>0}\left[(1+\theta)\|y\|^{2}S_{n}+(1+\theta^{-1})T_{n}\left(\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\inf_{\theta>0}\left[(1+\theta){\varepsilon}^{2}+(1+\theta^{-1})\left((\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})\sqrt{\frac{T_{n}}{S_{n}}}\right)^{2}\right]S_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\theta>0}\left[\left((1+\theta){\varepsilon}^{2}+(1+\theta^{-1}){{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{2}\right)\right]\ S_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}\right)^{2}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{k}^{2}.

(4.6)

Arguing as at the end of Proposition
3.3
we deduce that

a_{k}\leq({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n})b_{k}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
b_{k}\neq 0
.
We reach the same conclusion, via the same bounds (
4.5
) and (
4.6
), also in the case
n=1
when
S_{n}=w_{1}^{2}
,
T_{n}=4w_{1}^{2}
, and

a_{k}^{2}=w_{1}^{2}\left\|y_{k+1}+\mathfrak{a}_{k}x_{k+1}-\alpha_{k}x_{k}+\mathfrak{c}_{k}x_{k+1}-\gamma_{k+2}x_{k+2}\right\|_{X}^{2},\qquad k\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

Thus
\nu(A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k})\leq{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
.

This is the
\pi
version of Corollary
3.4
, which has an identical proof.

Corollary 4.2

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, suppose that
w_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
j=1,...,n
, with
at least one
w_{j}
non-zero, that
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}},L(X))
, with
\|\mathfrak{a}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\alpha\|_{\infty}
and
\|\mathfrak{c}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\gamma\|_{\infty}
, and that
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal. Then

\inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\nu(A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k})\leq\nu(A)+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n},

(4.7)

where
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
is given by (
4.3
).

To state the main theorem of this section we introduce a generalisation of the notation (
1.23
). For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}},L(X))
, let

\pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ :=\ \bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k},\quad{\varepsilon}>0,\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\Pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ :=\ \overline{\bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k}},\quad{\varepsilon}\geq 0.

(4.8)

Clearly, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
,

\pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ =\ \pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A),\quad{\varepsilon}>0,\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ =\ \Pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A),\quad{\varepsilon}\geq 0,

(4.9)

if
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{c}
are given by (
4.1
). Clearly, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}},L(X))
,
\pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)
is open and
\Pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)
is closed. Further, defining

\mu^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n}(B)\ :=\ \inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\mu(A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k})=\inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,\min\Big(\nu\left(A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k}\right),\,\nu\left((A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k})^{*}\right)\Big),

(4.10)

it holds, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}},L(X))
, by arguing as in the proof of Proposition
3.1
, that

\pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ =\ \{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n}(A-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}\},\qquad{\varepsilon}>0,

(4.11)

and that, if
X
has the Globevnik property, also

\overline{\pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)}\ =\ \Pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ =\ \{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n}(A-\lambda I)\leq{\varepsilon}\},\qquad{\varepsilon}>0.

(4.12)

Thus, if
X
has the Globevnik property,

\Pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ =\ \bigcap_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>{\varepsilon}}\pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}(A),\qquad{\varepsilon}>0.

(4.13)

In relation to the definition (
4.10
), note that

(A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k})^{*}=(A^{*})^{\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{a}}_{n,k}.

(4.14)

Theorem 4.3

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and suppose that
w_{j}\geq 0
, for
j=1,\ldots,n
, with at least one
w_{j}
non-zero, and that
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}},L(X))
, with
\|\mathfrak{a}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\alpha\|_{\infty}
and
\|\mathfrak{c}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\gamma\|_{\infty}
,
Then, where
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
is given by (
4.3
),

{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A),\quad{\varepsilon}>0.\qquad\textrm{Moreover,}\qquad{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\ \Pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A),\quad{\varepsilon}\geq 0,

(4.15)

provided
X
has the Globevnik property.

Proof.

To see that the first inclusion in (
4.15
) holds, suppose that
\lambda\in{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
. Then, by (
2.2
), either
\nu(A-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}
or
\nu(A^{*}-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}
. By applications of Corollary
4.2
, it follows, in the case
\nu(A-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}
, that
\nu(A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n})<{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and, in the case
\nu(A^{*}-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}
, recalling (
4.14
), that
\nu((A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k})^{*}-\lambda I_{n})=\nu((A^{*})^{\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{a}}_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n})<{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Thus, recalling the notation (
1.8
),
\mu(A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n})<{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, so that
\lambda\in{\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}A^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{n,k}
by (
1.9
), so that
\lambda\in\pi^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
. The second inclusion in (
4.15
) follows from the first inclusion by taking intersections, noting (
2.7
) and (
4.13
).

In particular, Theorem
4.3
holds with
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{c}
given by (
4.1
), giving the following corollary.

Corollary 4.4

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and suppose that
w_{j}\geq 0
, for
j=1,\ldots,n
, with at least one
w_{j}
non-zero, and that
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
.
Then the second inclusion of (
M

π

) holds with
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
given by (
4.3
). If
X
has the Globevnik property, then also the first inclusion of (
M

π

) holds with
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
given by (
4.3
).

As we did above for the
\tau
method, we will now minimize the penalty term
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
as a
function, (
4.3
), of the weight vector

w=(w_{1},...,w_{n})^{T}
. In the cases
n=1
and
2
it is easy to see that
{\varepsilon}_{n}
is minimized by taking
w_{1}=\cdots=w_{n}=1
, giving
{\varepsilon}_{1}=2(\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})
and
{\varepsilon}_{2}=\sqrt{2}(\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})
. For
n\geq 3
, from the definitions of
S_{n}
and
T_{n}
in
Theorem
4.4
, we know that

S_{n}=\|w\|_{2}^{2}
and
T_{n}=\|B_{n}w\|_{2}^{2}
, where

B_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1&&&\\
&1&-1&&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&1&-1\\
1&&&&1\end{pmatrix}\qquad\textrm{so that}\qquad B_{n}^{T}B_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&&&1\\
-1&2&-1&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&-1&2&-1\\
1&&&-1&2\end{pmatrix}.

Let us seek

\inf_{w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\,w\neq 0}\sqrt{\frac{T_{n}}{S_{n}}}\ =\ \inf_{w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\,w\neq 0}\sqrt{\frac{\|B_{n}w\|_{2}^{2}}{\|w\|_{2}^{2}}}\ =\ \min_{w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\,\|w\|=1}\sqrt{\frac{w^{T}B_{n}^{T}B_{n}w}{w^{T}w}}\ =\ \sqrt{\lambda_{\sf min}(B^{T}_{n}B_{n})},

(4.16)

noting that a vector
w
achieves this minimum if and only if it is an eigenvector of
B_{n}^{T}B_{n}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{\sf min}(B^{T}_{n}B_{n})
. It is clear that this minimum is strictly positive and that (taking
w_{1}=w_{2}=\cdots=w_{n}
) it is no larger than
2/\sqrt{n}
. Further,
\lambda\in(0,4)
is an eigenvalue of
B_{n}^{T}B_{n}
with
eigenvector
v=(v_{1},...,v_{n})^{T}
if and only if

\lambda\ =\ 2(1-\cos\theta)\ =\ 4\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2},\quad\mbox{for some }\theta\in(0,\pi),

(4.17)

and

\displaystyle v_{n}+(2-\lambda)v_{1}-v_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(4.18)

\displaystyle-v_{i+1}+(2-\lambda)v_{i}-v_{i-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,\qquad\textrm{ for }i=2,\ldots,n-1,

(4.19)

\displaystyle-v_{n-1}+(2-\lambda)v_{n}+v_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(4.20)

Note that if
(\lambda,v)
satisfies (
4.18
)-(
4.20
) then so does
(\lambda,\tilde{v})
, where
\tilde{v}:=(v_{n},\ldots,v_{1})
. Further,
(\lambda,v^{\dagger})
, where
v^{\dagger}:=v+\tilde{v}
satisfies
v^{\dagger}_{j}=v^{\dagger}_{n+1-j}
,
j=1,...,n
. Thus, to compute
\lambda
and a corresponding eigenvector
v
from (
4.18
)-(
4.20
), we may assume that

v_{j}=v_{n+1-j},\quad j=1,...,n.

(4.21)

As discussed around (
3.27
), (
4.19
) holds if and only if
v_{j}
is a linear combination of
\cos((j-1)\theta)
and
\sin((j-1)\theta)
. To within multiplication by a constant, the linear combination that satisfies (
4.21
) is

v_{j}\ =\ \sin((j-1)\theta)+\sin((n-j)\theta),\qquad j=1,2,\ldots,n,

(4.22)

and this satisfies (
4.18
) and (
4.20
) if and only if

F_{\pi}(\theta)=0
where, for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\displaystyle F_{\pi}(t)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sin((n-1)t)+2\cos(t)\sin((n-1)t)-\sin(t)-\sin((n-2)t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sin((n-1)t)+\sin(nt)-\sin(t)=2\sin((n-2)t/2)\cos(nt/2)+\sin(nt).

From this last expression for
F_{\pi}(t)
it is clear that
F_{\pi}(\pi/n)=0
and that
F_{\pi}(t)>0
for
0<t<\pi/n
. Thus
\lambda_{\sf min}(B^{T}_{n}B_{n})
is given by (
4.17
) with
\theta=\frac{\pi}{n}
. Since the corresponding eigenvector given by (
4.22
) satisfies
v_{j}>0
,
j=1,\ldots,n
, the following corollary follows from
Theorem
4.4
and
(
4.16
) and the observations above about the cases
n=1
and
2
.

Corollary 4.5

The conclusions of Theorem
4.3
hold with

{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}\ :=\ 2(\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})\sin\frac{\pi}{2n}.

(4.23)

In particular, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the second inclusion of (
M

π

)
holds with
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
given by (
4.23
), as does the first inclusion of (
M

π

) if
X
has Globevnik’s property.

Again, as in Corollary
3.7
, the example
of the shift operator (see Example
1.4
and §
8.1
below) shows that this value of
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
is the best
possible.

5
The
\tau_{1}
method: one-sided truncations

5.1
Proof of the inclusions (
M

τ
1

)

In this section, which follows the patterns of §
3
and §
4
, we establish the inclusions (
M

τ
1

).

Proposition 5.1

Let
{\varepsilon}>0
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, suppose that
w_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
j=1,\ldots,n
, with at least one
w_{j}
non-zero, and that
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal and
\|Ax\|\leq{\varepsilon}
, for some
x\in E
with
\|x\|=1
. Then, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, where
A^{+}_{n,k}
is defined by (
1.29
), it holds that

\nu(A^{+}_{n,k})\leq{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n},

(5.1)

where

{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}\ :=\ \left(\left\|\alpha\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\gamma\right\|_{\infty}\right)\sqrt{\displaystyle\frac{T_{n}}{S_{n}}}

(5.2)

with

S_{n}\ =\ \sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{j}^{2}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad T_{n}\ =\ w_{1}^{2}+w_{n}^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\left(w_{i+1}-w_{i}\right)^{2}.

Proof.

Let
y=Ax
, so
\|y\|\leq{\varepsilon}
. We argue as in the proof of Proposition
3.3
, using the notation
E_{j}
, defined in (
3.10
), for
j=-1,0,\ldots,n,n+1
, with
w_{-1}:=w_{0}:=w_{n+1}:=w_{n+2}:=0
.
Put

a_{k}:=\|A^{+}_{n,k}\tilde{x}_{n,k}\|
and
b_{k}:=\|\tilde{x}_{n,k}\|
, for

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, where

\tilde{x}_{n,k}:=(w_{1}x_{k+1},w_{2}x_{k+2},\ldots,w_{n}x_{k+n})^{T}
.
As in the proof of Proposition
3.3
, we have that
\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{k}^{2}=S_{n}
. Further, cf. (
3.12
) and (
3.13
),

\displaystyle a_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|w_{1}\gamma_{k+1}x_{k+1}\|_{X}^{2}+\|w_{n}\alpha_{k+n}x_{k+n}\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle~\rule{14.22636pt}{0.0pt}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\alpha_{j+k-1}w_{j-1}x_{j+k-1}\ +\ \beta_{j+k}w_{j}x_{j+k}\ +\ \gamma_{j+k+1}w_{j+1}x_{j+k+1}\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n+1}\left(w_{j}\|y_{j+k}\|_{X}+E_{j-1}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\|x_{j+k-1}\|_{X}+E_{j}\|\gamma\|_{\infty}\|x_{j+k+1}\|_{X}\right)^{2}.

So, for all
\theta>0
and
\phi>0
, using Lemma

3.2
twice,

\displaystyle a_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n+1}\left[(1+\theta)\left(w_{j}\|y_{j+k}\|_{X}\right)^{2}+(1+\theta^{-1})\left((1+\phi)\left|\alpha_{i-1}\right|^{2}\left(E_{j-1}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\|x_{j+k-1}\|_{X}\right)^{2}\right.\right.

\displaystyle~\rule{14.22636pt}{0.0pt}+\left.\left.(1+\phi^{-1})\left(E_{j}\|\gamma\|_{\infty}\|x_{j+k+1}\|_{X}\right)^{2}\right)\right].

Thus, for all
\theta,\phi>0
,

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}a_{k}^{2}\ \leq\ (1+\theta)S_{n}\|y\|^{2}+(1+\theta^{-1})\left[(1+\phi)T_{n}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}^{2}+(1+\phi^{-1})T_{n}\|\gamma\|_{\infty}^{2}\right].

Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
3.3
(cf. (
3.15
) and (
3.16
)) it follows that

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}a_{k}^{2}\leq\left({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}\right)^{2}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}b_{k}^{2},

so that
a_{k}\leq({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n})b_{k}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
b_{k}\neq 0
, which implies that
\nu(A_{n,k}^{+})\leq{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
.

The above result has the following straightforward corollary (cf. Corollary
3.4
).

Corollary 5.2

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, suppose that
w_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
j=1,...,n
, with
at least one
w_{j}
non-zero, and that
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal. Then

\inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\nu(A^{+}_{n,k})\ \leq\ \nu(A)+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n},

(5.3)

where
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
is given by (
5.2
).

The proof of the following main theorem is very similar to those of Theorems

3.5
and

4.3
.

Theorem 5.3

Suppose that
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
w_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
j=1,\ldots,n
, with at least one
w_{j}
non-zero. Then (
M

τ
1

) holds with
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
given by (
5.2
).

Proof.
We have justified already, below (
1.28
), the inclusions from the left in (
M

τ
1

).
To see that the second inclusion from the right in (
M

τ
1

) holds,
let

\lambda\in{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}(A)
. Then either
\nu(A-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}
or
\nu(A^{*}-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}
. By Corollary
5.2
it follows that either
\nu(A^{+}_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n}^{+})<{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, or
\nu((A^{*})^{+}_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n}^{+})<{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Thus, by (
1.30
),
\mu_{n}(A-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
, so that
\lambda\in\gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)
. The first inclusion from the right in (
M

τ
1

) follows by taking intersections, recalling (
2.7
).

Following the pattern of §
3.2
and §
4
, we now minimise
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
as a function of the weight vector
w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
over
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
, or equivalently over
\{w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}:\|w\|=1\}
. In order to minimise

{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
, given by (
5.2
),
we need to minimise
T_{n}/S_{n}
, where

\displaystyle S_{n}\ =\ \|w\|^{2}\quad\mbox{and}\quad T_{n}\ =\ w_{1}^{2}+(w_{2}-w_{1})^{2}+\cdots+(w_{n}-w_{n-1})^{2}+w_{n}^{2}\ =\ \|Bw\|^{2},

with

B=\begin{pmatrix}1&&&&\\
-1&1&&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&&\\
&&-1&1&\\
&&&-1&1\\
&&&&-1\end{pmatrix}_{(n+1)\times n}\textrm{so that}\quad B^{T}B=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&&&\\
-1&2&-1&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&-1&2&-1\\
&&&-1&2\end{pmatrix}_{n\times n}.

Clearly,

\inf_{\|w\|\neq 0}\frac{T_{n}}{S_{n}}\ =\ \inf_{\|w\|\neq 0}\frac{\|Bw\|^{2}}{\|w\|^{2}}\ =\ \inf_{\|w\|\neq 0}\frac{w^{T}B^{T}Bw}{w^{T}w}\ =\ \lambda_{\sf min}(B^{T}B),

the smallest eigenvalue of
B^{T}B
. One could now compute the eigenvalues of
B^{T}B
as in the previous
sections §
3.2
and §
4
. In this simple case (
-B^{T}B
is the discrete Laplacian),
this is a standard result (e.g.
[
6
]
). We have that

{\rm spec}\,(B^{T}B)\ =\ \left\{\lambda_{j}:=2-2\cos\frac{j\pi}{(n+1)}=4\sin^{2}\frac{j\pi}{2(n+1)}\ :\ j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\right\},

(5.4)

so that
\lambda_{\sf min}(B^{T}B)=\lambda_{1}
. Hence the minimal
value for
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
is

{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}\ :=\ (\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})\sqrt{\frac{T_{n}}{S_{n}}}\ =\ (\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}\ =\ 2(\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})\sin\frac{\pi}{2(n+1)}.

This minimum is realised
[
6
]
by the choice

w=(\sin\frac{j\pi}{n+1})_{j=1}^{n}
for the weight vector in
Theorem
5.3
.

Corollary 5.4

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the inclusions (
M

τ
1

) hold
with

{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}\ :=\ 2(\|\alpha\|_{\infty}+\|\gamma\|_{\infty})\sin\frac{\pi}{2(n+1)}.

(5.5)

The example of the shift operator (Example
1.4
and §
8.1
) shows that the above formula is sharp; (
M

τ
1

) does not hold for all tridiagonal
A\in L(E)
if
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
is any smaller than the above value.

5.2
Does an analogue of Proposition
3.1
hold for the
\tau_{1}
method?

For operators
B\in L(X)
(in particular when
X={\mathbb{C}}^{n}
and
B
is a finite square matrix), the resolvent norm

\lambda\ \mapsto\ \|(B-\lambda I)^{-1}\|

on
\rho(B):={\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\rm Spec}\,B
is a subharmonic function, subject to a maximum principle; it cannot have a local maximum in
\rho(B)
. Likewise, its reciprocal, which (recall (
1.8
) and (
1.11
)) is the Lipschitz continuous function

f_{B}:\ \lambda\ \mapsto\ \mu(B-\lambda I),

cannot have a local minimum on
\rho(B)
, indeed cannot have a local minimum on
{\mathbb{C}}
, except that it takes the minimum value zero on
{\rm Spec}\,B
.

The proof of the identity
\overline{\sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)}=\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)=\widehat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)
for the
\tau
method in Proposition
3.1
rests indirectly on this property, and the stronger result that
f_{B}
cannot be locally constant on any open subset of
\rho(B)
, which holds if
X
has the Globevnik property. The same is true for the proof of the corresponding property (
4.12
) for the
\pi
method. But the corresponding relationship for the
\tau_{1}
method, relating the two
\tau_{1}
sets defined in (
1.27
), that
\overline{\gamma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)}=\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)
, does not hold for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\varepsilon}>0
, and every tridiagonal
A
, as the example we give below shows.

The issue is that the appropriate version of the mapping
f_{B}
can have local (non-zero) minima, in the case that
B
is a finite rectangular matrix, as discussed in
[
83
, §X.46]
and in
[
75
]
. An example that illustrates this is the

4\times 2
matrix

B:=\begin{pmatrix}\delta&0\\
0&0\\
1&1\\
0&\delta\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{with}\qquad\delta\in(0,\textstyle\frac{1}{2}).

The relevant version of the mapping
f_{B}
, namely
f_{B}(\lambda):=\nu(B-\lambda I_{2}^{+})
, where
I_{2}^{+}
is as defined above (
1.30
) (with
X={\mathbb{C}}
),
has global minima (over all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
) at
0
and
1
(see the calculations in Lemma
5.5
below), with the values

f(0)=f(1)=\nu(B)=\delta>0.

To obtain our counterexample to
\overline{\gamma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)}=\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)
we build a tridiagonal bi-infinite matrix around
B
, given by
10
10

10

This is an example of a so-called
paired Laurent operator
, a class for which the spectrum can be computed explicitly. See, e.g.,
[
48
, §4.4.1]
,
[
62
, §3.7.3]
.

A:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}\ddots&\ddots&&&\\
,\ddots&0&\delta&&\\
&1&0&\pagecolor{black!10}\delta&\\
&&1&\pagecolor{black!10}0&\\
&&&\pagecolor{black!10}1&\pagecolor{black!10}1\\
&&&&\pagecolor{black!10}\delta&1\\
&&&&&\delta&1\\
&&&&&&\ddots&\ddots\end{array}\right).

(5.6)

The sets
\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)
and
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)
are defined by (
1.27
) in terms of
\mu_{n}(A-\lambda I)
, which in turn is expressed in terms of lower norms of rectangular matrices in (
1.30
). For the case
n=2
there are only three distinct matrices
A^{+}_{n,k}
as
k
ranges over
{\mathbb{Z}}
, namely

B_{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}\delta&\\
0&\delta\\
1&0\\
&1\end{pmatrix},\quad B_{2}:=B=\begin{pmatrix}\delta&\\
0&0\\
1&1\\
&\delta\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{and}\quad B_{3}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&\\
1&0\\
\delta&1\\
&\delta\end{pmatrix},

and only five different matrices
(A^{*})^{+}_{n,k}
with
n=2
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, namely

C_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
0&1\\
\delta&0\\
&\delta\end{pmatrix},\quad C_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
0&1\\
\delta&0\\
&0\end{pmatrix},\quad C_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
0&1\\
0&1\\
&0\end{pmatrix},\quad C_{4}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
1&\delta\\
0&1\\
&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{and}\quad C_{5}=\begin{pmatrix}\delta&\\
1&\delta\\
0&1\\
&0\end{pmatrix}.

Putting, for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
and
k\in\{1,\dots,5\}
,

f_{j}(\lambda)\ :=\ \nu(B_{j}-\lambda I_{2}^{+})\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{k}(\lambda)\ :=\ \nu(C_{k}-\lambda I_{2}^{+}),\qquad\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}},

we have

\mu_{n}(A-\lambda I)\ =\ \min\left\{\,f_{j}(\lambda)\,,\,g_{k}(\lambda):j\in\{1,2,3\},\,k\in\{1,\ldots,5\}\right\}\ =:\ h(\lambda).

(5.7)

Lemma 5.5

Let
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. Then:

a)

f_{2}(\lambda)\geq\delta
for all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
;

b)

0<\delta=f_{2}(0)<\frac{1}{2}<f_{3}(0)=g_{5}(0)<g_{2}(0)=g_{3}(0)=g_{4}(0)=1<f_{1}(0)=g_{1}(0)
.

As a consequence, the function
f_{2}
, and so also the function
h(\lambda)=\mu_{n}(A-\lambda I)
, have positive local minima at zero, namely

\mu_{n}(A)\ =\ h(0)\ =\ f_{2}(0)\ =\ \delta.

Proof.

a)
For all
x={x_{1}\choose x_{2}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
, we have

\|(B_{2}-\lambda I)x\|\ =\ \left\|\begin{pmatrix}\delta x_{1}\\
-\lambda x_{1}\\
x_{1}+(1-\lambda)x_{2}\\
\delta x_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right\|\ \geq\ \left\|\begin{pmatrix}\delta x_{1}\\
0\\
0\\
\delta x_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right\|\ =\ \delta\|x\|,

so that
f_{2}(\lambda)=\nu(B_{2}-\lambda I)\geq\delta
for all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

b)
We start with the computation of
f_{j}(0)
for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. Firstly,

f_{1}(0)=\nu(B_{1})=\sqrt{1+\delta^{2}}>1
, since
\|B_{1}x\|^{2}=(1+\delta^{2})\|x\|^{2}
for all
x\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
. Secondly,

f_{2}(0)=\nu(B_{2})\geq\delta
holds by a). To see equality, observe that
\|B_{2}x\|=\delta\|x\|
for
x={1\choose-1}
. Thirdly,

f_{3}(0)=\nu(B_{3})\in[1-\delta,1)
, since

\|B_{3}x-\delta(0,0,x_{1},x_{2})^{\top}\|=\|x\|
, so that
\|B_{3}x\|\geq\|x\|-\delta\|x\|
for all
x={x_{1}\choose x_{2}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
, whence
\nu(B_{3})\geq 1-\delta
. Computation of
\|B_{3}x\|
with
x={1\choose-\delta}
shows that
\nu(B_{3})<1
.

Carrying on to the
g_{k}(0)
with
k\in\{1,\dots,5\}
, again writing
x\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
as
x={x_{1}\choose x_{2}}
, we get:

g_{2}(0)=\nu(C_{2})=1
, since
\|C_{2}x\|\geq\|(x_{1},x_{2},0,0)^{\top}\|=\|x\|
for all
x\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
, with equality for
x={0\choose 1}
.

g_{3}(0)=\nu(C_{3})=1
, since
\|C_{3}x\|\geq\|(x_{1},x_{2},0,0)^{\top}\|=\|x\|
for all
x\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
, with equality for
x={1\choose 0}
.

g_{4}(0)=\nu(C_{4})=1
, since
\|C_{4}x\|\geq\|(x_{1},0,x_{2},0)^{\top}\|=\|x\|
for all
x\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
, with equality for
x={\delta\choose-1}
.

Finally, denoting the flip isometry
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\mapsto(x_{m},\dots,x_{1})
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
by
J_{m}
, we have
C_{1}=J_{4}B_{1}J_{2}
and
C_{5}=J_{4}B_{3}J_{2}
, showing that
g_{1}(0)=f_{1}(0)
and
g_{5}(0)=f_{3}(0)
.

By Lemma
5.5
, the tridiagonal matrix (
5.6
) has the properties that, for
n=2
and
0<\delta<1/2
,

0\in\Gamma_{\delta}^{n}(A)=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu_{n}(A-\lambda I)\leq\delta\}
but
\gamma_{\delta}^{n}(A)=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu_{n}(A-\lambda I)<\delta\}
does not intersect
{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{D}}
, for some
{\varepsilon}>0
. Thus
0\not\in\overline{\gamma_{\delta}^{n}(A)}
, and

\overline{\gamma_{\delta}^{n}(A)}\subsetneq\Gamma^{n}_{\delta}(A).

(5.8)

6
Proof of Theorem
1.9
and a band-dominated generalisation

The following result is a generalisation of Theorem
1.9
to the band-dominated case. It reduces to Theorem
1.9
if
A
is tridiagonal (in which case
A^{(n)}=A
, for each
n
, so that
w_{n}=1
and also
A_{n}=A
and
\delta_{n}=0
), provided also
X
is finite-dimensional or a Hilbert space (in which case
\eta_{n}=0
). Note that the requirement that
\{A_{ij}:i,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}\subset L(X)
be relatively compact is satisfied automatically if
X
is finite-dimensional.

Theorem 6.1

Suppose that
A\in BDO(E)
and that
\{A_{ij}:i,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}\subset L(X)
is relatively compact. Let
A^{(n)}\in BO(E)
be defined, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, by
A^{(n)}:=A
if
A
if tridiagonal, otherwise by (
1.41
) for some
\mathfrak{p}\geq 0
such that

\delta_{n}:=\|A-A^{(n)}\|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, and let
A_{n}:={\mathcal{I}}_{w_{n}}A^{(n)}{\mathcal{I}}^{-1}_{w_{n}}
, where
w_{n}
is the band-width of
A^{(n)}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{b}
is defined as above (
1.14
). In the case that
X
is finite-dimensional or a Hilbert space set
\eta_{n}:=0
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, otherwise let
(\eta_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
denote any positive null sequence. Then, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists a finite set
K_{n}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
such that:

\forall k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\;\;\exists j\in K_{n}\mbox{ such that }\|(A_{n})^{+}_{n,k}-(A_{n})^{+}_{n,j}\|\leq 1/n\mbox{ and }\|((A_{n})^{*})^{+}_{n,k}-((A_{n})^{*})^{+}_{n,j}\|\leq 1/n.

(6.1)

Further, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, using the notations (
1.45
) and (
1.39
),

\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A_{n})\ \subset\ {\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}+\eta_{n}}A\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}+\eta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})+1/n}(A_{n}),

(6.2)

where

\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A_{n})=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu_{n}^{\mathrm{fin}}(A_{n}-\lambda I)\leq{\varepsilon}\right\}

(6.3)

with

\mu_{n}^{\mathrm{fin}}(A_{n}-\lambda I)=\min_{j\in K_{n}}\min\Big(\nu\left((A_{n})^{+}_{n,j}-\lambda I^{+}_{n}\right),\,\nu\left(((A_{n})^{*})^{+}_{n,j}-\lambda I^{+}_{n}\right)\Big),\quad\mbox{for }\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}.

(6.4)

Moreover, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
,
\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}+2\delta_{n}+2\eta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})+1/n}(A_{n})\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
, in particular

\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})+2\delta_{n}+2\eta_{n}+1/n}(A_{n})\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
.

Proof.
Fix
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. To see (
6.1
) note first that the relative compactness of the matrix entries of
A
implies, by the definition of
A_{n}
, relative compactness also of the entries of
A_{n}
. Further, since the set of matrix entries of
A_{n}
is relatively compact, so that every sequence has a convergent subsequence, the same is true for

{\mathcal{S}}_{n}:=\{((A_{n})^{+}_{n,k},((A_{n})^{*})^{+}_{n,k}):k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}\subset((X^{w_{n}})^{(n+2)\times n})^{2}.

(6.5)

Thus there exists
K_{n}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
such that (
6.1
) holds since relatively compact sets are totally bounded.

To see (
6.2
), note that it follows from (
1.43
), (
M

τ
1

), and (
2.4
) (if
\eta_{n}>0
) or (
2.5
) (if
\eta_{n}=0
) that
\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}}(A_{n})\subset{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n}\subset{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}+\eta_{n}}A
. Again applying (
2.4
)/(
2.5
),

{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}}A\ \subset\ {\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}+\eta_{n}}A_{n}\ \subset\ \Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}+\eta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})}(A_{n})\ \subset\ \Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}+\eta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})+1/n}(A_{n}),

by (
M

τ
1

) and (
1.43
).
Thus, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
,

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta_{n}+\eta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})+1/n}(A_{n})\ \subset\ {\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+2\delta_{n}+2\eta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})+1/n}A.

Arguing as above Theorem
1.6
, we have that
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, so that
\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}+2\delta_{n}+2\eta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})+1/n}(A_{n})

\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
, by (
2.8
).

Remark 6.2
In the theorem above we note that there exists a finite set
K_{n}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
such that (
6.1
) holds. An equivalent statement is to say that there exists a finite set
S_{n}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
, where
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
is defined by (
6.5
), such that

\forall k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\;\;\exists(B_{1},B_{2})\in S_{n}\mbox{ such that }\|(A_{n})^{+}_{n,k}-B_{1}\|\leq 1/n\mbox{ and }\|((A_{n})^{*})^{+}_{n,k}-B_{2}\|\leq 1/n.

(6.6)

The above theorem thus remains true if we replace (
6.1
) with (
6.6
) and replace (
6.4
) with the formula

\mu_{n}^{\mathrm{fin}}(A_{n}-\lambda I)=\min_{(B_{1},B_{2})\in S_{n}}\min\Big(\nu\left(B_{1}-\lambda I^{+}_{n}\right),\,\nu\left(B_{2}-\lambda I^{+}_{n}\right)\Big),\quad\mbox{for }\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}.

(6.7)

Importantly, with these changes, the proof still holds if, instead of requiring that
S_{n}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
, we make the weaker requirement that
S_{n}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{S}}_{n}}
, the closure of
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
in
((X^{w_{n}})^{(n+2)\times n})^{2}
: the elements of
S_{n}
