need not be in
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
only in
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}_{n}}
. This observation is helpful because there are instances where it is much easier to identify
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}_{n}}
than
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
.

Theorem
1.9
and the above result are both based on the
\tau_{1}
method. Versions of these results hold also for the
\tau
and
\pi
methods, at least in the case that
A
is tridiagonal, provided (this is a substantial assumption) these methods do not suffer from spectral pollution for the particular operator
A
. Here is a version (cf. Theorem
1.9
) for the case that
A
is tridiagonal (recall from §
1.3
that every banded
A
can be written in tridiagonal form), written in the way suggested by the above remark. We use in this theorem the notation that, for tridiagonal
B\in L(E)
,

B^{(M)}_{n,k}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}B_{n,k},&\mbox{if $M=\tau$},\\
B^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k},&\mbox{if $M=\pi$},\end{array}\right.

(6.8)

where
t
in (
6.8
) is some fixed value with
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, and
B_{n,k}
and
B_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}
are as defined in (
1.19
) and (
1.20
).

Theorem 6.3

Suppose that
X
satisfies Globevnik’s property, that
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal, and that
\{A_{ij}:i,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}\subset L(X)
is relatively compact, and, where
M=\tau
or
\pi
, suppose that the
M
method does not suffer from spectral pollution for
A
in the sense of Definition
1.1
. Let
(\eta_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be the null sequence from that definition, and
(\eta^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be any other positive null sequence. Then, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists a finite set
S_{n}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{(M)}}
, where

{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{(M)}\ :=\ \left\{A^{(M)}_{n,k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\right\}\subset X^{n\times n},

(6.9)

such that:

\forall k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\;\;\exists B\in S_{n}\mbox{ such that }\|A^{(M)}_{n,k}-B\|\leq 1/n.

(6.10)

Further, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},M}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\ \subset\ {\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+\eta_{n}+\eta^{\prime}_{n}}A\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},M}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{(M)}_{n}+1/n}(A),

(6.11)

where
{\varepsilon}^{(M)}_{n}:={\varepsilon}_{n}
for
M=\tau
,
:={\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
for
M=\pi
, with
{\varepsilon}_{n}
and
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
defined as in (
3.6
) and (
1.24
), and

\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},M}_{{\varepsilon}}(A):=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\min_{B\in S_{n}}\mu\left(B-\lambda I_{n}\right)\leq{\varepsilon}\right\}.

(6.12)

Moreover, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
,
\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},M}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{(M)}_{n}+1/n}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
, in particular
\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},M}_{{\varepsilon}^{(M)}_{n}+1/n}(A)

\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
.

Proof.
That (
6.10
) holds follows from the relative compactness of
\{A_{ij}:i,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}\subset L(X)
, arguing as in the proof of Theorem
1.9
(and see Remark
6
). Now, by (
1.21
) and (
1.9
),

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\inf_{B\in S^{(\tau)}}\mu\left(B-\lambda I_{n}\right)\leq{\varepsilon}\right\}=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\inf_{B\in\overline{S^{(\tau)}}}\mu\left(B-\lambda I_{n}\right)\leq{\varepsilon}\right\},

by (
1.11
), so that
\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},\tau}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\subset\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)
. Similarly,
\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},\pi}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)\subset\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n,t}(A)
by (
1.23
), (
1.9
), and (
1.11
). Since
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)\subset\sigma_{{\varepsilon}+\eta_{n}^{\prime}}^{n}(A)
and
\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)\subset\pi_{{\varepsilon}+\eta_{n}^{\prime}}^{n}(A)
, the first inclusion in (
6.11
) follows from the assumption of the absence of spectral pollution. Note that (cf. (
1.43
)), if (
6.10
) holds, then, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
,

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)\ \subset\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},\tau}_{{\varepsilon}+1/n}(A)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n,t}(A)\ \subset\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},\pi}_{{\varepsilon}+1/n}(A),

as a consequence of (
1.11
) and since

\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{n}(A)=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\inf_{B\in S^{(\tau)}}\mu\left(B-\lambda I_{n}\right)<{\varepsilon}\right\},

and the same representation holds for
\pi^{n,t}_{\varepsilon}(A)
, with
S^{(\tau)}
replaced by
S^{(\pi)}
.
These inclusions and (
M

τ

) and (
M

π

) imply the second inclusion in (
6.11
).
Thus, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
,

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},M}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{(M)}_{n}+1/n}(A)\ \subset\ {\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{(M)}_{n}+\eta_{n}+\eta^{\prime}_{n}+1/n}A,

so that
\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},M}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{(M)}_{n}+1/n}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
, by (
2.8
).

7
The scalar case: computational aspects and the solvability complexity index

In this section we focus on the case that, for some
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
X={\mathbb{C}}^{p}
, which we equip with the Euclidean norm (the
2
-norm), so that
X
and
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{C}}^{p})
are Hilbert spaces, and the entries of the matrix representation
[A_{ij}]
of
A\in L(E)
are
p\times p
complex matrices, i.e.,
L(X)={\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
. This includes the special case that
p=1
, so that
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
and the entries
A_{ij}
are just complex numbers
11
11

11

This is a special case, but recall, as noted at the top of the paper, that, given a separable Hilbert space
Y
, our results for this special case apply to any
A\in L(Y)
that, with respect to some orthonormal basis
(e_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\subset Y
, has a matrix representation
A_{ij}=(Ae_{j},e_{i})\in{\mathbb{C}}
that is banded or band-dominated.
.

Our goals in this section are to provide computational details regarding the determination of membership of our
\tau
,
\pi
, and
\tau_{1}
inclusion sets in this case, to prove Proposition
1.10
and an extension of this result to the band-dominated case, and to note implications regarding the solvability complex index. In particular, a key conclusion in this section is that the computational problem of determining the spectrum of a band-dominated operator, given the inputs that we assume, has solvability complexity index SCI
{}_{A}=1
, indeed is in the class
\Pi^{1}_{A}
(our notations are those of
[
2
,
22
]
).

In the above set up, in which
X
and
E
are Hilbert spaces, standard formulae are available for computing the lower norms that our methods require. For any Hilbert spaces
Y
and
Z
and any
B\in L(Y,Z)
we have (see, e.g.,
[
61
, §1.3]
or
[
62
, §2.4]
)

(\nu(B))^{2}=\nu(B^{\prime}B)=\min{\rm Spec}\,(B^{\prime}B)=\left(s_{\min}(B)\right)^{2},

(7.1)

where
B^{\prime}\in L(Z,Y)
denotes the Hilbert space adjoint of
B
and
s_{\min}(B):=(\min{\rm Spec}\,(B^{\prime}B))^{1/2}
is its smallest singular value, so that (recall our notation (
1.8
))

\mu(B)=\left(\min(\nu(B^{\prime}B),\nu(BB^{\prime}))\right)^{1/2}=\min(s_{\min}(B),s_{\min}(B^{\prime})).

(7.2)

In particular, the above holds when
Y={\mathbb{C}}^{M}
and
Z={\mathbb{C}}^{N}
, for some
M,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, in which case
B
is an
N\times M
matrix and
B^{\prime}
is the conjugate transpose of
B
. In particular (see the discussion below (
1.8
)), if
M=N
, so that
Y=Z
and
B
is a square matrix, then

\mu(B)=\nu(B)=s_{\min}(B)=(\min{\rm Spec}\,(B^{\prime}B))^{1/2}=(\nu(B^{\prime}B))^{1/2}.

(7.3)

To decide whether a particular
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
is contained in one of our inclusion sets it is not necessary to actually compute any lower norms, it is enough to decide whether or not
\nu(B)\leq{\varepsilon}
or is
<{\varepsilon}
, for some non-negative threshold
{\varepsilon}
, and for
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
, where
{\mathcal{B}}
is some set of
N\times M
matrices, depending on which inclusion set method we choose (
\tau
,
\pi
, or
\tau_{1}
). By (
7.1
), this is equivalent to deciding whether the
M\times M
Hermitian matrix
{\varepsilon}^{2}I_{M}-B^{\prime}B
is positive semi-definite or positive definite, in particular

\nu(B)\leq{\varepsilon}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad{\varepsilon}^{2}I_{M}-B^{\prime}B\mbox{ is positive semi-definite}.

(7.4)

This can be tested by Gaussian elimination or by attempting an
LDL^{T}
version of the Cholesky decomposition
[
2
, Prop.10.1]
. These algorithms only require finitely many arithmetic operations. Precisely,
O(M^{3})
operations are sufficient, indeed
O(w^{2}M)
operations suffice if
B^{\prime}B
has band-width
w
(see, e.g.,
[
47
, Algorithm 4.3.5]
).

Let us emphasise that in this case we are considering where
X
is a finite-dimensional Hilbert space,
X
(and so also
E
) satisfy Globevnik’s property (see §
2
), so that all the inclusions (
M

τ

), (
M

π

), (
M

τ
1

) apply. In this finite-dimensional case the expressions for the key inclusion sets for the
\tau
and
\pi
methods simplify somewhat: we have by Proposition
3.1
that
\sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
and
\Sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
are given by (
1.26
) and, noting (
7.3
), the expression (
1.25
) for
\mu^{\dagger}(A-\lambda I)
simplifies to

\mu_{n}^{\dagger}(A-\lambda I)=\inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\nu(A_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n}),\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}},\;\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}.

(7.5)

Similarly, recalling from (
4.9
) that
\pi^{n,t}_{\varepsilon}(A)=\pi_{\varepsilon}^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}(A)
and
\Pi^{n,t}_{\varepsilon}(A)=\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n,\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}(A)
, when
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{c}
are given by (
4.1
), and letting
\mu_{n}^{\pi,t}
denote the function
\mu_{n}^{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}}
defined by (
4.10
) in the case that
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{c}
are given by (
4.1
), we have by
(
4.11
), (
4.12
), and (
7.3
) that, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
,

\pi^{n,t}_{\varepsilon}(A)=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu_{n}^{\pi,t}(A-\lambda I)<{\varepsilon}\},\;\;{\varepsilon}>0,\quad\Pi^{n,t}_{\varepsilon}(A)=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mu^{\pi,t}_{n}(A-\lambda I)\leq{\varepsilon}\},\;\;{\varepsilon}\geq 0,

(7.6)

where

\mu_{n}^{\pi,t}(A-\lambda I)=\inf_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\nu(A^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n}),\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}},\;\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}},\;\;t\in{\mathbb{T}}.

(7.7)

The corresponding representations for the
\tau_{1}
method inclusion sets are (
1.27
) and (
1.30
).

The above representations, and the discussion around (
7.4
), make clear that, in the case that
\{A_{n,k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
is finite, which, as we have noted in §
1.3.4
, holds if and only if the matrix representation of
A
has only finitely many entries, we can determine whether a given
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
is in one of the
\tau
-method-related sets,
\sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
and
\Sigma^{n}_{\varepsilon}(A)
, in only finitely many arithmetic operations. Similar remarks apply to the
\pi
and
\tau_{1}
methods. In the remainder of this section we consider questions of computation in finitely many operations in cases where
A
may have infinitely many distinct entries, proving Proposition
1.10
and an extension to the band-dominated case.

Proof of Proposition
1.10
.

Suppose
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Given the inputs specified in the theorem, to compute the finite set
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
, given by (
1.48
), one only needs to calculate
R:=\alpha_{\max}+\beta_{\max}+\gamma_{\max}
and check whether or not each of the finitely many points in
\mathrm{Grid}(n,R)
, given by (
1.47
), are in
\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}+2/n}(A)
, where
\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{\varepsilon}(A)
is given by (
1.45
) and (
1.46
). But this is just a question of deciding whether or not
\nu(B)\leq{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}+2/n
for finitely many matrices
B
, where
{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}
is given by (
1.49
), and, as noted above, this can be done in finitely many arithmetic operations.

Recall that
K_{n}={\mathcal{A}}_{p}(A,n)
so that (
1.44
) holds. To see that
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
it is enough, by Theorem
1.9
, and since
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\|A\|\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
, to show that
\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}+1/n}(A)\cap\|A\|\overline{D}\subset\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
. So suppose that
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}+1/n}(A)
and
|\lambda^{\prime}|\leq\|A\|
. Then, since
R\geq\|A\|
, it is easy to see that there exists
\lambda\in\mathrm{Grid}(n,R)
with
|\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}|\leq\sqrt{2}/n
. Since
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}+1/n}(A)
and recalling (
1.10
), we see that there exists
k\in K_{n}
such that either
\nu(A_{n,k}^{+}-\lambda I_{n}^{+})\leq{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}+(1+\sqrt{2})/n
or
\nu((A^{*})_{n,k}^{+}-\lambda I_{n}^{+})\leq{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}+(1+\sqrt{2})/n<{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}+3/n
, so that
\lambda\in\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}+3/n}(A)\subset\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}+3/n}(A)
, i.e.,
\lambda\in\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
. This implies, since
|\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}|\leq\sqrt{2}/n<2/n
, that
\lambda^{\prime}\in\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
.
Thus
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
, but also, by the definition (
1.48
) and Theorem
1.9
,

\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\subset\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}+3/n}(A)\subset{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}+3/n}(A)
, so that

{\rm Spec}\,A\ \subset\ \widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\ \subset\ {\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}+3/n}(A)+\frac{2}{n}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}.

It follows from (
2.8
) that
{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}+3/n}(A)+\frac{2}{n}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
, so that also
\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\to{\rm Spec}\,A
. Since
d_{H}(\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A),\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A))\leq 2/n
, we have also that
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\to{\rm Spec}\,A
.

Let us, as promised, extend Proposition
1.10
and the discussion of §
1.3.5
to the band-dominated case.
Let
\Omega=BDO(E)
, with
X={\mathbb{C}}
, so that
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
and the matrix entries of
A\in\Omega
are complex numbers. The mappings we will need, to provide us with the inputs needed to compute a sequence of approximations to
{\rm Spec}\,A
, for
A\in\Omega
, are
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{p}
,
{\mathbb{C}}_{p}
, for
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
, as defined in §
1.3.5
, plus the mapping

\mathcal{D}=({\mathcal{D}}_{1},{\mathcal{D}}_{2}):\Omega\times{\mathbb{N}}\to\Omega_{T}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}
,
({\mathcal{A}},n)\mapsto(A_{n},\eta_{n})
, where
A_{n}:={\mathcal{I}}_{n}A^{(n)}{\mathcal{I}}_{n}^{-1}
,
{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
is defined below (
1.14
),
A^{(n)}
is defined by (
1.41
) with
\mathfrak{p}=1
, and
\eta_{n}\geq 0
is such that
\eta_{n}\geq\|A-A^{(n)}\|
and
\eta_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

The sequence of approximations to
{\rm Spec}\,A
is
(\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, given by

\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A):=\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}(A_{n})+3/n+\eta_{n}}(A_{n})\cap\mathrm{Grid}(n,R_{n}),\qquad n\in{\mathbb{N}},

(7.8)

where
(A_{n},\eta_{n}):={\mathcal{D}}(A,n)
,
(\alpha_{\max},\beta_{\max},\gamma_{\max}):={\mathcal{B}}_{n}(A_{n})
,
R_{n}:=\alpha_{\max}+\beta_{\max}+\gamma_{\max}+\eta_{n}
(so that
R_{n}
is an upper bound for
\|A\|
),
K_{n}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
, in the definition (
1.45
) and (
1.46
), is given by
K_{n}:={\mathcal{A}}_{n}(A_{n},n)
, and (cf. (
1.39
) and (
1.49
))

{\varepsilon}_{n}^{*}(A_{n})\ :=\ (\alpha_{\max}+\gamma_{\max})\,\frac{22}{7(n+1)}\ \geq\ 2(\alpha_{\max}+\gamma_{\max})\,\sin\frac{\pi}{2(n+1)}\geq{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n}),

(7.9)

where
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})
is defined by (
1.39
). As we note in the following theorem, each element of this sequence can be computed in finitely many arithmetical operations, given finitely many evaluations of the input maps
{\mathcal{A}}_{p}
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{p}
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{p}
, and
{\mathcal{D}}
.

Theorem 7.1

For
A\in\Omega
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
, as defined in (
7.8
), can be computed in finitely many arithmetic operations, given
\eta_{n}:={\mathcal{D}}_{2}(A,n)
,
K_{n}:={\mathcal{A}}_{n}(A_{n},n)
,
(\alpha_{\max},\beta_{\max},\gamma_{\max}):={\mathcal{B}}_{n}(A_{n})
, and
((A_{n})_{n,k}^{+},((A_{n})A^{*})^{+}_{n,k}):={\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A_{n},k,n)
, for
k\in{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
, where
A_{n}:={\mathcal{D}}_{1}(A,n)
. Further,
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
, and also

\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\ :=\ \Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)+\frac{2}{n}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm Spec}\,A

as
n\to\infty
, with
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

We will prove Theorem
7.1
shortly, but let us interpret this theorem as a result relating to the solvability complexity index (SCI) of
[
1
,
2
,
22
]
, as we did for Proposition
1.10
in §
1.3.5
. Inspecting the definition of
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
, note that it is not necessary to explicitly compute the action of both components of
{\mathcal{D}}
, it is enough to evaluate
{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
, the second component of
{\mathcal{D}}
, to obtain
\eta_{n}={\mathcal{D}}_{2}(A,n)
, and then to evaluate compositions of
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
with
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
, and
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
, to obtain

K_{n}:=({\mathcal{A}}_{n}(\cdot,n)\circ{\mathcal{D}}_{1})(A)
,
(\alpha_{\max},\beta_{\max},\gamma_{\max}):=({\mathcal{B}}_{n}\circ{\mathcal{D}}_{1})(A)
, and
((A_{n})_{n,k}^{+},((A_{n})^{*})^{+}_{n,k}):=({\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\cdot,k,n)\circ{\mathcal{D}}_{1})(A)
.
Equipping
{\mathbb{C}}^{C}
with the Hausdorff metric as in §
1.3
,
the mappings

\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}^{C},\qquad A\mapsto\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\quad\mbox{and}\quad A\mapsto\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A),

are, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, general algorithms in the sense of
[
1
,
2
,
22
]
, with evaluation set (in the sense of
[
1
,
2
,
22
]
)

\Lambda:=\{{\mathcal{A}}_{n}(\cdot,n)\circ{\mathcal{D}}_{1},{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\circ{\mathcal{D}}_{1},{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\cdot,k,n)\circ{\mathcal{D}}_{1},{\mathcal{D}}_{2}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}},\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\};

each function in
\Lambda
has domain
\Omega
, and each can be expressed in terms of finitely many complex-valued functions; see the footnote below (
8.38
).
Further,
\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
can be computed in finitely many arithmetic operations and specified using finitely many complex numbers (the elements of
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
and the value of
n
). Thus, where
\Xi:\Omega\to{\mathbb{C}}^{C}
is the mapping given by
\Xi(A):={\rm Spec}\,A
, for
A\in\Omega
, the computational problem
\{\Xi,\Omega,{\mathbb{C}}^{C},\Lambda\}
has arithmetic SCI, in the sense of
[
1
,
2
,
22
]
, equal to one; more precisely, since also
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
A\in\Omega
, this computational problem is in the class
\Pi_{1}^{A}
, as defined in
[
2
,
22
]
.

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

Suppose
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Note first that
R:=\sup\{R_{n}:n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}<\infty
as (cf. (
1.17
))
R_{n}\leq 3\|A_{n}\|+\eta_{n}
,
(\eta_{n})
is a null sequence, and
\|A_{n}\|=\|A^{(n)}\|\leq\|A\|+\eta_{n}
. Thus, arguing as in the proof of Proposition
1.10
,
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
can be computed in finitely many arithmetic operations.

Since
K_{n}={\mathcal{A}}_{n}(A_{n},n)
, (
1.44
) holds with
A
replaced by
A_{n}
. To see that
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
, note that, by the first inclusion in (
2.3
),
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset{\rm Spec}\,_{\eta_{n}}A^{(n)}={\rm Spec}\,_{\eta_{n}}A_{n}
. Further, by Theorem
1.9
,
{\rm Spec}\,_{\eta_{n}}A_{n}\subset\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{\eta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})+1/n}
. Since also
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\|A\|\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
, it follows that
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{\eta_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}(A_{n})+1/n}\cap\|A\|\overline{D}
. Since
R_{n}\geq\|A\|
, it follows by arguing as in the proof of Proposition
1.10
that

{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A_{n})
.
Also, by definition and Theorem
1.9
,

\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A_{n})\subset\Gamma^{n,\mathrm{fin}}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}(A_{n})+3/n+\eta_{n}}(A_{n})\subset{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}(A_{n})+3/n+\eta_{n}}(A_{n})
,
and

{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}(A_{n})+3/n+\eta_{n}}(A_{n})\subset{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}(A_{n})+3/n+2\eta_{n}}(A)
,
by (
2.5
). Thus

{\rm Spec}\,A\ \subset\ \widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\ \subset\ {\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{*}_{n}(A_{n})+3/n+2\eta_{n}}(A)+\frac{2}{n}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}},

so that, by (
2.8
),
\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\to{\rm Spec}\,A
. Since
d_{H}(\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A),\widehat{\Gamma}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A))\leq 2/n
, we have also that
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\to{\rm Spec}\,A
.

8
Pseudoergodic operators and other examples

In this section, as in the previous section, our focus is the case that, for some
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
X={\mathbb{C}}^{p}
, which we equip with the Euclidean norm, so that
X
and
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{C}}^{p})
are Hilbert spaces, and the matrix entries
A_{ij}
are
p\times p
complex matrices. This includes the special case
p=1
, so that
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
and the entries
A_{ij}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Our aim is to
illustrate various of the results in earlier sections in the case that
A
is the tridiagonal matrix (
1.15
). We focus particularly on the study and computation of
\Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(V)
,
\Pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V)
, and
\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V)
, defined in (
1.21
), (
1.23
), and (
1.27
), respectively, that, by (
M

τ

), (
M

π

), and (
M

τ
1

), are inclusion sets for
{\rm Spec}\,A
, and the convergence of these sequences of inclusion sets as
n\to\infty
.

8.1
The shift operator

As a first example, we consider the shift operator
V
from Example
1.4

in more detail. As we noted in Example
1.4
,
{\rm Spec}\,V
is the unit circle

{\mathbb{T}}
for this operator. Moreover, we can obtain exact analytical descriptions for the inclusion sets
for
{\rm Spec}\,V
provided by the
\tau
,
\pi
, and
\tau_{1}
methods in (
M

τ

), (
M

π

),
and (
M

τ
1

). These are
\Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(V)
,
\Pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V)
, and
\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V)
, respectively, defined in (
1.21
), (
1.23
), and (
1.27
). In this case, as we noted in Example
1.4
,
\|\alpha\|_{\infty}=1
and
\|\gamma\|_{\infty}=0
so that, by Corollary
3.7
, (
1.24
), and (
1.28
),
{\varepsilon}_{n}=2\sin\frac{\pi}{4n+2}
,

{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}=2\sin\frac{\pi}{2n}
, and

{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}=2\sin\frac{\pi}{2n+2}
.

In general, computation of these inclusion sets for an operator
A
requires consideration of infinitely many submatrices of
A
, indexed by
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. But, as we have noted already in Example
1.4
, in the case
A=V
the relevant
n\times n
submatrices for the
\tau
and
\pi
methods are all the same: we have (see (
1.51
)) that
A_{n,k}=V_{n}
and
A^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k}=V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}
, for
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, where
V_{n}
and
V_{n}^{{\sf per},t}
are defined in (
1.51
), so that, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(V)={\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(V_{n})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V)={\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}),\quad t\in{\mathbb{T}}.

(8.1)

Similarly, recalling the definitions (
1.21
) and (
1.23
),

\sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(V)={\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(V_{n})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V)={\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}),\quad t\in{\mathbb{T}}.

For the
\tau_{1}
method the relevant submatrices for computing
\gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(A)
and
\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(A)
, given by (
1.27
), are
A^{+}_{n,k}
and
(A^{*})^{+}_{n,k}
, for
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, this notation defined in (
1.29
). In the case
A=V
we have, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, that

A^{+}_{n,k}\ =\ V_{n}^{+}\ :=\ \begin{pmatrix}0&0&\cdots&0&0\\
\hline\cr&&V_{n}\\
\hline\cr 0&0&\cdots&0&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{and}\quad(A^{*})^{+}_{n,k}\ =\ (V^{*})_{n}^{+}\ :=\ \begin{pmatrix}1&0&\cdots&0&0\\
\hline\cr&&V^{T}_{n}\\
\hline\cr 0&0&\cdots&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Thus, by (
1.27
), (
1.30
), and (
7.1
),

\gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V)\ =\ \left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:v(\lambda)<{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}\right\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V)\ =\ \left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:v(\lambda)\leq{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}\right\},

(8.2)

where

v(\lambda)\ :=\ \min\left(\nu(V_{n}^{+}-\lambda I_{n}^{+}),\nu((V^{*})_{n}^{+}-\lambda I_{n}^{+})\right)\ =\ \min\left(s_{\min}(V_{n}^{+}-\lambda I_{n}^{+}),s_{\min}((V^{*})_{n}^{+}-\lambda I_{n}^{+})\right).

With
{\mathbb{D}}
and
{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
as defined in §
1.3
, it follows from (
8.1
) and (
8.2
) that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathbb{D}}},

(8.3)

\displaystyle\Pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t^{1/n}{\mathbb{T}}_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}},\quad\mbox{for $t\in{\mathbb{T}}$,}\;\;\mbox{ and that}

(8.4)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\textstyle\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\,\setminus\,\left(1-({\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n})^{2}\right){\mathbb{D}},&n\geq 3,\\
\overline{{\mathbb{D}}},&n=1,2.\end{array}\right.

We have shown (
8.4
) already in Example
1.4
, and we will show the other identities above in a moment. Clearly, as predicted by (
M

τ

), (
M

π

)
and (
M

τ
1

), these identities show that

{\rm Spec}\,V\ \subset\ \Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(V)\cap\Pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V)\cap\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V),\quad t\in{\mathbb{T}},

but, as we will see in the calculations below, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
,

{\rm Spec}\,V\ \not\subset\ \sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(V),\qquad{\rm Spec}\,V\ \not\subset\ \pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V),\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad{\rm Spec}\,V\ \not\subset\ \gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V),

(8.8)

since

\sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(V)={\mathbb{D}},\;\;\pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V)=t^{1/n}{\mathbb{T}}_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}{\mathbb{D}},\;\;\gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathbb{D}}\,\setminus\,\left(1-({\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n})^{2}\right)\overline{{\mathbb{D}}},&n\geq 2,\\
{\mathbb{D}},&n=1.\end{array}\right.

(8.9)

This makes clear that
{\varepsilon}_{n}
,
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
and
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
are
just as large as necessary for (
M

τ

), (
M

π

),
and (
M

τ
1

) to hold.
Observe also that the above expressions imply that

d_{H}({\rm Spec}\,V,\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V))=4\sin^{2}\frac{\pi}{2(n+1)}\quad\mbox{and}\quad d_{H}({\rm Spec}\,V,\Pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V))=2\sin\frac{\pi}{2n},

so that
\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,V
as
n\to\infty
, as predicted by Theorem
1.2
, and also
\Pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,V
(this is a special case of Theorem
8.1
below). On the other hand,
d_{H}({\rm Spec}\,V,\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}^{n}(V))=1
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof of formulas (
8.3
)–(
8.8
).

Fix
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and put
R_{n}(\lambda):=\|(V_{n}-\lambda I_{n})^{-1}\|
for

\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
(with
R_{n}(0):=\infty
), where
V_{n}
is as in
(
1.51
). Firstly,
R_{n}
is invariant under rotation around

0
. Indeed, let
{\varphi}\in[0,2\pi)
and put

D_{n}:={\rm diag}(e^{{\rm i}{\varphi}},e^{{\rm i}2{\varphi}},...,e^{{\rm i}n{\varphi}})
. Then
D_{n}

is an isometry and
V_{n}D_{n}=e^{-{\rm i}{\varphi}}D_{n}V_{n}
, so that

\displaystyle R_{n}(e^{{\rm i}{\varphi}}\lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|(V_{n}-e^{{\rm i}{\varphi}}\lambda I_{n})^{-1}\|\ =\ \|e^{-{\rm i}{\varphi}}(e^{-{\rm i}{\varphi}}V_{n}-\lambda I_{n})^{-1}\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|(D_{n}^{-1}V_{n}D_{n}-\lambda I_{n})^{-1}\|\ =\ \|D_{n}^{-1}(V_{n}-\lambda I_{n})^{-1}D_{n}\|\ =\ R_{n}(\lambda).

Secondly,
R_{n}
is strictly monotonously decreasing on the half-axis

(0,+\infty)
. Indeed, suppose
0<\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\infty
and

R_{n}(\lambda_{1})\leq R_{n}(\lambda_{2})
. Since

\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}R_{n}(\lambda)=0
, there is a

\lambda_{3}\geq\lambda_{2}
with
R_{n}(\lambda_{3})=R_{n}(\lambda_{1})
. Thus

R_{n}
attains its supremum
M:=\max_{\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}]}R_{n}(\lambda)\geq R_{n}(\lambda_{2})\geq R_{n}(\lambda_{1})=R_{n}(\lambda_{3})
in the open disk

U:=\frac{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{3}}{2}+\frac{\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{1}}{2}{\mathbb{D}}
,
which contradicts the maximum principle of
[
44
]
, noted in
[
78
, Theorem 2.2]
.
Finally, recalling (
7.1
) and the notations
B_{n}
,
E_{n}(\phi)
, and
\varrho_{n}(\phi)
of
§
3.2
,
R_{n}(1)=\|B_{n}^{-1}\|
and
1/\|B_{n}^{-1}\|^{2}=(\nu(B_{n}))^{2}=\min{\rm Spec}\,(B_{n}^{T}B_{n})=\min{\rm Spec}\,(E_{n}(1))=\varrho_{n}(1)
. Further, by (
3.32
) and (
3.37
),
\varrho_{n}(1)={\varepsilon}_{n}^{2}
. Thus
R_{n}(1)=1/{\varepsilon}_{n}
.
Taking all of this together, we get that

\Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(V)=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:R_{n}(\lambda)>1/{\varepsilon}_{n}\}=\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
and
\sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(V)={\mathbb{D}}
.

We have shown in Example
1.4
that

V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}
is normal and
{\rm Spec}\,V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}=t^{1/n}{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
, so that

\Pi_{{\varepsilon}}^{n,t}(V)\ =\ {\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}}V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}\ =\ {\rm Spec}\,V^{{\sf per},t}_{n}+{\varepsilon}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\ =\ t^{1/n}{\mathbb{T}}_{n}+{\varepsilon}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}},\qquad{\varepsilon}>0.

(8.10)

Clearly, for each
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
,
\Pi_{{\varepsilon}}^{n,t}(V)
covers
{\rm Spec}\,V={\mathbb{T}}
if and only if it covers

t^{1/n}{\mathbb{T}}_{2n}
. The latter is the case if and only if
{\varepsilon}
is greater than or
equal to the Euclidean distance of two adjacent points of
{\mathbb{T}}_{2n}
,
which is
2\sin\frac{\pi}{2n}={\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
. Thus
{\rm Spec}\,V\subset\Pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V)
, but
{\rm Spec}\,V\not\subset\pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V)=\ t^{1/n}{\mathbb{T}}_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}{\mathbb{D}}
, since the points
t^{1/n}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, where
\mathfrak{S}_{n}:={\mathbb{T}}_{2n}\setminus{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
, which are points of intersection of the discs of radius
{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}
centred on
{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
, are not contained in
\pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V)
. Nor does
\pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V)
contain the other points of intersection of these discs, the points
rt^{1/n}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, with
r=1-({\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n})^{2}
, or any of the points
rt^{1/n}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, with
0\leq r\leq 1-({\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n})^{2}
or with
r\geq 1
. On the other hand, by elementary geometry,
\pi_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}^{n,t}(V)
contains the annulus
{\mathbb{D}}\setminus(1-({\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n})^{2})\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
. Thus

\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}\pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(V)\ =\ \bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}\left(t^{1/n}{\mathbb{T}}_{n}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}\,{\mathbb{D}}\right)\ =\ {\mathbb{D}}\setminus\left(1-({\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n})^{2}\right)\overline{{\mathbb{D}}},

and, by (
4.12
), also
\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}\,\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(V)=\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\setminus(1-({\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n})^{2}){\mathbb{D}}
.

To see that (
8.1
) holds, note that, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, recalling (
7.1
), and where
D_{n}:=I_{n}
if
\lambda=0
,
D_{n}:={\rm diag}(w^{1},...,w^{n})
with

w:=|\lambda|/\overline{\lambda}
if
\lambda\neq 0
,

\displaystyle\left(\nu(V_{n}^{+}-\lambda I_{n}^{+})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\nu((V-\lambda I)|_{E_{n,0}})\right)^{2}\ =\ \min{\rm Spec}\left(P_{n,0}(V-\lambda I)^{\prime}(V-\lambda I)|_{E_{n,0}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min{\rm Spec}\left((1+|\lambda|^{2})I_{n}-\lambda V^{T}_{n}-\overline{\lambda}V_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min{\rm Spec}\left(D_{n}^{-1}\Big((1+|\lambda|^{2})I_{n}-|\lambda|(V^{T}_{n}+V_{n})\Big)D_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min{\rm Spec}\left((1+|\lambda|^{2})I_{n}-|\lambda|(V^{T}_{n}+V_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+|\lambda|^{2}-2|\lambda|c_{n},

by (
5.4
), where
c_{n}:=\cos\frac{\pi}{n+1}=1-\frac{1}{2}({\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n})^{2}
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\left(\nu((V^{*})_{n}^{+}-\lambda I_{n}^{+})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min{\rm Spec}\left(P_{n,0}(V^{\prime}-\lambda I)^{\prime}(V^{\prime}-\lambda I)|_{E_{n,0}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min{\rm Spec}\left((1+|\lambda|^{2})I_{n}-\lambda V_{n}-\overline{\lambda}V^{T}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min{\rm Spec}\left((1+|\lambda|^{2})I_{n}-|\lambda|(V^{T}_{n}+V_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+|\lambda|^{2}-2|\lambda|c_{n},

so that

v(\lambda)=\left(1+|\lambda|^{2}-2|\lambda|c_{n}\right)^{1/2},\qquad\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}.

Now
\lambda\in\Gamma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}^{\prime\prime}}^{n}(V)
if and only if
v(\lambda)\leq{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
, so if and only if

(1-|\lambda|)^{2}\leq({\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n})^{2}(1-|\lambda|).

(8.11)

If
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}\geq 1
, which is the case for
n=1
and
2
, then (
8.11
) holds if and only if
|\lambda|\leq 1
. If
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}<1
, which is the case for
n\geq 3
, then (
8.11
) holds if and only if
1-({\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n})^{2}\leq|\lambda|\leq 1
. Similarly,
\lambda\in\gamma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}^{\prime\prime}}^{n}(V)
if and only if
v(\lambda)<{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
, which holds if and only if
|\lambda|<1
for
n=1
, if and only if
1-({\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n})^{2}<|\lambda|<1
, for
n\geq 2
(note that
{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{2}=1
).

8.2
Block-Laurent operators

Suppose now, generalising the shift operator example of §
8.1
, that
X={\mathbb{C}}^{p}
, for some
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and that
A
is given by (
1.15
) with each diagonal constant, so that, for some
\widehat{\alpha}
,
\widehat{\beta}
,
\widehat{\gamma}\in L(X)={\mathbb{C}}^{p\times p}
,
\alpha_{j}=\widehat{\alpha}
,
\beta_{j}=\widehat{\beta}
, and
\gamma_{j}=\widehat{\gamma}
, for
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, i.e.,
A
is a
block-Laurent
operator (a
Laurent
operator in the case
p=1
). Generalising the notation of §
8.1
, let
V\in L(E)
denote the forward shift operator, so that
(Vx)_{j}=x_{j+1}
, for
x\in E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
and
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then
12
12

12

In the following and similar expressions
\widehat{\alpha}
,
\widehat{\beta}
, and
\widehat{\gamma}
are to be understood as operating pointwise on the elements of a given
x=(x_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\in E
, so that, e.g.,
(\widehat{\alpha}x)_{i}:=\widehat{\alpha}x_{i}
, for
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

A=\widehat{\alpha}V^{1}+\widehat{\beta}V^{0}+\widehat{\gamma}V^{-1}=a(V),

where
a
is the
Laurent polynomial

a(z):=\widehat{\alpha}z^{1}+\widehat{\beta}z^{0}+\widehat{\gamma}z^{-1},\qquad z\in{\mathbb{T}}.

It is well-known that
A
is unitarily equivalent to multiplication by
a
, the
symbol
of
A
. Precisely (see, e.g., Theorem 4.4.9 and its proof in
[
30
]
), the Fourier operator
{\mathcal{F}}:E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)\to L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},X)
, defined by

({\mathcal{F}}x)(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}x_{j}z^{-j},\qquad z\in{\mathbb{T}},

is unitary, and

A={\mathcal{F}}^{-1}M_{a}{\mathcal{F}},

where
M_{a}:L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},X)\to L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},X)
is the operation of multiplication by
a\in C({\mathbb{T}},L(X))
, i.e.
(M_{a}\phi)(z)=a(z)\phi(z)
, for
\phi\in L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},X)
,
z\in{\mathbb{T}}
. Thus
[
30
, Theorem 4.4.9]

{\rm Spec}\,A={\rm Spec}\,M_{a}=\bigcup_{z\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\rm Spec}\,a(z),

(8.12)

and, similarly,

{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A={\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}M_{a}=\bigcup_{z\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}a(z)\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A={\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}M_{a}=\bigcup_{z\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}a(z),\quad{\varepsilon}>0.

(8.13)

In this block-Laurent case similar spectral computations hold for the matrices
A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}
, given by (
1.20
), that arise in the
\pi
method. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, and all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}\ =\ \widehat{\alpha}V_{n,t}^{1}+\widehat{\beta}V_{n,t}^{0}+\widehat{\gamma}V_{n,t}^{-1}\ =\ a(V_{n,t})

where

V_{n,t}\ :=\ \begin{pmatrix}0&&&tI_{X}\\
I_{X}&0\\
&\ddots&\ddots\\
&&I_{X}&0\end{pmatrix}_{\!\!n\times n}.

A simple computation shows that, for any
s\in{\mathbb{T}}
with
s^{n}=t
(cf. the calculation in Example
1.4
),

V_{n,t}\ =\ sD_{n}^{-1}V_{n,1}D_{n}\qquad\text{with}\qquad D_{n}:={\rm diag}(s^{1}I_{X},s^{2}I_{X},\dots,s^{n}I_{X}),

so that

A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}\ =\ a(V_{n,t})\ =\ a(sD_{n}^{-1}V_{n,1}D_{n})\ =\ D_{n}^{-1}a(sV_{n,1})D_{n},

(8.14)

so that
A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}
is unitarily equivalent to
a(sV_{n,1})
. Moreover (see, e.g.,
[
32
]
,
[
83
, §II.7]
),

V_{n,1}
can be diagonalized as
V_{n,1}={\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{n}M_{n}{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
, where
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}:X^{n}\to X^{n}
is the discrete Fourier transform

{\mathcal{F}}_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\omega^{jk}I_{X}\right)_{j,k=1}^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad M_{n}:={\rm diag}(\omega^{1}I_{X},\omega^{2}I_{X},\dots,\omega^{n}I_{X})\quad\text{with}\quad\omega:=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi i}{n}\right).

Combining this observation with (
8.14
), we see that

A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}\ =\ D_{n}^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{n}\ a(sM_{n})\ {\mathcal{F}}_{n}D_{n}\quad\mbox{with}\quad a(sM_{n})\ =\ {\rm diag}(a(s\,\omega^{1}),\ldots,a(s\,\omega^{n})),

(8.15)

in particular that
A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}
is unitarily equivalent to
a(sM_{n})
.
Since

\{\omega^{1},\omega^{2},\dots,\omega^{n}\}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:z^{n}=1\}={\mathbb{T}}_{n}
, we see, similarly to (
8.12
) and (
8.13
), that

{\rm Spec}\,A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}={\rm Spec}\,a(sM_{n})=\bigcup_{z\in s{\mathbb{T}}_{n}}{\rm Spec}\,a(z),

(8.16)

and that, for
{\varepsilon}>0
,

{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}=\bigcup_{z\in s{\mathbb{T}}_{n}}{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}a(z)\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}=\bigcup_{z\in s{\mathbb{T}}_{n}}{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}a(z).

(8.17)

Note that the set
s{\mathbb{T}}_{n}
is independent of which root
s
we choose as the solution of
s^{n}=t
.

The above observations lead, applying Theorem
1.4
, to the following result about convergence of the
\pi
method in the case that
A
is block-Laurent. Of course, we already have, as a consequence of Theorem
1.2
, that the
\tau_{1}
method is convergent (recall that
X
has Globevnik’s property as it is finite-dimensional), and we know, from the example in §
8.1
, that the
\tau
method is not convergent for tridiagonal block–Laurent operators in general, even in the special case that
X={\mathbb{C}}
.

Theorem 8.1

If
X={\mathbb{C}}^{p}
, equipped with the Euclidean norm, and
A\in L(E)
is a tridiagonal block-Laurent operator, then, for every
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
,
A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}=A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t}
,

\Pi^{n,t}_{\varepsilon}(A)\ =\ {\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t},\quad\mbox{for }{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\pi^{n,t}_{\varepsilon}(A)\ =\ {\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t},\quad\mbox{for }{\varepsilon}>0.

(8.18)

Further,
the
\pi
method does not suffer from spectral pollution for
A
for all
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
; indeed, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\varepsilon}>0
,

{\rm Spec}\,A\ =\ \bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\rm Spec}\,A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t},\quad{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ =\ \bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t},\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ =\ \bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t},

(8.19)

so that, as
n\to\infty
,

\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)\ =\ {\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t}\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\;\;\mbox{ for }{\varepsilon}\geq 0,

in particular,
\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)={\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t}\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
, and

\pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)\ =\ {\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t}\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\;\;\mbox{ for }{\varepsilon}>0.

Proof.
That
A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}=A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t}
, for
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and the representations (
8.18
), are clear from the definitions (
1.20
) and (
1.23
) of
A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}
,
\Pi^{n,t}_{\varepsilon}(A)
, and
\pi^{n,t}_{\varepsilon}(A)
. That (
8.19
) holds follows by comparing (
8.12
) and (
8.13
) with (
8.16
) and (
8.17
), noting that
\cup_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}(s{\mathbb{T}}_{n})={\mathbb{T}}
. The second of the equalities in (
8.19
) implies, by Definition
1.1
, that, for every
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, the
\pi
method does not suffer from spectral pollution for
A
. The remaining results follow from (
8.18
) and Theorem
1.4
, noting that
X
satisfies Globevnik’s property (see §
2
) since
X
is finite-dimensional.

8.3
Periodic tridiagonal matrices

Suppose
A
is given by (
1.15
) with
X={\mathbb{C}}
so that
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
,
with all three diagonals
p
-periodic for some
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then
A

can be understood as a block-Laurent operator, so as to make use of the results from the previous section. Precisely, as in our discussion around (
1.14
), define
E_{p}:=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X^{p})
, equipping
X^{p}={\mathbb{C}}^{p}
with the Euclidean norm (
1.14
), and let
A_{p}:={\mathcal{I}}_{p}^{-1}V^{k}AV^{-k}{\mathcal{I}}_{p}
, for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, where
V
is the shift operator as defined in §
8.1
. Then
A_{p}\in L(E_{p})
is a tridiagonal block-Laurent operator with constant entries on the diagonals,
\widehat{\alpha}
,
\widehat{\beta}
,
\widehat{\gamma}\in L(X)={\mathbb{C}}^{p\times p}
, where

(\widehat{\alpha}\,|\,\widehat{\beta}\,|\,\widehat{\gamma})\ =\ \left(\begin{array}[]{cccc|cccc|cccc}&&&\alpha_{p+k}&\beta_{1+k}&\gamma_{2+k}&&&&&&\\
&&&&\alpha_{1+k}&\beta_{2+k}&\ddots&&&&&\\
&&&&&\ddots&\ddots&\gamma_{p+k}&&&&\\
&&&&&&\alpha_{p+k-1}&\beta_{p+k}&\gamma_{1+k}&&&\end{array}\right)_{p\times 3p}.

Noting that the
n\times n
matrix
(A_{p})^{{\sf per},t}_{n,0}
with entries in
{\mathbb{C}}^{p\times p}
is identical to the
(np)\times(np)
matrix
A^{{\sf per},t}_{np,k}
, we have the following corollary of Theorem
8.1
. The first equation of (
8.20
) is well-known (see
[
8
,
9
,
30
]
). In the case
n=1
it expresses
{\rm Spec}\,A
as the union over
t
of the eigenvalues of the
p\times p
matrix
{\rm Spec}\,A_{p,k}^{{\sf per},t}
.

Corollary 8.2

Suppose that
X={\mathbb{C}}
and
A\in L(E)
is tridiagonal with, for some
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\alpha_{j+p}=\alpha_{j}
,
\beta_{j+p}=\beta_{j}
,
\gamma_{j+p}=\gamma_{j}
, for
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then, for every
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
{\varepsilon}>0
,

{\rm Spec}\,A\ =\ \bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\rm Spec}\,A_{pn,k}^{{\sf per},t},\quad{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ =\ \bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{pn,k}^{{\sf per},t},\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ =\ \bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{T}}}{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{pn,k}^{{\sf per},t}.

(8.20)

Further, as
n\to\infty
, if
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, then

{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}A_{pn,k}^{{\sf per},t}\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\;\;\mbox{ for }{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\quad\mbox{in particular}\quad{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}A_{pn,k}^{{\sf per},t}\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm Spec}\,A,

and

{\rm spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}A_{pn,k}^{{\sf per},t}\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\;\;\mbox{ for }{\varepsilon}>0.

The above result implies that, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
and
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
,

\Pi^{pn,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)\ =\ \bigcup_{k=1}^{p}{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}A_{pn,k}^{{\sf per},t}\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A,

as
n\to\infty
, and similarly that
\pi^{pn,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, for
{\varepsilon}>0
. A natural question is: does
\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, as
n\to\infty
, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
? I.e., does the whole sequence converge and not just the subsequence
\Pi^{pn,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)
? The answer is not necessarily!

As an example where the whole sequence does not converge, suppose that
A
has
p
-periodic diagonals with
p=2
, and that the entries of
A
are chosen so that the block-Laurent operator
A_{p}={\mathcal{I}}_{p}^{-1}A{\mathcal{I}}_{p}
has
\widehat{\alpha}=\widehat{\gamma}=0
and

\widehat{\beta}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right),

so that
A_{2}
is block diagonal and
A_{2}
and
A
are self-adjoint. Then
{\rm Spec}\,A={\rm Spec}\,A_{2}={\rm Spec}\,\widehat{\beta}=\{-1,1\}
; indeed, since
A
is normal, we have further that
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A={\rm Spec}\,A+{\varepsilon}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}=\{-1,1\}+{\varepsilon}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
. But, if
n
is odd, then, for
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, the last column of
A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t}
is zero, so that
0\in{\rm Spec}\,A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t}
, so that (see §
2
)
{\varepsilon}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\subset{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,0}^{{\sf per},t}
, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
. Thus
({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n})\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\subset\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)
, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
, in particular
({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}){\rm i}\in\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)
. Thus, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
,

d_{H}(\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A),{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A)\geq{\rm dist}(({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}){\rm i},{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A)=\sqrt{1+({\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n})^{2}}-{\varepsilon}\geq\sqrt{1+{\varepsilon}^{2}}-{\varepsilon},

so
\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)
does not converge to
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
.

On the other hand, the numerical results in Figures
8.1
and
8.2
, both examples with
p=3
, suggest that there are periodic operators for which the whole sequence does converge. It appears in both figures that
\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
(note that the values of
n
we choose are not multiples of 3). Note also, as predicted by Theorem
1.2
, that
\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
. Further, it appears in these two examples (and also in Example
1.4
) that
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
, the
\tau
method inclusion set, converges to
\widehat{{\rm Spec}\,}A
, as
n\to\infty
, where
\widehat{\rm Spec}\,A
denotes the complement of the unbounded component of
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\rm Spec}\,A
.

n=16

n=32

n=64

n=128

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{$\tau$ method}}}{{\rule{0.0pt}{28.45274pt}}}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{$\pi$ method}}}{{\rule{0.0pt}{28.45274pt}}}

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{$\tau_{1}$ method}}}{{\rule{0.0pt}{28.45274pt}}}

The
\tau
,

, and

inclusion sets,

,

, and
\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)
, respectively, for
n=16,\,32,\,64
, and
128
, in the case that
\alpha=(\cdots,0,0,0,\cdots)
,

\beta=(\cdots,-\frac{3}{2},1,1,\cdots)
and
\gamma=(\cdots,1,2,1,\cdots)
are
3
-periodic. These are each inclusion sets for
{\rm Spec}\,A
, given by the first equation of (
8.20
) with
n=1
, and shown in each figure as the red curve.

and

are plotted in Matlab, using Matlab’s inbuilt contouring routine. In particular, the plots of
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)

is, by Proposition

and (

), and recalling (

), the contour on which

\mu_{n}^{\dagger}(A-\lambda I)=\min_{k=1,\ldots,p}\nu(A_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n})=\left(\min_{k=1,\ldots,p}\min{\rm Spec}\left((A_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n})^{\prime}(A_{n,k}-\lambda I_{n})\right)\right)^{1/2}

(8.21)

has the value
{\varepsilon}_{n}
. This contour is determined by Matlab’s contouring routine, interpolating values of the right hand side of (
8.21
) plotted on a fine grid of values of
\lambda
covering the respective regions shown in Figures

and

.

, and (

) for
\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)
.
