8.4
Pseudoergodic and random operators

Let
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
and

\Sigma_{\gamma}
be nonempty compact subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
and let
A
be
of the form (
1.15
) with
\alpha_{j}\in\Sigma_{\alpha}
,

\beta_{j}\in\Sigma_{\beta}
and
\gamma_{j}\in\Sigma_{\gamma}
for all

j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Following Davies
[
28
]
(and see
[
17
]
or
[
19
, Definition 1.1]
) we say that
A
is
pseudoergodic with respect to
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}

if, for all
{\varepsilon}>0
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and all tridiagonal
matrices
B_{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
with subdiagonal entries in

\Sigma_{\alpha}
, main diagonal entries in
\Sigma_{\beta}
,
and superdiagonal entries in
\Sigma_{\gamma}
,
there exists a
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\|A_{n,k}-B_{n}\|<{\varepsilon}
. We say that
A
is
pseudoergodic
if, for some non-empty compact
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
,
A
is pseudoergodic with respect to
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
.
Pseudoergodicity was introduced by Davies
[
28
]
to study spectral properties of random operators while eliminating probabilistic arguments. Indeed (see
[
28
, §5]
), if the matrix entries in (
1.15
) are independent (or at least not fully correlated) random variables with probability measures whose supports are
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
, then, by the usual probabilistic arguments (sometimes termed the
infinite monkey theorem
), the operator
A
is pseudoergodic almost surely.

The study of random and pseudoergodic operators, even restricting attention to the tridiagonal case that we consider here, has a large literature, both in the self-adjoint and, more recently, in the non-self-adjoint case. A readable, short introduction is
[
83
, §36, §37]
, and see
[
17
]
and the references therein; see also
[
62
, §3.4.10]
or
[
12
]
for pseudoergodicity interpreted in the language of limit operators. Falling within the scope of the results of this section are:

1.

Random (self-adjoint, bi-infinite) Jacobi operators, including discrete Schrödinger operators with random bounded potentials (so
\Sigma_{\alpha}=\Sigma_{\gamma}=\{1\}
and
\Sigma_{\beta}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
), including cases exhibiting Anderson localisation (see, e.g.,
[
71
]
and the references therein).

2.

The (bi-infinite) non-self-adjoint version of the Anderson model,
with applications to superconductors and biological growth problems, introduced by Hatano & Nelson

[
54
,
55
,
56
]
. In this case
\Sigma_{\alpha}
and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
are real singleton sets (the
\alpha
and
\gamma
diagonals are constant) and
\Sigma_{\beta}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
. For this model the behaviour of
{\rm Spec}\,A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},1}
as
n\to\infty
has been described by Goldsheid & Khoruzhenko

[
45
,
46
]
,
{\rm Spec}\,A
has been studied by a variety of methods in Davies

[
27
,
28
,
29
]

and Martínez
[
66
,
67
]
, and approximations to
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
have been computed by Colbrook
[
19
]
, via the representation he establishes that
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{2n+1,-n-1}^{{\sf per},1}
.

3.

The so-called Feinberg-Zee random hopping matrix, the case
\Sigma_{\alpha}=\{-1,1\}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}=\{0\}
,

\Sigma_{\gamma}=\{1\}
, which has been studied in
[
36
,
37
,
59
,
11
,
12
,
14
,
49
,
50
,
15
]
, and which we will return to below; also, its generalisation to the case
\Sigma_{\alpha}=\{\pm\sigma\}
, for some
\sigma\in(0,1)
, in Chandler-Wilde & Davies
[
14
]
.

4.

The special case when
A
is bidiagonal, meaning that
\Sigma_{\alpha}=\{0\}
or
\Sigma_{\gamma}=\{0\}
; see
[
82
,
63
]
.

Given non-empty compact sets
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
,
\Sigma_{\gamma}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
, let
M(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})
denote the set of operators
A
of the form (
1.15
) such that
\alpha_{j}\in\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\beta_{j}\in\Sigma_{\beta}
,
\gamma_{j}\in\Sigma_{\gamma}
, for
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. The key result in the theory of pseudoergodic operators is the following: if
A
is pseudoergodic with respect to
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
, then

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ =\ \bigcup_{B\in M(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})}{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B,\quad{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\quad{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ =\ \bigcup_{B\in M(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})}{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B,\quad{\varepsilon}>0.

(8.22)

The first of these equalities in the case
{\varepsilon}=0
dates back to
[
28
]
; the second is shown analogously as
[
17
, Corollary 4.12]
; the first for
{\varepsilon}>0
then follows by taking closures in the second identity
13
13

13

In more detail, taking closures in the second identity, since
\overline{{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B}={\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B
, for any tridiagonal
B
with scalar entries, we see that
\bigcup_{B\in M(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})}{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B\subset\overline{\bigcup_{B\in M(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})}{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B}={\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
. Since
A\in M(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})
, also
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\subset\bigcup_{B\in M(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})}{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B
.
.

Clearly, (
8.22
) implies that if
B\in M(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})
then
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}B\subset{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
, and
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B\subset{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, for
{\varepsilon}>0
. In particular this is true if
B
is a Laurent operator, i.e. if, for some
\widehat{\alpha}\ \in\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\widehat{\beta}\in\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\widehat{\gamma}\in\Sigma_{\gamma}
,
\alpha_{j}=\widehat{\alpha}
,
\beta_{j}=\widehat{\beta}
,
\gamma_{j}=\widehat{\gamma}
, for
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, in which case, by (
8.19
) with
n=1
,

{\rm Spec}\,B={\mathcal{E}}(\widehat{\alpha},\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\gamma}):=\{z\widehat{\alpha}+\widehat{\beta}+z^{-1}\widehat{\gamma}:z\in{\mathbb{T}}\},

which is an ellipse in the complex plane (degenerating to a line if
|\widehat{\alpha}|=|\widehat{\gamma}|
). It will be key to the proof that the
\pi
method does not suffer from spectral pollution, in Theorem
8.3
below, that also
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B\subset{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, if
B\in M(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})
and the coefficients
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
of
B
are periodic, so that Corollary
8.2
applies.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{\cap}
denote the set of
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
which are in the interior of
{\mathcal{E}}(\widehat{\alpha},\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\gamma})
, for every
\widehat{\alpha}\ \in\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\widehat{\beta}\in\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\widehat{\gamma}\in\Sigma_{\gamma}
, and let

\alpha_{*}:=\min_{z\in\Sigma_{\alpha}}|z|,\;\;\alpha^{*}:=\max_{z\in\Sigma_{\alpha}}|z|,\;\;\gamma_{*}:=\min_{z\in\Sigma_{\gamma}}|z|,\;\;\gamma^{*}:=\max_{z\in\Sigma_{\gamma}}|z|.

(8.23)

The main theoretical result of this section is the following. Recall, as is the case for any tridiagonal
A\in L(E)
(irrespective of pseudoergodicity), that, in addition to the results shown in this theorem, the inclusions (
M

τ

), (
M

π

), and (
M

τ
1

) apply to
A
.

Theorem 8.3

Suppose that
X={\mathbb{C}}
, so that
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
, that
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
,
\Sigma_{\gamma}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
are non-empty and compact, and that
A
is pseudoergodic with respect to
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
. Then the
\pi
method does not suffer from spectral pollution for
A
for any
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
. Further, the
\tau
method does not suffer from spectral pollution for
A
if either: (a)
\gamma_{*}\leq\alpha^{*}
and
\alpha_{*}\leq\gamma^{*}
; or (b)
E_{\cap}
is empty. Moreover, as
n\to\infty
,

\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\quad{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\quad{\varepsilon}>0;

(8.24)

if
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, also

\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\quad{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\quad{\varepsilon}>0.

(8.25)

Further, if (a) or (b) holds, also

\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\quad{\varepsilon}\geq 0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\quad{\varepsilon}>0.

(8.26)

Proof.
For
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, and
{\varepsilon}>0
, applying Corollary
8.2
, we see that
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}\subset{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B
for the unique
B\in M(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})
with
n
-periodic coefficients that has
B_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}=A_{n,k}^{{\sf per},t}
. But
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}B\subset{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
by (
8.22
). Thus the
\pi
method does not suffer from spectral pollution for
A
for any
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
. Let
E_{+}:=\ell_{2}({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{C}})
and suppose that
A_{+}\in L(E_{+})
is tridiagonal and pseudoergodic, i.e.,
A_{+}\in\Psi E_{+}(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})
, in the notation of
[
17
, §1]
. Then, for
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
{\varepsilon}>0
,
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{n,k}\subset{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{+}
, by
[
17
, Corollary 4.14 b)]
. Moreoever,
{\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{+}={\rm spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, by
[
17
, Proposition 4.13 c)]
, if (a) or (b) hold. Thus, if (a) or (b) hold, the
\tau
method does not suffer from spectral pollution for
A
.

Noting that
X={\mathbb{C}}
trivially has the Globevnik property, (
8.24
) follows from Theorem
1.2
, (
8.25
) follows from Theorem
1.4
, and, provided that (a) or (b) holds, (
8.26
) follows from Theorem
1.3
.

Remark 8.4

In the case of the Feinberg-Zee random hopping matrix, i.e. the case that
\Sigma_{\alpha}=\{-1,1\}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}=\{0\}
,
\Sigma_{\gamma}=\{1\}
, we have that
\alpha_{*}=\alpha^{*}=\gamma_{*}=\gamma^{*}=1
, so that (a) holds in the above theorem and
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
. This result, for the specific Feinberg-Zee random hopping case, was shown previously, starting from (
M

τ

), in
[
12
, Theorem 4.9]
.

Remark 8.5
We note that Colbrook in
[
19
]
has proved results similar to the results in the above theorem for the
\pi
method, by similar arguments. He shows in
[
19
]
, for very general classes of pseudoergodic operators
A
, including tridiagonal pseudoergodic operators as defined here, that the pseudospectra of periodised finite section approximations converge to those of
A
, precisely that

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A_{2n+1,-n-1}^{{\sf per},1}\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A,\quad\mbox{as}\quad n\to\infty,

(8.27)

for every
{\varepsilon}>0
.

8.4.1
The case that
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
are finite sets

When
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
are finite sets, as holds, for example, in the Feinberg-Zee case, then the infinite collections of finite matrices, indexed by
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, in the definitions (
1.21
), (
1.23
), and (
1.27
)/(
1.30
)
of the
inclusion sets
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
,
\sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
,
\Pi^{n,t}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)
,
\pi^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}}(A)
,
\Gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)
, and
\gamma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}(A)
, are in fact (large) finite sets of matrices. Let
M_{n}(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})
denote the set of all
n\times n
tridiagonal matrices with subdiagonal entries in
\Sigma_{\alpha}
, diagonal entries in
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and superdiagonal entries in
\Sigma_{\gamma}
. This set has cardinality
N_{\alpha}^{n-1}N_{\beta}^{n}N_{\gamma}^{n-1}
, where

N_{\alpha}:=|\Sigma_{\alpha}|
,
N_{\beta}:=|\Sigma_{\beta}|
, and
N_{\gamma}:=|\Sigma_{\gamma}|
. Further, recalling the notation (
6.9
), it is easy to see that, if
A
is pseudoergodic with respect to
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
, then

{\mathcal{S}}^{(\tau)}_{n}:=\{A_{n,k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}=M_{n}(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma}).

(8.28)

Similarly, where
B^{t}_{n}(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\gamma})
, for some fixed
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, denotes the set of all
n\times n
matrices
B
with
B_{ij}=\delta_{i,1}\delta_{j,n}t\widehat{\alpha}+\delta_{i,n}\delta_{j,1}\overline{t}\widehat{\gamma}
,
i,j=1,\ldots,n
, for some
\widehat{\alpha}\in\Sigma_{\alpha}
and
\widehat{\gamma}\in\Sigma_{\gamma}
, it holds that

{\mathcal{S}}^{(\pi)}_{n}:=\{A^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}=M_{n}(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})+B^{t}_{n}(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\gamma}),

(8.29)

which set has cardinality no larger than
N_{\alpha}^{n}N_{\beta}^{n}N_{\gamma}^{n}
, indeed exactly this cardinality for
n\geq 3
.

In the Feinberg-Zee case these sets have cardinalities
|{\mathcal{S}}^{(\tau)}_{n}|=2^{n-1}
and
|{\mathcal{S}}^{(\pi)}_{n}|=2^{n}
, respectively. Thus, given
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
, determining whether
\lambda\in\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
requires half the computation of determining whether
\lambda\in\Pi^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
. Since both these inclusion sets converge to
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
, by Theorem
8.3
and Remark
8.4
, this suggests computing
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
rather than
\Pi^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
, as an approximation to
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
.

In Figure
8.3
, taken from
[
12
]
, we plot
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\supset{\rm Spec}\,A
for
n=6
,
12
, and
18
. Concretely, by (
1.26
), (
7.5
),
and (
8.28
),

\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\min_{B\in M_{n}(\{\pm 1\},\{0\},\{1\})}\nu(B-\lambda I_{n})\leq{\varepsilon}_{n}\right\}

and, by (
7.4
),

\nu(B-\lambda I_{n})\leq{\varepsilon}_{n}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad{\varepsilon}_{n}^{2}I_{n}-(B-\lambda I_{n})^{\prime}(B-\lambda I_{n})\;\;\mbox{ is positive semi-definite}.

Thus, to decide whether
\lambda\in\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
one has to decide whether or not
2^{n-1}
Hermitian matrices of size
n\times n
are positive semi-definite. For
n=18
this requires consideration of the positive definiteness of 131,072 matrices of size
18\times 18
for each
\lambda
on some grid covering the domain of interest.

Figure 8.3:
Plots in black of
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
, for
n=6
,
12
, and
18
, in the Feinberg-Zee case that
A
is pseudoergodic with respect to
\Sigma_{\alpha}=\{-1,1\}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}=\{0\}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}=\{1\}
.
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
is an inclusion set for
{\rm Spec}\,A
for each
n
, by (
M

τ

), and
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
, by Theorem
8.3
and Remark
8.4
. Shown in red in each plot is the closure of the numerical range of
A
, which (see
[
12
, Lemma 3.1]
) is the square with corners at
\pm 2
and
\pm 2{\rm i}
. This is also an inclusion set for
{\rm Spec}\,A
(see §
1.5
). Overlaid in blue in each plot is the set
\pi_{30}\cup{\mathbb{D}}
, where
\pi_{n}
(which can be computed by Corollary
8.2
) is the union of
{\rm Spec}\,B
over all
B\in M(\{-1,1\},\{0\},\{1\})
that are periodic with period
p\leq n
. For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\pi_{n}\subset{\rm Spec}\,A
by (
8.22
), and
{\mathbb{D}}\subset{\rm Spec}\,A
by
[
11
, Theorem 2.3]
.

In Figure
8.3
we also plot
\overline{\mathrm{Num}\,A}
, the closure of the numerical range of
A
, computed in
[
12
]
to be the square
S={\rm conv}\{\pm 2,\pm 2{\rm i}\}
. Disappointingly,
\overline{\mathrm{Num}\,A}
is a sharper inclusion set than
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)
, at least for these modest values of
n
, i.e.
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\supset S=\overline{\mathrm{Num}\,A}\supset{\rm Spec}\,A
, for
n=6
,
12
,
18
. If the inclusion
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\supset\overline{\mathrm{Num}\,A}
held for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
it would follow, since
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\overline{\mathrm{Num}\,A}
and
\Sigma^{n}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
, that
{\rm Spec}\,A=\overline{\mathrm{Num}\,A}=S
. To show that this is not the case we carried out calculations in
[
12
]
to demonstrate that
\lambda\not\in\Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(A)
for
n=34
and
\lambda=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}{\rm i}
, a point on the boundary of the square
S
. This calculation is a matter of checking that
2^{33}\approx 8.6\times 10^{9}
matrices of size
34\times 34
are positive definite. This is a large calculation, but one suited to a massively parallel computer architecture, with each processor assigned a subset of the nearly 9 billion matrices to check their positive definiteness. We note also that this calculation is an example of using our inclusion sets to show that a particular operator is invertible (see Remark
1.3.3
). These calculations, showing that, for this particular choice of
\lambda
,
\lambda\not\in\Sigma_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}(A)
for
n=34
, prove that
\lambda\not\in{\rm Spec}\,A
, i.e.
\lambda I-A
is invertible.
For more information about these calculations and those in Figure
8.3
, see
[
12
]
. For a review of what is known about
{\rm Spec}\,A
for this Feinberg-Zee case, and a list of challenging open problems, see
[
15
]
.

8.4.2
The case that
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
are infinite sets, and the SCI

Consider now the case that the sets
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
are not necessarily finite. (We have in mind applications where one or more of these sets is infinite, or all the sets are finite but the cardinality of
M_{n}(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})
is unfeasibly large for computation as in the previous subsection.) It is easy to see that, if
A
is pseudoergodic with respect to
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
, then, where
{\mathcal{S}}^{(\tau)}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{(\pi)}
are defined by (
8.28
) and (
8.29
),

\overline{{\mathcal{S}}^{(\tau)}_{n}}=\overline{\{A_{n,k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}}=M_{n}(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})

(8.30)

and, for the chosen fixed
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
,

\overline{{\mathcal{S}}^{(\pi)}_{n}}=\overline{\{A^{{\sf per},t}_{n,k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}}=M_{n}(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\beta},\Sigma_{\gamma})+B^{t}_{n}(\Sigma_{\alpha},\Sigma_{\gamma}).

(8.31)

(These equations are equivalent to (
8.28
) and (
8.29
) in the case that
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
are all finite.)

This is a case where it is relatively clear how to select a finite set
S_{n}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{S}}^{(M)}_{n}}
, for
M=\tau
or
\pi
, such that (
6.10
) holds, so that the approximations to
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
proposed in Theorem
6.3
can be computed. Indeed, suppose that we can find sequences of finite sets
(\Sigma^{n}_{\alpha})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
(\Sigma^{n}_{\beta})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
(\Sigma^{n}_{\gamma})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset\Sigma_{\gamma}
such that

d_{H}(\Sigma_{G},\Sigma^{n}_{G})\leq\frac{1}{3n},\qquad\mbox{for }\;\;n\in{\mathbb{N}},\;\;\mbox{ and }G\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}.

(8.32)

Then
S_{n}=S_{n}^{(M)}
, as defined in the following lemma, is finite and satisfies (
6.10
).

Lemma 8.6

Suppose that
A
is pseudoergodic with respect to
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
, and set, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

S^{(M)}_{n}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}M_{n}(\Sigma^{n}_{\alpha},\Sigma^{n}_{\beta},\Sigma^{n}_{\gamma}),&\mbox{if $M=\tau$},\\
M_{n}(\Sigma^{n}_{\alpha},\Sigma^{n}_{\beta},\Sigma^{n}_{\gamma})+B^{t}_{n}(\Sigma^{n}_{\alpha},\Sigma^{n}_{\gamma}),&\mbox{if $M=\pi$.}\end{array}\right.

Then
S^{(M)}_{n}
is a finite subset of
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{(M)}}
and (
6.10
) holds with
S_{n}=S_{n}^{(M)}
.

Proof.

Let
M=\tau
or
\pi
and let
S_{n}:=S_{n}^{(M)}
. It is clear that
S_{n}
is finite, since
\Sigma^{n}_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma^{n}_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma^{n}_{\gamma}
are finite. Further, for
G=\alpha
,
\beta
, or
\gamma
, and every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
z\in\Sigma_{G}
, there exists
z_{n}\in\Sigma^{n}_{G}
such that
|z-z_{n}|\leq 1/(3n)
. But this implies, for every
B\in{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{(M)}=\{A_{n,k}^{(M)}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
, where
A_{n,k}^{(M)}
is defined by (
6.8
), that there exists
B^{n}\in S_{n}
such that
|B_{ij}-B^{n}_{ij}|\leq 1/(3n)
, for
i,j=1,\ldots,n
, where
B_{ij}
and
B^{n}_{ij}
denote the elements in row
i
and column
j
of
B
and
B^{n}
, respectively. But this implies that
\sum_{j=1}^{n}|B_{ij}-B^{n}_{ij}|\leq 1/n
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|B_{ij}-B^{n}_{ij}|\leq 1/n
, which imply that
\|B-B_{n}\|\leq 1/n
.

Combining the above lemma with Theorem
6.3
and with the results on absence of spectral pollution in Theorem
8.3
, we have immediately the following result, noting that the definition (
8.33
) coincides with (
6.12
) by (
7.3
).

Corollary 8.7

Suppose that
A
is pseudoergodic with respect to
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
, and that
S^{(M)}_{n}
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
M=\tau
or
\pi
, is defined as in Lemma
8.6
, where
(\Sigma^{n}_{\alpha})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
(\Sigma^{n}_{\beta})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
(\Sigma^{n}_{\gamma})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset\Sigma_{\gamma}
are finite sets satisfying (
8.32
). Further, let

\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},M}_{{\varepsilon}}(A):=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}:\min_{B\in S^{(M)}_{n}}\nu\left(B-\lambda I_{n}\right)\leq{\varepsilon}\right\},\quad\mbox{for }n\in{\mathbb{N}},\;\;M=\tau\mbox{ or }\pi,\mbox{ and }{\varepsilon}\geq 0.

(8.33)

Then, for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},\pi}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}+1/n}(A),

(8.34)

and

\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},\pi}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}+1/n}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
, in particular
\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},\pi}_{{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n}+1/n}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
. Moreover, if conditions (a) or (b) in Theorem
8.3
are satisfied, than also

{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A\ \subset\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},\tau}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}+1/n}(A),

(8.35)

for
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and

\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},\tau}_{{\varepsilon}+{\varepsilon}_{n}+1/n}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
as
n\to\infty
, in particular
\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},\tau}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}+1/n}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
.

As we did in §
1.3.5
and §
7
, we can go beyond the above corollary and define a sequence of approximations
(\Pi^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
to
{\rm Spec}\,A
, when
A
is pseudoergodic, such that each
\Pi^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
is a finite set that can be computed in finitely many arithmetic operations, and
\Pi^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
.

Let
\Omega_{\Psi}
denote the set of all tridiagonal matrices (
1.15
), with
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{C}})
, that are pseudoergodic. For a given
A\in\Omega_{\Psi}
, with coefficients
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\ell^{\infty}({\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{C}})
, let

\Sigma_{\alpha}:=\overline{\{\alpha_{j}:j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}},\quad\Sigma_{\beta}:=\overline{\{\beta_{j}:j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}},\quad\Sigma_{\gamma}:=\overline{\{\gamma_{j}:j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}}.

Note that, with these definitions,
A
is pseudoergodic with respect to
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
.
As in §
1.3.5
, let
P(S)
denote the power set of a set
S
. The inputs we need to compute
\Pi^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, are provided by the following mappings (cf. §
1.3.5
):

1.

A mapping
{\mathcal{B}}:\Omega_{\Psi}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
,
A\mapsto(\alpha_{\max},\beta_{\max},\gamma_{\max})
, such that
\alpha_{\max}\geq\|\alpha\|_{\infty}
,
\beta_{\max}\geq\|\beta\|_{\infty}
,
\gamma_{\max}\geq\|\gamma\|_{\infty}
.

2.

A mapping
{\mathcal{E}}:\Omega_{\Psi}\times{\mathbb{N}}\to(P({\mathbb{C}}))^{3}
,
(A,n)\mapsto(\Sigma_{\alpha}^{n},\Sigma_{\beta}^{n},\Sigma_{\gamma}^{n})
, such that
\Sigma^{n}_{\alpha}\subset\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma^{n}_{\beta}\subset\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma^{n}_{\gamma}\subset\Sigma_{\gamma}
are finite sets satisfying (
8.32
).

Note that
{\mathcal{B}}
is the mapping
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
of §
1.3.5
, restricted to
\Omega_{\Psi}\subset\Omega_{T}^{1}
.

Given
A\in\Omega_{\Psi}
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
let (cf. (
4.23
))

{\varepsilon}_{n}^{\dagger}\ :=\ (\alpha_{\max}+\gamma_{\max})\,\frac{22}{7n}\ \geq\ 2(\alpha_{\max}+\gamma_{\max})\,\sin\frac{\pi}{2n}\geq{\varepsilon}^{\prime}_{n},

(8.36)

where

(\alpha_{\max},\beta_{\max},\gamma_{\max}):={\mathcal{B}}(A)
.
Recalling the definition (
1.47
), the sequence
(\Pi^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is given by

\Pi^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A):=\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},\pi}_{{\varepsilon}^{\dagger}_{n}+3/n}(A)\cap\mathrm{Grid}(n,R),\qquad n\in{\mathbb{N}},

(8.37)

where
R:=\alpha_{\max}+\beta_{\max}+\gamma_{\max}
(so that
R
is an upper bound for
\|A\|
), and where
\Delta^{n,\mathrm{fin},\pi}_{{\varepsilon}}(A)
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\varepsilon}\geq 0
, is defined by (
8.33
), with
S_{n}^{(\pi)}
defined as in Lemma
8.6
, for some
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
, with
(\Sigma_{\alpha}^{n},\Sigma_{\beta}^{n},\Sigma_{\gamma}^{n}):={\mathcal{E}}(A,n)
.

Proposition 8.8

For
A\in\Omega_{\Psi}
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\Pi^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
can be computed in finitely many arithmetic operations, given
(\alpha_{\max},\beta_{\max},\gamma_{\max}):={\mathcal{B}}(A)
and
(\Sigma_{\alpha}^{n},\Sigma_{\beta}^{n},\Sigma_{\gamma}^{n}):={\mathcal{E}}(A,n)
. Further,
\Pi^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
as
n\to\infty
, and also

\widehat{\Pi}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\ :=\ \Pi^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)+\frac{2}{n}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}\ \,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,\ {\rm Spec}\,A

as
n\to\infty
, with
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\widehat{\Pi}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\subset{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{\dagger}_{n}+3/n}(A)+\frac{2}{n}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

We omit the proof of the above result which is a minor variant of the proof of Proposition
1.10
in §
7
. But let us interpret this proposition as a result relating to the solvability complexity index (SCI) of
[
1
,
2
,
22
]
, as we did for Proposition
1.10
in §
1.3.5
. Equipping
{\mathbb{C}}^{C}
with the Hausdorff metric as in §
1.3
,
the mappings

\Omega_{\Psi}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{C},\qquad A\mapsto\Pi^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\quad\mbox{and}\quad A\mapsto\widehat{\Pi}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A),

are, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, general algorithms in the sense of
[
1
,
2
,
22
]
, with evaluation set (in the sense of
[
1
,
2
,
22
]
)

\Lambda:=\{{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{E}}(\cdot,n):n\in{\mathbb{N}}\};

(8.38)

each function in
\Lambda
has domain
\Omega
, and each can be expressed in terms of finitely many complex-valued functions; cf. the footnote below (
8.38
).
Further,
\widehat{\Pi}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
can be computed in finitely many arithmetic operations and specified using finitely many complex numbers (the elements of
\Pi^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
and the value of
n
). Thus, where
\Xi:\Omega_{\Psi}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{C}
is the mapping given by
\Xi(A):={\rm Spec}\,A
, for
A\in\Omega_{\Psi}
, the computational problem
\{\Xi,\Omega_{\Psi},{\mathbb{C}}^{C},\Lambda\}
has arithmetic SCI, in the sense of
[
1
,
2
,
22
]
, equal to one; more precisely, since also
{\rm Spec}\,A\subset\widehat{\Pi}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
A\in\Omega_{\Psi}
, this computational problem is in the class
\Pi_{1}^{A}
, as defined in
[
2
,
22
]
.

Remark 8.9 (Rate of convergence)
It follows from the last inclusions in Proposition
8.8
that, if
A\in\Omega_{\Psi}
, i.e., if
A
is pseudoergodic with respect to some non-empty compact sets
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
, then

d_{H}({\rm Spec}\,A,\widehat{\Pi}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A))\leq\delta_{n}:=d_{H}({\rm Spec}\,A,{\rm Spec}_{{\varepsilon}^{\dagger}_{n}+3/n}A)+2/n\to 0,

as
n\to\infty
by (
2.8
). Further note, by (
8.22
), that
\delta_{n}
depends only on
n
and the sets
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
. Thus the convergence
\widehat{\Pi}^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
is uniform in
A
, for all
A
that are pseudoergodic with respect to
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
. By contrast, the rate of convergence of the approximation (
8.27
) to
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
(note that
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
is given in terms of
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
by (
8.22
)) depends on the particular realisation of
A
.

To make this concrete (see also the related discussions in
[
12
, §4.3]
and
[
41
]
), consider the Feinberg-Zee case that
\Sigma_{\alpha}=\{-1,1\}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}=\{0\}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}=\{1\}
, and suppose
A
, taking the form (
1.15
), is a random Feinberg-Zee matrix, with
\beta_{j}=0
and
\gamma_{j}=1
, for
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and with the entries
\alpha_{j}\in\{-1,1\}
i.i.d. random variables, with
p:=\mathrm{Pr}(\alpha_{j}=-1)\in(0,1)
. Then, almost surely,
A

is pseudoergodic with respect to
\Sigma_{\alpha}
,
\Sigma_{\beta}
, and
\Sigma_{\gamma}
, and, by the above result, the convergence
\Pi^{n}_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\,\begin{picture}\put(13.0,16.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\tiny\rm H}}\put(15.0,5.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\to$}}\end{picture}\,{\rm Spec}\,A
is (almost surely) independent of the particular realisation of
A
and independent of
p
. But the rate of convergence of (
8.27
) depends on
p
. In particular, given any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, it holds, with arbitrarily high probability if
p
is sufficiently small, that
\alpha_{j}=1
, for
|j|\leq n
, so that
A^{{\sf per},1}_{2n+1,-n-1}
is symmetric,
{\rm Spec}\,A^{{\sf per},1}_{2n+1,-n-1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
, and
{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A^{{\sf per},1}_{2n+1,-n-1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}+{\varepsilon}\overline{{\mathbb{D}}}
. But this implies that

d_{H}({\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A,{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A^{{\sf per},1}_{2n+1,-n-1})\geq 2,

since
2{\rm i}\in{\rm Spec}\,A
by
[
15
, Theorem 2.5, Lemma 2.6]
, so that
(2+{\varepsilon}){\rm i}\in{\rm Spec}_{\varepsilon}A
.

9
Open problems and directions for further work

We see a number of open problems and possible directions for future work. Firstly, in our own work in preparation we are considering two extensions to the work reported above, both of which are announced, in part, in the recent conference paper
[
13
]
. The first is that our inclusion results (
M

τ

), (
M

π

), (
M

τ
1

), for tridiagonal bi-infinite matrices of the form (
1.15
), have natural extensions to semi-infinite matrices and, indeed, finite matrices. (The inclusion sets for finite matrices can be viewed as a generalisation of the Gershgorin theorem, which (see §
1.1
) was a key inspiration for the work we report here.)
The second extension, announced in part in
[
13
]
, is that it is possible, building on the results in this paper for the case of the spectrum, to write down a sequence of approximations, defined in terms of spectral properties of finite matrices, that is convergent to the
essential spectrum
, with each approximation also an inclusion set for the essential spectrum.

In addition to the above we see the following open questions/possible directions for future work:

1.

How can the algorithms, implicit in the definitions of our approximation sequences, be implemented so as to optimise convergence as a function of computation time?
Progress towards efficient implementation of something close to the approximation on the right hand side of (
6.2
), building on the work in (a draft of) this paper, has been made in work by Lindner and Schmidt
[
64
,
75
]
.

2.

Can the algorithms in this paper be used or adapted to resolve open questions regarding the spectra of bounded linear operators arising in applications?
We have indicated one application of this sort in §
8.4.1
, an example which is a pseudoergodic operator where the set of
\tau
-method matrices,
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{(\tau)}=\{A_{n,k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
, has cardinality
|{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{(\tau)}|
that is finite but grows exponentially with
n
. There are interesting potential applications, notably the Fibonacci Hamiltonian and non-self-adjoint variants
[
24
,
25
,
41
]
, where
|{\mathcal{S}}_{n}^{(\tau)}|
grows only linearly with
n
, allowing exploration of spectral properties with much larger values of
n
. At the other extreme, where
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
(and the corresponding
\pi
and
\tau_{1}
method sets) are infinite and so must be approximated as in Theorems
6.3
and
7.1
, one might seek to use or adapt our methods to study the spectra of (bounded) integral operators
{\mathcal{A}}
on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
, for example those that arise in problems of wave scattering by unbounded rough surfaces (e.g.,
[
85
]
). Via a standard discretisation (see, e.g.,
[
62
, §1.2.3]
or
[
16
, Chapter 8]
) the operator
{\mathcal{A}}
on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
is unitarily equivalent to an operator
A
on
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
with
X=L^{2}[0,1]
. Thus, concretely, one might use the inclusion sets in this paper, in the manner indicated in Remark
1.3.3
, to study invertibility of
\lambda I-{\mathcal{A}}
on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
, for some specific
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
of relevance to applications.

3.

Can our inclusion set analysis for
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
be extended to
E^{p}:=\ell^{p}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
, for
1\leq p\leq\infty
?
Although the spectrum of an operator of the form (
1.15
) does not
change if we regard
A
as acting on
E^{p}
, with
p\in[1,\infty]
,
rather than on
E

[
72
, Corollary 2.5.4]
, the pseudospectrum does depend on
p
. We note that many results on approximation of pseudospectra of operators on
E
extend to operators on
E^{p}
(see, e.g.,
[
5
,
12
,
17
,
19
]
), and that Proposition 6 in Lindner & Siedel
[
65
]
, derived for other purposes, can be seen as a key step in tackling this question.

4.

Can our inclusion sets and convergence results for
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
be extended to
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{N},X)
for arbitrary
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
?
We see no reason why this extension should not be possible. Indeed, Proposition 6 in Lindner & Seidel
[
65
]
can be seen as a first version (a version without the weights
w_{j}
) of an extension of our key Proposition
5.1
to this more general case, indeed to the case
E=\ell^{p}({\mathbb{Z}}^{N},X)
, for
p\in[1,\infty]
. (Note that the proof of Proposition 6 provided in
[
65
, pp. 909–910]
takes inspiration from the proof of Proposition
5.1
in this paper and in
[
18
]
.) This extension would be attractive so as to tackle a wider range of applications (see, e.g., the multidimensional applications tackled in
[
19
]
).

5.

Can our results, which are all for bounded linear operators on
E=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{Z}},X)
, be extended to (unbounded) closed, densely defined operators?
Such an extension would be attractive for many applications. We note that other recent work on approximation of spectra and/or pseudospectra (e.g.,
[
31
,
3
,
4
,
21
,
22
]
) extends to this setting.
